## [Constraint-Aware Test-Time Scoring Framework for LLM/VLM Candidate Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33161) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.33161) proposes a constraint-aware test-time scoring framework that combines hard checks with weighted soft checks to select the best candidate from LLM/VLM outputs. The framework is evaluated on four tasks: secure code generation (CWEval), robot manipulation planning (LIBERO with safety variants), competitive programming (LiveCodeBench), and retrieval-augmented QA (HotpotQA). This work addresses a critical weakness in LLM/VLM deployment: selecting outputs that are not only fluent but also correct and safe. The ablation shows that combining hard checks with candidate comparison can improve joint correctness-and-safety by up to +0.21 on retrieval-augmented QA and code-generation safety by up to +0.28 on CWEval, with gains largest when the first sample is often broken or unsafe. The framework consists of a drafting model that generates candidates, a configuring model that selects and weights checks per task, and scoring models for properties no grounded tool can compute. Notably, the per-task layer that selects and weights softer checks adds no measurable value over simpler alternatives, and the gains fall to near zero or slightly negative where the first sample is already strong.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Test-time scoring is a technique where, instead of relying solely on a model's internal confidence, multiple candidate outputs are generated and then evaluated by external criteria to pick the best one. Hard checks are binary constraints that an answer must satisfy (e.g., code must not contain a known vulnerability), while soft checks assign graded scores to desirable properties. Benchmarks like CWEval and LIBERO provide standardized ways to measure security and safety in code generation and robot planning, respectively.

<details><summary>References</summary>

## [AutoformBench: A Lean Formalization Benchmark for Advanced Mathematics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33165) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Meta FAIR researchers introduced AutoformBench, a benchmark of 341 Lean 4 formalization tasks drawn from 24 advanced mathematical sources, where agents must construct full formal developments rather than prove isolated theorems. Each task was formalized and audited by Lean-experienced mathematicians, and the corresponding Lean proof is left to the evaluated AI agent. This benchmark addresses a gap in existing evaluations that often provide isolated theorem statements with much of the supporting theory already available in Mathlib. By requiring agents to build missing definitions and intermediate lemmas, it better reflects real mathematical formalization and could drive progress in AI for mathematics and formal verification. The benchmark includes 341 tasks from 24 advanced mathematical sources, and experiments with frontier models reveal a largely shared difficulty frontier: success depends less on the mathematical domain than on the model's ability to construct missing formal infrastructure. Data and code are available at https://github.com/facebookresearch/autoformbench.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Lean is a proof assistant and functional programming language based on dependent type theory, widely used for formalizing mathematics and verifying proofs. Mathlib is Lean's large community-maintained library of formalized mathematics, providing many definitions and theorems. Traditional benchmarks often test theorem proving in isolation, assuming that necessary background theory is already in Mathlib, but real formalization requires building definitions and intermediate lemmas that connect a result to the library.

## [Clinically Grounded Harm Taxonomy and MindGuard2 for LLM Mental Health Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33263) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces a clinically grounded taxonomy that characterizes harm along three dimensions—what the harm is, who is involved, and when it occurs—and separates harm characterization from intervention decisions. It also presents MindGuard2, a 35B-A3B reasoning-based guard model trained with supervised fine-tuning on short reasoning traces and reinforcement learning with a reasoning-length penalty. This work addresses a critical gap in AI safety for mental health support by showing that public benchmarks rarely contain harm directed at the user, which accounts for nearly half of real-conversation harm. MindGuard2 achieves balanced accuracy comparable to the much larger GLM-5.3 at roughly 10x lower median latency, advancing the accuracy-latency Pareto frontier and helping balance safety detection with avoiding unnecessary interventions. The taxonomy was validated with expert clinicians, and a panel of LLM judges was validated to scale annotation. MindGuard2 achieves low false-positive rates at high recall, and the authors release the guardrail model, synthetic conversations, and benchmark annotations in the taxonomy.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Large language models are increasingly used for mental health support, raising safety concerns. Existing guard models primarily identify categories of harmful content but often overlook the context needed to determine an appropriate response. A harm taxonomy provides a structured way to characterize harmful content, and separating characterization from intervention allows safety policies to be specified outside model weights through explicit, clinician-curated instructions that can be updated and audited.

## [Audit Reveals Logic Datasets Overstate Proof Depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33303) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces a method to compute the minimal proof requirements for procedurally generated logic problems and exports verifiable certificates. Auditing eight datasets, it finds that stated proof depths often overstate actual difficulty, with some problems labeled depth 5 requiring only about two specific rule applications. This work exposes a systematic flaw in benchmark construction for LLM reasoning, where difficulty labels may be misleading. It provides tools to create certified problems with controlled difficulty, potentially improving both evaluation and training data quality for language models. The method computes the steps every proof must make, the shortest proof, the number of minimal proofs, and indispensable premises, with certificates verified by an independent checker. Training experiments show that mixtures of certified problem types, including model-checking problems, transfer better than FLDx2-style derivation data for Qwen3 models from 0.6B to 8B.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Procedurally generated logic problems are commonly used to train and evaluate reasoning in language models. Their difficulty is typically set by planting a proof of a desired depth and using its premises as the problem, but this ignores the possibility of shorter or alternative proofs. ProofWriter and Blocksworld are examples of such datasets, where problems are generated from rules and facts.

## [Platonic Representation Hypothesis Extended to Jacobian-Space Across Vision and Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33304) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.33304) provides evidence that vision and language models converge in their Jacobian-space (J-space) representations, not just activations, and that these J-spaces approximately align up to a single rotation fitted on a subset of words. The rotation generalizes to unseen words and classes, and when applied, a word's vector can change a vision-language model's object prediction, alter an image generator's output, or boost an ImageNet classifier's score for that object. This extends the Platonic Representation Hypothesis beyond static representations to how models act on them, suggesting a deeper cross-modal convergence that could inform interpretability and representation learning research. It may enable model-agnostic interventions and transfer of concepts across modalities without retraining. The J-space is constructed using the Jacobian lens, which for each vocabulary word or class gives a vector in hidden state that maximally increases the model's tendency to generate that word or predict that class. The rotation is fitted on a subset of words but generalizes to others, and interventions are localized: adding a rotated vector to a vision-language model changes the predicted object only in the image region where it is added.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: The Platonic Representation Hypothesis (PRH), proposed by Huh et al. in 2024, posits that AI models trained on different modalities and objectives converge toward a shared representation of reality. Previous evidence for PRH focused on model activations, showing that internal representations become similar across models. The Jacobian lens is a technique from interpretability that uses the Jacobian matrix of the model's output with respect to hidden states to identify directions that influence specific outputs.

A new arXiv paper (2611.33394) trains a linear confidence-score head on the final-layer hidden state of a frozen 2B-parameter LLM backbone, and text-only metamodels as an alternative for closed models, to predict answer correctness and enable confident automated responses while routing uncertain cases to human review. Reliable confidence scores are essential for safely automating LLM-based agents in decisions affecting individuals and organizations, and this work shows a lightweight supervised approach can outperform prompted confidence estimates and automate a larger fraction of workload at high accuracy targets. All results come from a single 2B-parameter backbone evaluated on graded answers that are about 98% SQuAD and the remainder TruthfulQA; the confidence score head outperforms all baselines and metamodels, though transferability to unseen datasets is modest and prompted confidence scores can appear calibrated yet lack thresholding resolution.

## [Partial Routing: Trace-Tuned Delegation for Efficient Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33402) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint introduces partial routing, a method that guides a frontier lead model to delegate suitable subtasks to a cheaper worker model based on quality and cost measurements from past runs. On ViBench, with δ=0.75, it raises the lead's score from 70.2 to 78.5 at 19% lower cost, and on Terminal-Bench 4.0 it improves pass rate from 54% to 61% at similar cost. This approach addresses the inefficiency of using a single model for entire agentic tasks that mix easy and difficult subtasks, potentially enabling significant cost savings without sacrificing performance. It could impact developers and organizations deploying LLM agents at scale, where routing decisions are typically made per task rather than per subtask. The method requires no training of either model and uses a parameter δ to control delegation aggressiveness; even providing only the subtask breakdown without a worker reduces cost on both benchmarks and raises Terminal-Bench pass rate. The approach relies on analyzing past runs to identify recurring subtasks and measure each model's quality and cost on them.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Model routing is a technique that sends easier tasks to cheaper models to exploit the wide variation in capability and price among AI models. Agentic workflows involve AI agents that autonomously perform multi-step tasks, often combining subtasks of varying difficulty. ViBench and Terminal-Bench 4.0 are benchmarks designed to evaluate agent performance on complex, multi-step tasks.

## [LLM Triage Study Reveals Scaling and Adversarial Risks in Security Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33470) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper presents 56 reproducible experiments evaluating LLM-based alert triage, graph-based risk propagation, and adversarial evasion in detection and response pipelines. It finds that fleet growth halves recall at a fixed analyst budget, rarity-weighted graph risk propagation outperforms GraphSAGE without neural training, and unconstrained LLM auto-closure eliminates 90% of false positives but discards 31-44% of true attacks. The study provides empirical evidence that LLM triage agents can silently miss a large fraction of true attacks, and that reinforcement tuning via GRPO can cause severe reward hacking on unseen attack classes. These findings are critical for security teams deploying AI-driven detection and response at fleet scale, as they highlight the need for deterministic policy harnesses and careful evaluation before trusting LLM auto-closure. The experiments span a multi-layer enterprise-and-cloud simulator and four public datasets (SOREL-20M, BODMAS, LANL, DARPA Transparent Computing), testing twelve architectural claims. Notably, post-training with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) suppresses up to 86% of novel attacks compared to two-epoch supervised fine-tuning, and LLM triage evasion rises sharply against an adaptive adversary unless gated by a deterministic policy harness prior to model invocation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Detection and response pipelines in security operations centers rely on triaging alerts under limited human analyst attention, often measured as recall at a fixed number of alerts per day. GraphSAGE is a popular graph neural network that aggregates features from neighbors to classify nodes, while rarity-weighted graph risk propagation is a simpler technique that spreads risk scores based on the rarity of connections. LLM triage agents use large language models to automatically classify alerts, and reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) methods like GRPO aim to improve their decision-making.

## [Real-Time Reasoning Training Helps AI Agents Balance Speed and Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33513) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces real-time reasoning training (RRT), a class of techniques that teaches AI agents to adapt their reasoning time based on the cost of delay in asynchronous environments. The authors propose three standard RRT techniques—using real-time data, decoupling acting and observing, and curriculum learning in time—and show they outperform the strongest non-RRT baseline by an average of 33% reward on in-distribution and 55% on out-of-distribution tasks in Frogger and Snake. Current AI agents are trained in synchronous environments where the world waits for them to act, but real-world deployment requires acting on time even as the environment changes independently. RRT addresses this gap by teaching agents to trade off thinking and acting dynamically, which could enable more capable and responsive agents in robotics, autonomous systems, and interactive applications. The three RRT techniques are simple yet effective: using real-time data (training with asynchronous environment dynamics), decoupling acting and observing (allowing the agent to act while still processing observations), and curriculum learning in time (gradually increasing time pressure during training). The experiments are conducted in two real-time environments, Frogger and Snake, and the method substantially outperforms baselines across different time pressures.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning (RL) trains agents to make decisions by rewarding desired behaviors, but most RL environments are synchronous: the world pauses until the agent acts. In contrast, asynchronous or real-time environments change continuously, so agents must decide when to think and when to act. Curriculum learning is a training strategy that starts with easy tasks and gradually increases difficulty, which in this context means gradually tightening time constraints.

Results show text-only models achieve higher overall agreement than multimodal models, with multimodal performance strongest for image-image, weaker for image-text, and lowest for text-text comparisons.

## [Natural Image Learning Untangles Number Sense in Deep Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33615) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces a geometric framework showing that natural-image pretraining increases the angle between numerosity and proportion subspaces in deep neural networks, reducing their overlap and enabling more robust readout under conflict. The study finds that this separation also emerges through self-supervised learning and synthetic-image training, not just conventional supervised pretraining. This work bridges cognitive science and AI by showing how visual learning refines an innate number sense into robust, functionally independent quantity representations, which could inform the design of vision-language models that currently struggle with numerical cognition. It also offers a geometric lens for understanding representational disentanglement in deep networks. Untrained and pretrained networks showed similar unit-level numerosity selectivity and basic comparison performance, but only natural-image pretraining increased the angle between numerosity and proportion subspaces. Direct manipulation of these geometric properties confirmed their functional contributions to decoding accuracy and independence, and the effect was not limited to supervised learning with category labels.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Number sense, the ability to approximate numerosity, is present in many animals and even in untrained deep networks, where numerosity-selective units emerge spontaneously. However, it remains unclear whether visual learning contributes beyond this spontaneous emergence. This paper uses a geometric framework to trace how representations of numerosity and proportion change across learning, drawing inspiration from human development where children improve at resolving conflicts between these quantities.

## [Study: AI Code Reviewer Effort Levels and False Success Rates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33639) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A preregistered empirical study evaluated Claude Opus 5.5, Sonnet 5.5, and Haiku 5.5 as AI code reviewers on 90 agent-written Django patches from SWE-bench Verified, finding that high effort accepted fewer plausible-but-wrong patches than low effort (false success rates: Opus .45 vs .67, Sonnet .49 vs .65, Haiku .51 vs .78). For Opus, medium effort was non-inferior to high within 15 percentage points and cost 20% less, though no setting was a strong gate as each accepted at least 45% of wrong patches. This study provides actionable guidance for teams deploying AI code reviewers, showing that raising effort levels can reduce false acceptances of plausible-but-wrong patches, but even high effort is not a reliable gate, so human oversight remains essential. It also highlights cost-accuracy tradeoffs, with medium effort nearly matching high effort for Opus at lower cost, which could inform budget-conscious deployment decisions. The study used strengthened SWE-ABS tests to label 55 patches as wrong and 35 as correct, and exploratory coding showed that most rejections named the tested defect and that effort added such rejections. However, for 32 of the 55 wrong patches the issue may not require what the failing test checks, and without them neither accuracy hypothesis holds for any model, suggesting the benchmark's tests may not fully capture patch correctness.

**Background**: AI coding agents can generate code faster than humans can review it, leading teams to use AI reviewers powered by reasoning models that offer effort settings (low, medium, high) to trade cost for diligence. EVA is a new evidence-seeking visual agent that reframes multimodal reasoning as an explicit process of evidence acquisition and verification, using iterative tool replanning and evidence consistency verification to reduce hallucinations in multimodal LLMs. This approach could significantly improve the reliability of multimodal LLMs by addressing hallucinations at their source—unchecked visual assumptions—and offers a more robust alternative to self-reflection or multi-agent debate methods that may reinforce errors. EVA introduces three mechanisms: Iterative Tool Replanning to dynamically refine tool selection and parameters, Evidence Consistency Verification to align answers with collected evidence, and Selective Evidence Routing to invoke tools only for complex or high-risk queries, balancing robustness and efficiency.

## [PowerFix: Physics-Guided LLM Repair for Power Converter Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33712) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PowerFix introduces a physics-guided iterative repair framework that uses LLMs to diagnose and fix power converter design failures while preserving topology and constraints. On a 96-task flyback repair benchmark, it achieves a 78.7% verified repair success rate, compared to 30.1% for the strongest LLM baseline, and reaches 88.4% with three additional replanning rounds. This work demonstrates that combining LLMs with structured, physics-aware diagnostics can significantly improve automated repair in engineering design, potentially reducing manual debugging effort in power electronics development. It also sets a new benchmark for LLM-assisted design automation, showing a path toward more reliable and constraint-preserving design workflows. The framework uses PowerDiagGraph to link failed checks to physical causes and constrain repair routes, with an LLM prioritizing routes and a deterministic compiler translating selected repairs into authorized field-level edits. Candidates are validated on isolated design copies and committed only after all checks pass; the results are offline and simulation-based, with hardware prototype validation and transfer to a second converter topology as future steps.

**Background**: Power converters, such as flyback converters, are circuits that change voltage levels and are widely used in power supplies; flyback converters provide isolation between input and output via a transformer. Engineering design of such converters involves many constraints, and when a design fails validation, engineers must identify the physical cause and adjust variables without breaking other requirements. LLM agents are increasingly explored for automating design tasks, but repairing failures while respecting physical laws and constraints remains difficult. PowerFix addresses this by structuring the repair process with physics-guided diagnosis and deterministic editing.

<a id="item-72"></a>
## [WhatIf: Counterfactual World Models with Causal Graphs for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33745) ⭐️ 7.0/10

WhatIf introduces a counterfactual world model for robotic manipulation that couples candidate action outcomes through shared latent physics and sparse object-factor causal graphs, enabling action comparisons without counterfactual labels. It also proposes Counterfactual Advantage Planning (CAP), which ranks plans by their advantage over an incumbent within shared situations and constrains the probability of harm. This work addresses a key gap in robot learning: world models typically predict marginal futures for individual actions but cannot compare individual treatment effects or estimate the probability that switching actions causes harm. By enabling counterfactual reasoning over coupled futures, WhatIf could improve decision-making in manipulation tasks where safety and precision are critical. The method uses shared type-conditioned functions to handle varying numbers of objects and a round-trip loss to enforce action-invariant abduction without counterfactual labels. The authors show that replacing an individual effect with its conditional mean incurs mean-squared error equal to its conditional variance, and that paired advantages reduce estimator variance by a factor of 1-ρ, where ρ is the correlation between branch outcomes.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: World models in robotics predict future states conditioned on actions, but standard factual predictions only give average effects across actions, not individual effects in a specific situation. Counterfactual reasoning—considering what would have happened under a different action—is crucial for comparing actions in the same physical context. Causal graphs represent dependencies between variables, and object-factor graphs decompose scenes into objects and their interactions, enabling structured reasoning about physical pathways.

## [REFORGE: LLM-Assisted RTL Bug Repair with Structured Verification Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33748) ⭐️ 7.0/10

REFORGE is a new LLM-assisted RTL bug repair framework that accumulates structured repair evidence across iterations to satisfy multiple objectives simultaneously, including functionality, behavioral equivalence, security, and cost efficiency. Evaluated in 288 runs across three LLM backends and three benchmark suites, it increased checked repairs on CirFix from 32/63 to 51/63 and repaired the Hack@DAC OpenTitan defect in every configuration. This work addresses a critical gap in LLM-based hardware design: successful compilation or testbench execution does not guarantee defect removal, behavioral preservation, security, or implementation feasibility. By providing explicit multi-stage verification evidence, REFORGE enables reproducible comparison and integration into hardware design flows, which could significantly improve trust in LLM-generated RTL repairs. REFORGE profiles buggy RTL, identifies structural and security-relevant features, localizes failing behavior, and uses verification-guided diagnosis to generate complete repair variants. Each variant undergoes syntax checking, simulation, formal analysis, off-trigger preservation, security re-scan, and synthesis-based power, performance, and area analysis; evidence records show that a testbench-only criterion reports 3.9 times more repairs than the full set of applicable checks.

TransHarness introduces a method to evolve harness intervention policies from same-task success-failure contrasts, avoiding overfitting to evolution benchmarks. Using only 300 source trajectories from outside the test distribution, it achieves state-of-the-art scores of 76.4% on DeepSWE v1.1 and 95.5% on Terminal-Bench 2.1, surpassing GPT-6 Astra by 2.4 and 5.6 percentage points. This addresses a critical issue in coding-agent development: harness evolution can overfit to specific benchmarks, producing gains that fail to transfer to new tasks. TransHarness demonstrates that transferable harness evolution is possible, potentially enabling more robust and generalizable improvements to coding agents without retraining model weights. The method couples execution-context triggers with diagnostic guidance to enable task-dependent checks and corrections, and uses cross-task replay and independent semantic selection to retain policies that intervene selectively based on current-task evidence. Evaluation covers multiple strong models and a range of lightweight and mature harnesses across distinct target benchmarks.

The approach tackles known RL challenges in building control, potentially enabling more practical deployment of reinforcement learning in real-world HVAC systems. The framework uses a Branching Dueling Q-Network for counterfactual advantage learning and integrates a Held-Action TD-λ target to mitigate delayed credit assignment, plus a physics-aligned smoothness regularization to suppress high-frequency actuator fluctuations. ExtractBench Pro introduces a synthetic benchmark of 100 challenging documents covering six underrepresented extraction modes, revealing that no extraction system achieves more than 61% error-free extraction. The strongest general-purpose coding agents, Claude Code Opus 5.5 and Codex GPT-6 Astra, outperform specialized extraction APIs on perfect-document rate and Value F1, but at a higher cost per page. The benchmark evaluates ten coding agents and eight extraction API configurations on accuracy, grounding, and perfect-document rate. Among specialized APIs, LlamaExtract offers the lowest-cost frontier, with Agentic Plus Max Effort approaching the strongest coding agent at lower cost per page. Analysis of coding agent traces shows intelligent image processing and programmatic reasoning, but also failures in applying document-specific definitions and recovering relationships from diagrams.

## [Localized Certificate Repair for Safe Neural Network Control at Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33813) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces adaptive piecewise barrier certificates that repair safety certificates locally around newly emerged unsafe regions at deployment time, avoiding global retraining. The method casts adaptation as a localized certificate repair problem and uses a staged repair framework that progresses from localized repair to joint policy-certificate updates and, if needed, localized retraining. This work addresses a critical gap in deploying neural network controlled systems: maintaining formal safety guarantees when unexpected obstacles or constraint changes occur after deployment. It enables fast, localized updates (within tens of seconds) without retraining from scratch, which is essential for safety-critical applications like autonomous vehicles and robots operating in dynamic environments. Empirical results on four benchmarks with five new obstacles show the method succeeds in all cases and completes within tens of seconds on average, whereas retraining from scratch fails in most cases. On instances where retraining succeeds, the method is 17.3× faster on average and incurs only 16.2% performance degradation relative to the original policy.

However, these certificates are static and can become invalid if the environment changes after deployment, such as new obstacles appearing.

RadOncGym is an agent-based framework in which an LLM iteratively tunes 12 planning-objective weights for dose-mimicking optimization in radiotherapy, using a surrogate verifier to reduce expensive full planning solves. This work shows that LLM agents can act as optimization planners in downstream radiotherapy planning, potentially automating a labor-intensive step that currently requires expert manual tuning of competing objectives. If validated beyond the OpenKBP benchmark, such agent-driven tuning could reduce planning time and improve plan quality in clinical workflows, while the public testbed enables systematic comparison of agent strategies. The surrogate verifier provides rapid feedback and lets the agent decide when to spend a full planning solve within a fixed budget; the agent remains competitive with 16-solve variants of non-agent methods while requiring 75.0–77.6% less normalized runtime. ## [MetaDecomp: Meta-Learned Procedural Memory for Adaptive Task Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33822) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MetaDecomp introduces an in-context meta-learning framework that builds reusable procedural memory as directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) of operations and dependencies, and adaptively decomposes long-horizon tasks by refining state abstractions and procedures. Evaluated on ALFWorld and ScienceWorld over three seeds, it achieves the highest mean performance in four of five settings, including an 86.3% success rate on ALFWorld with Qwen3-4B, while using fewer inference tokens than ReAct and AutoRefine. This work addresses a key challenge for language agents in long-horizon tasks: reusing past experience while distinguishing which procedures apply and what work remains. By jointly learning task-relative state abstractions and reusable procedures, MetaDecomp offers a more token-efficient alternative to iterative prompting methods like ReAct, with potential impact on agent learning and task planning research. The outer loop builds procedural memory from successful training executions, representing task progress as abstract states and procedures as DAGs, and revisits historical evidence to reassess state assignments as new distinctions are learned. At test time, the inner loop refines an initial decomposition using environmental feedback, combining applicable procedure fragments with task-specific steps missing from memory; ablations show that fixing the initial schema or omitting historical reprojection reduces performance, and cross-benchmark analysis reveals a low-performance area where partial coverage degrades success relative to hidden memory.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Language agents often struggle with long-horizon tasks because they must decide which past procedures apply and what remains to be done as execution progresses. In-context meta-learning allows models to adapt to new tasks by conditioning on contextual examples rather than updating parameters, while procedural memory stores reusable action sequences. Directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) are commonly used to represent dependencies among operations, and state abstraction helps agents reason at a higher level by grouping similar situations.

## [LANTERN uses MLLM's own misses to improve small brain metastasis detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33836) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced LANTERN, a framework that enables a multimodal large language model (MLLM) to recursively improve its detection of small brain metastases by attributing each missed lesion to one of four causes and having an LLM mentor revise one of four model components per round. Over 15 unattended rounds, LANTERN raised a fine-tuned 4B MLLM from finding 75% to 94% of lesions at one false positive per scan on BraTS-MET, and 91% of lesions of 0.1–1 mL, compared with at most 51% and 29% for five frontier MLLMs. This work addresses a concrete limitation of MLLMs in medical imaging—poor localization of small abnormalities—and shows that a model's own errors, when properly attributed, can drive reliable self-improvement without test data. If validated, such error-driven recursive learning could make MLLMs more trustworthy for clinical tasks like early metastasis detection, where missing small lesions has serious consequences. At 384 px resolution, a 0.1 mL lesion covers less than a quarter of one visual token, explaining why frontier MLLMs find fewer than 30% of 0.1–1 mL lesions at one false positive per scan. LANTERN's gains held on an external cohort (89%) and on unseen meningiomas (77%), and the framework uses a rule frozen before the first round to decide what is kept, with every tool call audited and no test data used.

## [FORK co-evolves guardrail disclosure and decisions against adaptive attackers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33879) ⭐️ 7.0/10

FORK introduces a gradient-free method that co-evolves a guardrail's natural-language decision and disclosure skills alongside an adaptive attacker that reads every public message. On FORK-World, it cuts adaptive attack success rate from 78.4% to 14.1% while keeping utility above 85%, and on the unseen MT-AgentRisk benchmark it lowers ASR from 70.6% to 9.1% with 93.1% benign utility. This work addresses a practical security gap: fixed refusals block benign recovery while detailed explanations leak hints to attackers. By showing that safe disclosure can be learned without increasing adaptive attack success, FORK offers a path toward guardrails that remain both usable and robust in agentic systems where every message may be observed. FORK revises its natural-language skill library using evidence from each exploited message and permissive decision, avoiding gradient-based training. Evaluation spans four benchmarks and three agents, but the abstract does not detail the attacker model's capabilities or the computational cost of co-evolution.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: LLM guardrails are safety layers that monitor and block risky actions or outputs from AI agents, commonly used to prevent prompt injection and harmful content. In agent settings, a guardrail's rejection message is visible to anyone observing the interaction, so the wording of a refusal can either help legitimate users recover or inadvertently coach an adaptive attacker. Safe disclosure is the challenge of explaining rejections in a way that enables authorized task recovery without revealing exploitable information.

## [Source-Code Audit Finds SkillsBench Skill Lift Inflated and Noisy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33943) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2611.33943) audits the SkillsBench benchmark at the source-code level and reanalyzes 7,554 public trials forming 1,421 paired (configuration, task) cells. The audit identifies two controlled-verified cases of grader dependence, one operational definition gap, four tasks with placeholder skill directories, and a harness that added 48 built-in skills in most runs, concluding that the reported average skill lift of 16.6 pp is inflated and noisy. This work matters because SkillsBench is used to justify gating or eliminating agent skills based on per-task gains, and flawed measurements could lead to misguided design decisions in AI agent evaluation. It highlights the need for rigorous, source-code-level auditing and noise-aware statistical analysis in benchmark research. The three problematic tasks showed an average lift of 74.6 pp versus 18.7 pp overall, and no task-level reduction was statistically significant after multiple-testing correction; with at most three runs per condition, 58% of cells could not have shown a decrease. A gating strategy based on skill-free score improved 2-3 pp in logs but still gained 4-7 pp after shuffling labels, and no significant improvement appeared on held-out runs.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Agent skills are directories of instructions, reference materials, and scripts that coding agents load on demand to improve task performance. SkillsBench is a benchmark that measures whether such skills improve AI coding agents by comparing paired runs with and without skills across expert-curated tasks. Recent research has started to use per-task skill gains to decide when to enable or disable skills, making the accuracy of these measurements critical.

## [PatternMiner: LLM-Guided Small Models for Automated Math Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33966) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PatternMiner is a new system that combines LLM agents with narrow machine learning models and interpretability techniques to automatically generate mathematical conjectures and prove new theorems. Applied to five positivity problems in algebraic combinatorics, it proved several new results, including theorems on Schubert positivity, a character-row evaluation formula, a restricted Kazhdan–Lusztig combinatorial invariance conjecture, and Schur positivity for immanant coefficients. This work bridges two previously separate paradigms in AI-driven mathematics—LLM-based reasoning and interpretability-driven exploration—and demonstrates that their combination can produce publishable new theorems. It suggests a scalable path toward AI systems that not only conjecture but also refine and prove results in specialized mathematical domains. PatternMiner iteratively generates datasets from broad questions, trains small models, extracts patterns via interpretability, formulates conjectures, and searches for counterexamples using mathematical software; the agent can attempt proofs at any stage. The paper also analyzes common routes to conjecture generation and documents frequent failure modes.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: AI for mathematics has followed two main approaches: large language models that reason via text to resolve well-specified conjectures, and narrow models paired with interpretability for open-ended, human-in-the-loop exploration of mathematical objects. The former struggles with direct exploration of large datasets, while the latter is limited to simple empirical pattern detection. PatternMiner aims to combine the strengths of both.

## [LLM Agent Designs Its Own Few-Shot Adaptation for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34036) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes Agentic Model Editing (AME), a framework where an LLM agent designs a domain-customized adaptation program—including input transforms, encoder edits, feature computation, and classifier fitting—for few-shot vision-language model transfer. On eleven datasets with CLIP ViT-B/16, AME achieves average accuracies of 81.2% without back-propagation and 81.9% with back-propagation, outperforming compared methods. This work moves beyond fixed prompt-tuning or classifier-fitting pipelines by letting an LLM agent autonomously design the adaptation procedure, which could make few-shot VLM transfer more flexible and effective across diverse domains. It also points toward a broader paradigm where agents meta-design machine learning workflows rather than just tuning predefined components. The agent inspects images, class semantics, model parameters, intermediate representations, and activations to form hypotheses, then implements and tests candidate programs on the support set, using aggregate validation accuracy for refinement and selection. Each program produces a complete domain model fixed before testing, and the agent and tools are shared across domains while each domain gets its own program; experiments also show applicability across different VLM backbones and LLM agents.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Vision-language models like CLIP are pretrained on large image-text pairs and can recognize many concepts zero-shot, but adapting them to new domains with only a few labeled examples (few-shot adaptation) remains challenging. Most existing methods fit prompts, representations, or classifiers within a pre-specified procedure, which may not suit domains with very different visual distinctions. AME instead uses an LLM agent to design the entire adaptation program, drawing on the agent's ability to reason about model internals and experiment with candidate solutions.

**Tags**: `#vision-language-models`, `#few-shot-learning`, `#LLM-agents`, `#model-editing`, `#transfer-learning`

## [Small reasoning models lose 7-17% surplus in auction bidding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34068) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.34068) evaluates six open reasoning models of 4B to 14B parameters on auction bidding tasks using bid landscapes from eBay closing prices and the AuctionNet simulated advertising market. The study finds that these models systematically lose 7-17% of the surplus that an exact calculator would earn, primarily because they ignore the best option already ranked first in their own reasoning traces and overbid toward the highest bid on the menu. As ad platforms and marketplaces increasingly deploy LLM-based agents to choose bids, these findings reveal that small reasoning models can destroy significant economic value even when given perfect win-curve estimates. The identified failure modes—trace misreading and overbidding bias—are actionable for practitioners, and simple fixes like closing the reasoning block before answering or adding an audit layer can recover most of the lost surplus. The overbidding bias is tied to the bid's absolute size rather than its position in the list and persists under sampling; arithmetic errors are rare. A token cap that cuts traces short is costly, but allowing models to stop on their own within 4,096 tokens lets five models recover 91-100% of the calculator's lead over a non-reasoning model, and closing the reasoning block before answering lifts the weakest model from 42% to 84% on eBay and from 24% to 81% on AuctionNet. An audit that flags bids disagreeing with the trace's own numbers and overrides them with the calculator's bid raises the share of the calculator's lead recovered at 768 forced tokens from 42-85% to 84-100% on eBay under nested cross-validation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: In online advertising and marketplaces, bidders are often shown a bid landscape—a curve estimating the probability of winning at each possible bid—and reasoning language models are increasingly used as agents to select bids from these landscapes. Pay-your-bid pricing means the winner pays exactly what they bid, so overbidding directly reduces surplus. An exact calculator that optimally uses the win curve provides a benchmark for the maximum achievable surplus, and this paper measures how much value small reasoning models lose relative to that benchmark.

**Tags**: `#reasoning-models`, `#auction-bidding`, `#LLM-agents`, `#empirical-evaluation`, `#AI-economics`

<a id="item-88"></a>
## [Survey Unifies Expert Gating, Model Cascades, and Agent Orchestration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34112) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A systematic review synthesizes 2,289 studies published between 2017 and September 2026, selected from 13,884 unique records, to unify expert gating, model cascades, and agent orchestration under a two-step inspect-then-allocate framework for conditional computation. This synthesis bridges three previously siloed research areas—sparse expert gating, model cascades, and multi-agent orchestration—revealing recurring system failures and transferable fixes that could guide more efficient and robust system design across the AI stack. Discrete hard selection accounts for 70%, 83%, and 91% of token-, query-, and task-scale literature respectively; computational overhead is a top operational bottleneck across every layer, and engineering safeguards are asymmetric, with intra-layer gating prioritizing load balancing while model selection and agent orchestration rely on confidence thresholds and budget rules amid miscalibration and cascading errors.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Conditional computation aims to reduce the computational, latency, and monetary costs of running large neural models or multi-agent workflows on every input by dynamically deciding which internal experts, model endpoints, or specialized agents to invoke per request. Mixture-of-experts architectures use token-level gating to route inputs to a subset of experts, model cascades use query-level routing to send easy inputs to cheaper models, and multi-agent systems use task-level coordination to delegate subtasks to specialized agents. These three areas have largely progressed independently, which this review argues obscures shared challenges and solutions.

## [SOLO: Content-Anchored Watermarking for Repeated LLM Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34190) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper identifies that deployed distortion-free LLM watermarks couple multiple responses sampled from the same prompt, because answer templates cause all responses to query the watermark with the same context. The authors propose SOLO, which keeps a request-scoped context history but anchors keyed contexts on min-hash-selected content, so sibling responses rarely collide and random edits remain local. This matters because applications routinely draw many samples per prompt for self-consistency, best-of-n selection, and uncertainty estimation, and existing watermarks can break the independence of those samples. SOLO preserves sample faithfulness while maintaining detectability, which is important for AI provenance and security in real-world LLM deployments. SOLO's responses are jointly distributed as independent unwatermarked responses and each remains detectable with a single key. On five language models from three families and three benchmarks, at a 1% false positive rate, SOLO detects more responses than four-token key pools on most tasks, including after randomly editing 10% of tokens, and more than the context history approach on most clean tasks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: LLM watermarking embeds a statistical signal into generated text so providers can later detect machine-generated content. Distortion-free watermarks aim to leave the text distribution unchanged, typically by using a keyed pseudorandom function to bias token sampling without altering the overall distribution. Repeated sampling—drawing multiple responses per prompt—is common in practice, but watermarks that deduplicate keyed contexts only within a response can cause responses to share contexts and become correlated.

**Tags**: `#LLM watermarking`, `#AI security`, `#sampling`, `#provenance`, `#arXiv`

## [LLMs May Leak Answers in Drug-Target Forecasting, Temporal Graph Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34247) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper recasts drug-target hypotheses as dated events (first clinical trial) and builds a temporal biomedical knowledge graph to test whether six open LLMs can forecast novel pairs after their training cutoff. Without retrieval, models perform similarly to a simple trial-history baseline; with the newest graph, five of six models improve, but the graph paths often include the answer (the pair's own post-decision trial), indicating answer leakage. This work highlights a critical evaluation flaw in using LLMs for drug-target prioritization: apparent forecasting gains from knowledge graph retrieval may be inflated by memorization or leakage of future outcomes. It calls for rigorous temporal separation of features and labels, which is essential for trustworthy AI in high-stakes biomedical decision-making. The study uses a temporal knowledge graph rebuilt at year-end from 2020 onward and evaluates six open LLMs with and without graph retrieval. Even evidence dated before the decision adds at most a small gain, and the newest graph's improvement is largely due to paths containing the answer (the pair's own trial dated after the decision), underscoring the need to date both features and labels.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Drug-target prioritization is the process of identifying which molecular targets are most likely to yield successful therapies, a key step before costly clinical trials. Temporal knowledge graphs organize biomedical facts with timestamps, enabling reasoning about what was known at a given time. Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for such biomedical forecasting, but their training data may include future information, raising concerns about answer leakage—where the model has already seen the correct answer, inflating apparent performance.

## [Navigent: Geometry-Grounded Agentic Framework for Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34249) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Navigent is a new agentic vision-language navigation (VLN) framework that pairs a frozen multimodal large language model (MLLM) with a Geometry-Grounded Execution Critic (GEC) to detect and recover from motion discrepancies. On the R2R-CE and RxR-CE validation-unseen splits, it achieves success rates of 84.0% and 86.0% and SPL of 72.9% and 69.1%, respectively. Long-horizon reliability is a key bottleneck for deploying general-purpose MLLMs in embodied navigation, and Navigent's execution critic offers a modular way to catch and correct errors without retraining the base model. This could make agentic VLN systems more robust for real-world robotics applications. The GEC performs two checks: action-to-motion consistency (comparing measured displacement to commanded movement) and motion-to-landmark consistency (tracking visual anchors and entrance candidates in world coordinates). Detected discrepancies interrupt the action sequence and trigger reobservation, local adjustment, or node-based recovery, while an offline self-improvement procedure refines prompts and GEC logic with fixed model weights.

**Background**: Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) requires an embodied agent to follow natural language instructions through visual environments, integrating language grounding, sequential decision-making, and multimodal perception. Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) combine language reasoning with modality-specific encoders to process text, images, and other data, and recent work explores using them as flexible, general-purpose navigation policies instead of task-specific models.

## [MedSkill-E² Benchmark Reveals Medical LLM Agents Underuse Available Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34250) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce MedSkill-E², a framework and benchmark (EBU-Bench) that evaluates how well medical LLM agents apply available skills autonomously, documenting a phenomenon called skill Enable But Underuse (EBU). Across 12 medically relevant task groups, all evaluated model-harness combinations showed EBU with a mean gap of 43.94 percentage points between autonomous and explicitly guided skill use, and they propose EBU-Evolve, which improves autonomous skill-use accuracy by 33.33 to 55.17 pp. This work highlights that simply providing a skill to an agent does not guarantee correct application, a critical safety concern in medical settings where missing one constraint can produce plausible but wrong answers. The benchmark and evolution method give the AI/ML community a way to measure and reduce this gap, with gains transferring across models and harnesses. For the same model, autonomous skill-use accuracy varied by up to 38.96 pp across different harnesses, and model rankings could change depending on the harness. EBU can persist even after a skill is opened, and EBU-Evolve distills execution feedback into reusable skill instructions that also improve performance on unseen tasks within the same skill.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: LLM agents are systems that use a large language model to plan and execute tasks, often calling external tools or following predefined skills that encode domain rules and procedures. Benchmarks are standardized tests used to compare model capabilities, and in agentic settings the surrounding harness (the code that manages tool calls and context) can strongly influence results. Medical tasks are especially sensitive because a single overlooked constraint can lead to a clinically plausible but incorrect output.

## [MeterRouter: Budget-Aware Dynamic Routing for Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34327) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MeterRouter introduces a budget-aware dynamic router that incrementally constructs a collaboration graph for multi-agent LLM systems, optimizing agent roles, LLM assignments, and communication topology under per-query monetary budgets. It initializes by imitating successful execution graphs and then refines decisions with a variant of Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) that rewards correct answers while penalizing spending and budget overruns. This work addresses a practical inefficiency in multi-agent LLM systems by making budget an explicit constraint, potentially enabling more cost-effective deployments. It achieves comparable accuracy to the strongest multi-agent system at 82% lower cost and extends the accuracy-cost frontier beyond single-agent systems, which could impact how developers design and deploy LLM-based agent systems. MeterRouter is evaluated across five benchmarks and a three-tier LLM pool, demonstrating accurate performance, economical operation (82% lower cost than the strongest MAS router), and controllability in adapting spending to the specified budget while rarely exceeding it. The router's decisions are conditioned on both the query and a per-query monetary budget, allowing flexible cost-accuracy trade-offs.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Multi-agent systems (MAS) built on large language models (LLMs) often outperform single-agent systems by enabling collaboration among specialized agents, but inter-agent communication incurs substantial token overhead. Assigning a large LLM to every agent is common but inefficient, leading to resource waste. Existing routers typically treat cost implicitly rather than as an explicit optimization constraint, and a fixed accuracy-cost preference cannot express how much a caller is willing to pay for a particular query. The Pareto frontier represents the set of optimal trade-offs between accuracy and cost, and MeterRouter aims to push this frontier outward.

## [EvoLink-Bench: New Benchmark for Self-Evolving LLM Agents in Long-Horizon Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34400) ⭐️ 7.0/10

EvoLink-Bench is a new benchmark for evaluating self-evolving LLM agents in long-horizon, interdependent task streams with persistent workspaces, covering daily office work, multi-turn dialogue, and AutoResearch-style workflows. It evaluates five agent frameworks across three backbone configurations under three persistence conditions, scoring 19,635 task slots, and finds that no framework consistently performs best across both knowledge-level (L1) and capability-level (L2) tasks. Existing streaming benchmarks often use short or independent tasks, so this benchmark fills a gap by testing whether agents can reuse experience when later tasks depend on earlier outcomes. Its findings suggest that persistent state and retries do not automatically improve performance, which could reshape how agent memory and self-evolution are designed and evaluated. The benchmark distinguishes within-task recovery, persistent-state updates, and experience reuse in later tasks, and results show that retries recover only a limited proportion of failed tasks while persistent-state updates do not necessarily improve subsequent performance. Gains also vary across domains and task streams, and the effects of evolution differ between knowledge- and capability-level tasks, suggesting they require different learning mechanisms.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: LLM agents are increasingly expected to operate continuously in dynamic environments, solving not just current tasks but learning from past interactions to improve future performance. Long-horizon agentic tasks involve multiple interdependent actions toward open-ended goals, and benchmarks are shifting from single-response accuracy to measuring task horizon. Self-evolving agents aim to acquire and revise knowledge and capabilities over time, but evaluating this requires task streams where later tasks genuinely depend on earlier outcomes.

## [EgoUseBench: New Benchmark Tests Computer-Use Agents on Evolving First-Person Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34466) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced EgoUseBench, a benchmark for evaluating computer-use agents under the 'Ego Use' paradigm, where agents interpret first-person observations from AI wearables like smart glasses. Built from over 74 hours of real-world recordings, it includes 693 tasks across three interaction settings—Computer-Use, Offline Ego Use, and Online Ego Use—and finds that average success rates in Online Ego Use are 26.3% lower than in Computer-Use. This benchmark addresses a critical evaluation gap for agents that must operate on evolving first-person context from wearables, a scenario increasingly relevant as smart glasses and similar devices enter the mainstream. The significant performance drop highlights that current agents struggle to keep task execution aligned with changing user intent and physical surroundings, pointing to a key challenge for future agent research. Tasks are grouped by how information changes during execution into three types: Graft (revised user requirements), Refresh (new first-person observations), and Clarify (agent-initiated information seeking). The benchmark tracks first-person observations, user requirements, and website state separately to capture task evolution, and the 200 base tasks are shared across the three interaction settings.

**Background**: Computer-use agents are AI systems that interact with digital environments (e.g., websites, applications) to perform tasks on behalf of users. AI wearables like smart glasses provide continuous first-person video and sensor data, enabling a new paradigm called 'Ego Use' where tasks originate from the user's ongoing physical activities rather than explicit instructions. However, first-person observations are limited by the wearer's viewpoint and can be incomplete or ambiguous, requiring agents to revise their understanding as new observations arrive.

## [Deterministic Forward-Only Paradigm Challenges LLM-Based Weight-Space Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34612) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint introduces Forward Weight-Space Programming (FWSP), a deterministic pipeline that performs structured neural weight editing without an LLM optimizer, achieving 83.3% routing accuracy versus 33.3% for a 27B LLM using the same tool library. It also proposes Protected FisherGate, a closed-form covariance-whitened readout edit that yields 84.2% HumanEval pass@1 on DeepSeek-R1-0528-8B with a single frozen patch. This work challenges the assumption that LLMs are necessary for core weight-space editing, potentially enabling more efficient and reproducible model editing without expensive LLM inference. If validated, it could simplify reasoning LLM optimization and reduce token costs for tasks like code generation and mathematical reasoning. FWSP combines probing, localization, stratified operators (O1-O4), automatic blind routing, and paired verification with zero additional per-token computation. The Protected FisherGate edit beats 512-token-reserved budget forcing by +14.6 percentage points while using 23.4% fewer tokens, and the paper proves two operator boundaries—subspace entanglement and frozen representation span—where FWSP-localized LoRA bridges forward-only and gradient adaptation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Weight-space programming refers to editing the internal parameters of a neural network directly, rather than fine-tuning via gradients or prompting. Recent approaches use LLMs to script these edits with forward passes only, but this paper argues that the underlying algebra of structured editing does not require an LLM. Budget forcing is a technique for controlling reasoning length in LLMs by allocating a token budget, and HumanEval is a benchmark for code generation.

## [HoMe: Evaluation-Driven Optimization of Memory Harnesses for AI Assistants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34624) ⭐️ 7.0/10

HoMe is an evaluation-driven framework that iteratively optimizes memory system harnesses for long-running AI assistants by identifying shared failure mechanisms and reducing evaluation cost. Validated on the open-source ReMe memory system with LongMemEval-S and BEAM benchmarks, HoMe discovered harnesses that improved end-task performance over the original strong harness while consuming far fewer query-time tokens, using only six formal candidate evaluations per benchmark. Memory systems are critical for long-running AI assistants, but optimizing their harnesses (code, prompts, configurations) remains largely manual and ad-hoc. HoMe provides a systematic, cost-effective approach to harness optimization, potentially enabling more reliable and efficient AI assistants that can maintain context over extended interactions. HoMe combines session-level memory versioning with execution traces to localize failures to specific sessions and harness components, and uses candidate gating, smoke tests, adaptive sampling, and reuse of constructed memory to reduce evaluation cost. The approach requires an initial full evaluation but then achieves improvements with only six formal candidate evaluations per benchmark.

**Background**: An agent harness is everything that wraps around an LLM (tool execution, memory, context management, state persistence), excluding the model itself. Memory systems for AI assistants rely on such harnesses to construct, maintain, and retrieve memories across sessions. Optimizing these harnesses is challenging because small changes can have unpredictable effects, and evaluating many candidates is expensive.

## [ZeroHero: Training Sub-50M Language Agents from Scratch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34635) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ZeroHero introduces a framework for training small language agents (SLAs) with fewer than 50 million parameters from scratch, using a large language model as an expert to collect successful trajectories for pretraining, followed by post-training with proximal policy optimization (PPO) and reasoning alignment. The agents were evaluated in Crafter, WebShop, and Alfworld-Dialogue, approaching expert performance on WebShop and surpassing their demonstration experts on Crafter training rollouts and held-out Alfworld-Dialogue tasks. This work shows that specialized language agents may not require massive pretrained models, suggesting a path toward much cheaper and more efficient agent deployment. It could influence how researchers and practitioners build task-specific agents for interactive environments, reducing reliance on large LLMs for every application. The agents are pretrained to predict the expert's responses given interaction history, then fine-tuned on their own interactions via PPO, with reasoning alignment adding expert supervision on generated language alongside environment rewards. Evaluation spans three multistep environments requiring both language understanding and generation, but the work is limited to an arXiv abstract without released code or community discussion.

Crafter is a 2D open-world survival benchmark designed to test deep exploration and generalization in reinforcement learning agents.

## [Failure-Driven Data Flywheel Boosts VLA Model Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34698) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces a failure-driven data flywheel that generates targeted scenarios by inserting task-relevant entities into reconstructed real-world scenes, evaluates VLA models against behavioral requirements, and uses failure maps to prioritize data generation. In autonomous driving tests, five rounds of post-training improved the critical-phase collision-free rate for occluded-pedestrian emergence from 0.261 to 0.579 for AutoVLA and from 0.390 to 0.765 for ReCogDrive, with real-vehicle validation showing ReCogDrive achieving 4 of 5 safe passages versus 1 of 5 for the base model. This approach addresses the data scarcity problem for teams without large deployed fleets, enabling targeted post-training of VLA models to meet specific behavioral requirements. It could accelerate the deployment of autonomous driving systems by efficiently improving safety-critical capabilities like occluded-pedestrian handling and stop-sign yielding. The flywheel re-evaluates failure rates after each round and assigns larger data generation quotas to conditions where the model still fails. Fine-grained evaluation revealed that both VLA models learn anticipatory deceleration before reaching the occluding vehicle, but braking after pedestrian emergence remains limited; for stop-sign yielding, the stop-before-line rate rose from 0.100 to 0.897 for AutoVLA and from 0.000 to 0.667 for ReCogDrive, though sequential training caused a slight decrease in pedestrian collision-free rate (at most 0.032).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) models are pretrained on large-scale data to map visual inputs and language instructions to actions, but they often fail to meet all behavioral requirements of a new platform. Post-training adapts these models to specific tasks, but teams without a large deployed fleet lack the data to address failures. A data flywheel is a iterative process where model failures drive the generation of new training data, which in turn improves the model, creating a self-reinforcing cycle.

## [Theory in context boosts LLM math problem solving, but gains shrink with scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34751) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper measures how much providing a correct mathematical theory in context improves an LLM's problem-solving performance, finding that adding the right theory to Qwen3-14B's context raises pass@1 on pattern waiting-time problems from 0.37 to 0.83 and cuts median tokens for correct solutions from 10.2k to 6.3k, while length-matched worked examples do not help. The study also shows that the gain shrinks as model scale and token budget grow, and that models can partially induce theories themselves, recovering up to 60% of the oracle gain on some planted families. This work quantifies the value of explicit theories for LLM reasoning and shows that models can find theoretical pieces before they can assemble them, which has implications for designing prompting strategies, retrieval-augmented generation, and training methods that encourage theory induction. It also highlights that the benefit of theory is largest for smaller models or limited budgets, suggesting a way to improve efficiency in resource-constrained settings. The study uses a length-matched protocol to control for token count and separates theory induction from recall by planting unpublished theorems in custom probability families. A strong open model required to explore training problems with code before writing recovers 60% of the gain on one planted family, 48% on a second, and almost none on a third, and merging multiple induced notes recovers 61% of the gain on the classic family, above the best single note (47%).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Large language models (LLMs) like Qwen3-14B are increasingly used for mathematical problem solving, but their performance can depend heavily on the context provided. In-context learning allows models to adapt from examples or instructions without weight updates, and pass@1 is a common metric measuring the probability that the top-generated answer is correct. Theory induction refers to a model's ability to derive general principles from specific problem instances, as opposed to recalling memorized theorems.

## [TASK Memory Combats Use-Time Memory Hallucinations in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34762) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper identifies 'use-time memory hallucinations' in LLM agents, where retrieved memory conflicts with parametric knowledge, and proposes TASK memory, which combines text notes, activation steering, and knowledge editing to mitigate these hallucinations. Across five open models, TASK reduces memory-ignoring hallucinations to at most 1.5% and cuts neighbor misuse from 39.2% to 7.5%. This work addresses a critical but under-explored failure mode in retrieval-augmented LLM agents, where correct retrieved information is ignored or misapplied, affecting reliability in knowledge-intensive applications. The proposed method outperforms BM25 RAG and recent context-steering methods, suggesting a practical path to more trustworthy agent memory systems. TASK uses a removable knowledge edit to supply an in-model address via subject key, a pair of steering vectors to decide per-query whether to adopt memory, and a text note for content. On frozen models under a pre-registered test-once protocol, it improves a 300-memory library over BM25 RAG by 16.4 percentage points, an advantage that holds at 3,000 memories, and adds 7.5 pp multi-hop accuracy on MQuAKE.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: LLM agents often use external memory via retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), which retrieves relevant text to supplement the model's parametric knowledge (facts stored in weights). However, conflicts between retrieved memory and parametric knowledge can cause hallucinations, such as ignoring the memory or applying it incorrectly. Activation steering and knowledge editing are techniques to control model behavior by modifying internal activations or weights, respectively.

## [Knowledge-Base Gradient Descent: Self-Structuring Learning through Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34841) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes Knowledge-Base Gradient Descent, a method that iteratively edits the structure of a persistent knowledge base guided by discrepancies between expected answers and responses obtained from the current knowledge base. At each round, it proposes a structural edit, rebuilds the knowledge base, and adopts the edit if the learning loss decreases, with a specific instantiation called Schema Descent for graph schemas. This approach could significantly improve retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems by automatically optimizing knowledge base structures for better question answering, potentially reducing manual effort in knowledge management and enhancing LLM performance on domain-specific queries. The method expresses the loss change of an edit as a derivative with respect to the probability of selecting it by randomizing over candidate edits, and evaluates joint edits as single candidates due to their interactions. Experiments on large documents show that updated knowledge cards and graph schemas achieve higher mean Answer F1 on held-out generated questions, with schema gains in every document, but on human questions, mean F1 rises for schemas and falls for cards, indicating that updated structures serve the kind of question they were learned from.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is a technique that enhances large language models by retrieving relevant information from external knowledge sources before generating responses, reducing hallucinations and enabling access to up-to-date or domain-specific data. The structure of a knowledge base—such as a graph schema or linked documents—determines how effectively an application can retrieve and connect information to answer questions. Traditional gradient descent is an optimization algorithm that iteratively adjusts parameters to minimize a loss function; here, the concept is adapted to edit the discrete structure of a knowledge base.

## [Filing-Evidence benchmark tests small open models on SEC filing extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24990) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Filing-Evidence, a benchmark of 1,000 questions over 90 recent 10-K and 10-Q filings from 45 US companies, and evaluated ten small open instruct models (7-21B parameters) on extracting numbers with machine-checkable evidence (page and verbatim quote). Accuracy ranged from 17.1% to 84.5%, with Qwen3.5-9B achieving the best result, but automatic checkers still accepted 16 to 167 wrong answers per 1,000. This benchmark addresses the critical need for verifiable evidence in financial document extraction, showing that even small models can achieve high accuracy on consumer hardware while revealing that automatic checkers are not yet reliable enough to eliminate human oversight. It provides a rigorous evaluation framework for AI/ML and finance NLP, highlighting the gap between fluent answers and verified ones. The benchmark uses answer keys from filers' inline XBRL tags, and errors in the plain setup were primarily scale/unit errors (57%) and wrong period or column selection (30%). Reasoning mode significantly reduced such errors for 4 of 5 models that offer it, and retrying after a failed check raised accuracy by up to 3.9 points but never reduced it.

## [Gridworld Environment Attributes Failure Modes in Vision-Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33162) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces MultiNet v2.0-Gridworld, an interactive maze environment that independently manipulates four mechanism types (key-door pairs, switch-gate pairs, distractors/decoys, and ordered combinations) to isolate distinct failure modes in vision-language models. Using breadth-first search over the joint state space, it computes exact optimal action counts for precise grading, and in a 540-episode evaluation, three vision-language models solved only 6 of 150 episodes, with no switch-gate episode solved. This work addresses a key limitation in agentic benchmarks where a single success rate conflates different failure causes, providing a diagnostic tool that can guide targeted improvements in vision-language models. It will primarily benefit researchers working on agent evaluation and failure analysis. The environment fixes the renderer, action space, and scoring function while allowing independent addition, modification, or removal of mechanisms, and uses BFS over position, facing direction, inventory, and mechanism state to set difficulty and grade unfinished episodes. The evaluation protocol is fixed on 15 held-out mazes over 540 episodes, then applied to 50 mazes, revealing that 45 of 50 mazes were not solved by any model.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) are AI systems that process both visual and textual information, and they are increasingly used as agents that interact with environments to complete tasks. Agentic benchmarks typically report a single success rate, which makes it hard to tell whether a failure comes from misreading the environment, misunderstanding mechanics, ordering errors, or recovery failures. Gridworlds are simplified 2D environments commonly used in reinforcement learning research to study agent behavior, and breadth-first search (BFS) is a graph traversal algorithm that can find the shortest path in an unweighted state space, here used to compute the minimum number of actions needed to solve each maze.

## [Multimodal LLMs Struggle with Duration Perception, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33176) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.33176) evaluates duration perception in 21 multimodal large language models using a melody recognition task inspired by psychophysics. Most models fail to recognize well-known melodies without labeled in-context examples, and even with examples, only seven achieve reliable recognition, with limited generalization. As multimodal models are increasingly deployed in tasks requiring temporal perception, understanding their limits is crucial for safety and reliability. This study reveals that current models lack robust duration perception, which could impact applications like audio analysis, music recommendation, and human-computer interaction. The study finds that recognition peaks shift with the speed of in-context examples, melody titles induce prediction bias, and providing scientific pitch notation does not prevent accuracy decline at long note durations for most models. In humans, the recognition window is a property of the perceiver; in models, it is largely a property of the prompt.

**Background**: Psychophysics is the quantitative study of the relationship between physical stimuli and the sensations they produce, often used to define perceptual thresholds. Scientific pitch notation is a standardized way to specify musical pitch by combining note names with octave numbers.

## [Neighbor Crowding Predicts Feature Stability in Sparse Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33182) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.33182) shows that cross-seed feature stability in TopK sparse autoencoders can be predicted using six cheap statistics from a single training run, with neighbor crowding—a geometric property of the dictionary—being the strongest predictor. This work could save significant computational cost for mechanistic interpretability researchers, as it eliminates the need to train multiple SAE seeds to identify stable features, making interpretability studies more efficient and scalable. The study examines feature stability across different training token levels and finds that features in dense regions of the unit sphere are more likely to be stable than isolated ones; the six statistics are derived from a single run and are computationally cheap.
