## [RAP: Radial-Angular Random Projection for Hyperbolic Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34298) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Radial-Angular Random Projection (RAP), a dimensionality reduction method for hyperbolic representations that preserves radial coordinates exactly and applies Euclidean random projection only to angular components. RAP achieves the lowest mean relative distortion across various datasets, reducing distortion by approximately 48%–49% over the strongest hyperbolic baseline on broad-radius data. Hyperbolic representations are increasingly used in Transformers, vision-language models, and large language models, but no oblivious dimensionality reduction primitive existed for them. RAP fills this gap, enabling efficient compression of hyperbolic embeddings and potentially improving retrieval performance in vision-language tasks. RAP is hyperbolic-to-hyperbolic by construction, costs one Gaussian matrix-vector product, and reduces hyperbolic compression to a normalized angular Johnson-Lindenstrauss question on the sphere. The paper proves a finite-set guarantee with explicit sinh(√c r_x) sinh(√c r_y) conditioning, complemented by a local tangent-space JL theorem and an arcosh-conditioning lemma for the fully hyperbolic comparator.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Random projection is a basic compression primitive for Euclidean representation learning, with the Johnson-Lindenstrauss lemma guaranteeing low-distortion embeddings into O(ε^{-2} log n) dimensions. Hyperbolic representations, which capture hierarchical and tree-like structures, are gaining attention in modern machine learning, but extending random projection to hyperbolic space is non-trivial due to its curved geometry. The hyperboloid model represents hyperbolic points on a hyperboloid in Minkowski space, and RAP operates directly in this model.

## [Players perceived as AI earn 18% less in group bargaining experiment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34322) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A large-scale experiment with 2,234 participants found that human players presented as AI earned about 18% less per round and were included in fewer final agreements in a repeated three-player majoritarian bargaining game. The payoff gap persisted even when all three players agreed, and smaller payoffs in agreements that included them accounted for most of the difference. The results suggest that social perceptions of AI can create real economic costs for individuals who are represented by or associated with AI, with implications for AI-mediated representation and fairness in collective decision-making. As AI becomes more embedded in bargaining and negotiation, these biases could affect who benefits from agreements. The study used a repeated three-player majoritarian bargaining game where players saw opponents labeled as Human or AI, and instructions described AI as acting independently or on behalf of a human, but all decisions were made by humans. The payoff gap was driven mostly by smaller payoffs in agreements that included the AI-labeled players, and a gap remained even when all three players agreed.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Bargaining games are standard tools in experimental economics for studying how people negotiate and share resources. In majoritarian bargaining, a majority coalition can determine outcomes, so being included in agreements is crucial. Previous research has shown that people may exhibit algorithm aversion—distrust or dislike of algorithmic decision-makers—which can influence economic interactions.

Researchers introduced LUDIUS, a benchmark that evaluates vision-language models on embodied social intelligence through 15 social skills across six simulated household editions, each spanning one simulated week. Three editions cover all skills in different homes, while the other three focus on multi-person interactions, safety, and context-specific norms. This benchmark addresses the underexplored area of social intelligence in human-robot interaction, providing a structured way to measure how well vision-language models can reason about people's perspectives and act appropriately. It could drive progress in embodied AI and help standardize evaluation for socially aware robots. The evaluation harness supplies models with camera views and resident reactions, retrieves interaction history, and converts tool calls into robot verbal responses and actions. Models struggle most when reasoning about what a resident saw or heard earlier, reconciling competing interests, or fulfilling requests under safety and household rules.

## [Puzzling-Bench: A 23,777-Puzzle Benchmark for Evaluating LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34331) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Puzzling-Bench, a benchmark of 23,777 human-written puzzles with community-verified answers drawn from the Puzzling Stack Exchange archive (2014–2026), covering riddles, ciphers, logic grids, lateral-thinking problems, wordplay, and recreational mathematics, with a third including images. They evaluated nine frontier and open models using a two-tier scorer (normalized exact match then an LLM judge), finding that the best model, GPT-6.1, reaches 74.8% on a shared 255-puzzle set, while a hard core of 17 puzzles defeats all nine models. This benchmark provides a large, diverse, and contamination-aware testbed for LLM reasoning, with a post-cutoff slice of 254 puzzles posted after the training cutoff of six of the nine models, including the best one. It reveals that model failures are strongly correlated and that even the second-best model contributes only a single puzzle to a greedy ensemble, highlighting limited reasoning diversity among current LLMs. The benchmark includes per-puzzle metadata such as author-accepted answers, LLM-extracted reference answers (checked on a 50-puzzle human review), author hints, community tags, and creation dates, enabling hint ablations and category analysis. The LLM judge was validated with three judges from two model families, showing 95–97% agreement (kappa 0.82–0.96), moving no model by more than 1.6 points, and no own-family preference; on a 100-puzzle multimodal pilot, withholding the image costs the two best models 24 and 35 points.

**Background**: Puzzling Stack Exchange is a community Q&A site where users post puzzles and others submit answers, with the author accepting the correct one; this archive provides a natural source of human-written puzzles with verified solutions. LLM benchmarks often suffer from contamination, where test data leaks into training corpora, inflating scores; a post-cutoff slice helps detect this by using puzzles created after models' training data was collected. LLM-as-a-judge is a common evaluation method where a strong LLM grades another model's output, but it requires validation for bias and consistency.

## [SC-AIRL models state-dependent bounded foresight for human decision-making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34351) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SC-AIRL (State-Conditional Adversarial IRL) introduces a planner with per-depth policies that share a reward but differ in explicit planning horizon, mixed by a state-conditional router. On pedestrian crossing and highway overtaking tasks, it raises top-1 imitation accuracy from 0.549 to 0.647 and from 0.629 to 0.718 over Standard AIRL, while inferred planning depth correlates with self-reported anxiety and motor impulsivity. This work addresses a key mismatch between standard IRL's infinite-horizon assumption and humans' bounded, state-dependent foresight, improving behavioral modeling in naturalistic tasks. It also offers an interpretable, model-derived measure of planning depth that could link computational models to psychological traits, benefiting researchers in IRL, cognitive modeling, and human behavior analysis. The method uses per-depth policies that share one reward but sum it over different explicit horizons, with a state-conditional router mixing them based on the current state. Evaluated on held-out episodes in pedestrian crossing and highway overtaking, SC-AIRL also more closely reproduces participant behavior in rollouts, and the inferred planning depth correlates with anxiety and motor impulsivity.

**Background**: Inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) infers the latent reward function underlying observed behavior, often assuming an effectively infinite planning horizon. Adversarial IRL (AIRL) frames reward learning as a adversarial game between a discriminator and a policy, but still typically assumes long-horizon planning. Human decision-making, however, involves bounded foresight that varies by state, which standard IRL does not capture.

## [Soft-prompt skills fail to compose in single call on frozen language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34398) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.34398) shows that independently learned soft-prompt skills on frozen language models achieve at most 7.6% mean composition accuracy in a single call, compared with 88.2–100% when executed over two calls with an explicit intermediate output. It also finds that blocks of the same operation are not interchangeable: replacing learned partner blocks with independently learned blocks of the same operations drops median composition accuracy from 98.0% and 95.3% to 12.7% and 0.5% on two Qwen bases, even though each replacement scores at least 90% alone. This finding challenges the assumption that acquiring individual skills is sufficient to build a reusable skill library for self-evolving agents, showing that compatibility and retention must be evaluated alongside acquisition. It has direct implications for agent architectures that rely on modular soft prompts or skill blocks, as naive composition can fail catastrophically even when each skill works well in isolation. Neutral-insertion training improves robustness but leaves substantial interference that block length and per-token norm alone cannot explain on two of four digit bases. Repairing a composition can impair the original skill, and rehearsal improves retention but transfer remains limited on Qwen2.5-0.5B; internal interventions constrain explanations without identifying a shared failure mechanism.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Soft prompts are trainable continuous vectors prepended to a frozen language model's input, allowing task-specific behavior without changing model weights. In agent systems, skills are often learned independently as such soft prompts and then expected to compose at inference time, but this paper shows that task proficiency alone does not guarantee a working compositional interface. The study uses digit-task bases and record-editing models, including Qwen2.5-0.5B, to test composition, interchangeability, and retention.

## [CHASE: Curiosity-Guided Skill Acquisition for Embodied Task Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34416) ⭐️ 7.0/10

CHASE is a curiosity-guided framework that enables robots to resolve task uncertainty and acquire missing skills during execution through hypothesis-driven planning and verified skill learning. It achieves 50-80% task success under combined information and capability gaps, with 70-90% evidence resolution in simulation and real-robot experiments. This approach addresses a critical challenge in robotics: operating in partially observed environments with incomplete skills. By closing the loop between uncertainty resolution and capability acquisition, CHASE could enable more autonomous and adaptable robots in real-world settings, impacting embodied AI research and applications. The framework consists of four modules connecting dynamic scene understanding, hypothesis-driven planning, on-demand skill acquisition, and verified execution. The planner balances information value, skill reuse, and acquisition/execution cost, while missing skills trigger bounded exploration, learning from verified segments, and held-out admission with separate checks for physical effects and acquired evidence.

**Background**: In embodied AI, robots often face partially observed environments where they must make decisions with incomplete information and limited skills. Researchers introduced OVTSID3, a training-free framework that unifies region grouping, concept identification, and temporal comparison within DINOv3's representation space, supporting both zero-shot textual queries and one-shot visual prompting via a masked reference pair. It outperforms all prior training-free open-vocabulary change detection methods on four benchmarks, raising average F1 by 8.9 points, and achieves state-of-the-art on the SECOND semantic change detection benchmark. OVTSID3 is training-free and leverages DINOv3's self-supervised representations, supporting zero-shot textual queries or one-shot visual prompting through a masked reference pair; it also extends to semantic change detection. The framework was evaluated on two building and two natural-disaster benchmarks, though details on computational cost and scalability are not provided in the abstract.

## [Dianjing: Minimal Adaptation Turns Video Prior into Action-Conditioned World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34435) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Dianjing, a minimal adaptation recipe that converts a strong video foundation model (MiniMax H3, 33B parameters) into an action-conditioned world model by freezing the backbone and training only 0.34% of parameters on about 65 hours of robot video across two embodiments. The recipe uses unified physical action conditioning, concentrates training at high noise levels, and adds native history conditioning, achieving top scores on four of five WorldOracle tracks. This work suggests that strong video priors already provide most of what is needed for world modeling, so lightweight action grounding could enable scalable policy evaluation and improvement for robotics without costly real-world rollouts. It lowers the barrier for building action-conditioned world models and is directly relevant to embodied AI research. Controlled ablations show that where training is allocated across noise levels matters more than how actions enter the network. The method requires no additional robot-specific pretraining or camera calibration, and on WorldOracle it ranks second on the AgiBot-Long track.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

Dianjing bridges this gap by minimally adapting a large video foundation model to be conditioned on robot actions.

## [Graph Anchors Fix Latent World Model Planning Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34509) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The authors identify two failure modes of latent-distance goal metrics in world-model planners: loss of directional signal near the goal and over-weighting of the robot arm over the manipulated object. They propose Graph Anchors, a training-free planner that replaces distant goals with nearby graph-anchored targets, raising success rates on PointMaze-Medium from 13% to 85%, on PointMaze-Large from 8% to 87%, and on Push-T from 5% to 71% for goals up to 400 steps away. This work provides a simple, training-free fix that significantly improves long-horizon planning in frozen latent world models, which could make model-based RL more practical for robotics tasks requiring contact-rich manipulation and distant goals. It also offers a diagnostic ranking test to predict where closed-loop planning will break down. The method builds a sparse graph over the model's training frames once and aims each planning step at a node a few moves ahead along the shortest path to the goal, keeping paths on recorded transitions where contact matters. The ranking test cleanly separates the two failures across five environments, six visual representations (including raw pixels and V-JEPA 2), and nine Meta-World tasks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Latent world models learn a compact representation of the environment and predict action outcomes in that latent space, enabling planning without direct interaction. Planners typically judge a plan by the latent distance between the predicted end state and a goal image, but this distance can be misleading. Graph Anchors addresses this by using a graph of recorded transitions to guide planning toward nearby, reachable targets instead of distant goals.

## [StreamRecall Benchmark and EviStream Memory Target Streaming Video QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34545) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces StreamRecall, a benchmark for context-aware multi-evidence reasoning in streaming video question answering, containing 4,983 questions over 462 videos with context-aligned query times and fine-grained temporal evidence annotations. It also proposes the Evidence Utilization Cascade for diagnosing model reasoning and presents EviStream, a training-free causal visual memory mechanism that preserves reasoning-critical evidence under a fixed KV budget. Existing video question answering benchmarks mainly evaluate models in offline settings, while real-world streaming scenarios require retrieving and reasoning over evidence distributed across video history. StreamRecall addresses this gap and reveals substantial gaps between current vision-language models and realistic streaming requirements, providing a foundation for evidence-aware streaming video intelligence. The benchmark includes 4,983 questions over 462 videos with context-aligned query times and fine-grained temporal evidence annotations. EviStream is a training-free causal visual memory mechanism that operates under a fixed KV budget, and the Evidence Utilization Cascade diagnoses reasoning through evidence localization, grounding, chain completion, and answer derivation.

## [SFT Can Retain Prior Capabilities with Joint Hyperparameter Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34579) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.34579) challenges the belief that supervised fine-tuning (SFT) inherently causes catastrophic forgetting. It shows that by jointly tuning hyperparameters for both new-task gain and retention—specifically using lower learning rates and batch sizes—vanilla SFT can retain prior capabilities with little to no forgetting, performing competitively with Self-Distillation Fine-Tuning (SDFT), an on-policy method designed to mitigate forgetting. This finding could significantly influence fine-tuning practices, as it suggests that careful hyperparameter selection can mitigate forgetting without resorting to more complex on-policy methods. It also raises broader questions about the root causes of catastrophic forgetting and challenges the assumption that on-policy learning is essential for continual learning. The paper demonstrates that while maximizing new-task performance can induce catastrophic forgetting, alternative hyperparameter choices (lower learning rate and batch size) yield similar gains with minimal forgetting. Although SDFT can be more robust to hyperparameter choices, it still requires a retention-aware criterion to avoid forgetting, whereas vanilla SFT with joint tuning can be competitive.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Catastrophic forgetting refers to the drastic loss of previously acquired capabilities when a neural network learns new tasks, a central challenge in continual learning. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is a common method to adapt pre-trained language models to specific tasks, but it is widely believed to be prone to such forgetting. Recent works attributed this to SFT's off-policy nature, arguing that on-policy learning (where the model learns from its own generated outputs) is key to retention, leading to methods like Self-Distillation Fine-Tuning (SDFT).

## [VisualMontage: Authentic Image Sequences Can Manipulate Vision-Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34603) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces VisualMontage, a multi-agent framework that simulates 'visual cognitive collusion' attacks, where coordinated authentic images and locally faithful captions trick vision-language model (VLM) agents into accepting attacker-chosen claims. The authors also release a benchmark of 544 target cases and 33.2K candidate images across eight domains, and evaluate the attack on 12 VLMs, achieving mean success rates of 61.93% for proprietary models and 68.71% for open-weight models. This work exposes a novel security threat: even without fake images, coordinated authentic posts can manipulate VLM agents' reasoning and amplify misinformation on social media. The high cross-model transferability and residual success rates after defenses highlight an urgent need for safeguards that assess how collections of posts jointly shape agent conclusions. The attack uses identical feeds across models without victim-specific optimization, yet achieves 61.93% (proprietary) and 68.71% (open-weight) mean success, with 92.65% of generated feeds passing independent model-based validity auditing. Even after downstream review, residual attack success rates range from 35.3% to 76.7%, and controlled comparisons show captions can increase model-rated posting naturalness while reducing attack success.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) are AI systems that jointly interpret images and text, enabling tasks like visual question answering and image captioning. As VLM-based agents increasingly browse and analyze social-media content, adversaries can exploit their multimodal reasoning. 'Cognitive collusion' refers to coordinated strategies that exploit cognitive biases and consensus dynamics to mislead decision-making, and this paper extends that concept to the visual domain by combining authentic images with locally faithful captions.

## [Survey Systematizes Security Assurance for Model-as-a-Service](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34623) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper decomposes Model-as-a-Service (MaaS) into a five-layer stack with five trust boundaries, defines six adversary classes and six security goals, and uses this framework to code 120 studies and evaluate 50 defenses under unified criteria. It finds that 44.2% of research focuses on behavioral integrity, while service integrity, availability/cost, and accountability together account for only 13.3%. This systematization highlights major research gaps in service integrity, availability, and accountability, and shows that defenses are evaluated on production services at less than half the rate of attacks. It provides a unified taxonomy that can guide both researchers and practitioners in securing LLM cloud services. The paper also notes that none of the cryptographic or hardware mechanisms that protect against the provider can be adopted unilaterally by customers, and it proposes a research agenda by comparison with current standards and regulations.

## [DecisionReplay: A Corpus and Simulator for Ensembling Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34627) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DecisionReplay releases a corpus of probability vectors from 17 commercial decision models across 35 benchmarks, along with a deterministic offline simulator that replays any ensemble, router, or cascade policy without new model calls. The paper reports empirical findings: pooling all models loses 2.7 accuracy points to the best single model per benchmark, stacking gains 1.0 point at 3.4x cost, and two models that are both wrong choose the same wrong option 72% of the time. This dataset and simulator enable fast, cheap, and reproducible evaluation of ensemble, routing, and cascade methods, which are increasingly important for optimizing cost and accuracy when using multiple LLMs. The findings challenge the assumption that pooling models is beneficial and highlight correlated errors, informing future research and system design. The corpus includes token counts, billed cost, and measured latency for every call, and the simulator is deterministic, allowing any policy to be replayed exactly. The best router matches the best single model at 64% of its cost, and certified early exit cuts the cost of a stacked ensemble by 43% without changing an answer.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Ensembling, routing, and cascading are techniques that combine multiple models to improve accuracy or reduce cost. Ensembling aggregates outputs (e.g., by pooling probabilities or stacking), routing selects a model per query, and cascading runs models in sequence with early exit. Decision models are endpoints that return a probability for every answer option, enabling probabilistic combination. Evaluating these methods typically requires running all models on all items, which is expensive; DecisionReplay provides a precomputed corpus to simulate policies offline.

## [LatentRL-QAS Enables Transfer Across Qubit Counts for Quantum Architecture Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34634) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LatentRL-QAS introduces a latent space transfer framework for reinforcement learning-based quantum architecture search, where a circuit variational autoencoder encodes circuits on any number of qubits into a fixed-size latent space. This allows an RL agent to operate with the same state, action, and network size regardless of qubit count, and it discovers five- and six-qubit absolutely maximally entangled (AME) states in 89-99% of evaluation episodes, outperforming a baseline RL-QAS with a 10-17x larger network. This work addresses a key limitation in RL-based quantum architecture search: the inability to transfer learned knowledge across different problem sizes. By decoupling the RL agent from the number of qubits, it provides a foundation for transferable circuit design across quantum optimization problems, potentially accelerating the discovery of quantum circuits for larger systems. The latent space is periodically refreshed with the agent's new discoveries and grown from smaller-system solutions for larger systems, so what is transferred is the representation of circuits, not network weights or past experience. The method remains robust under depolarizing noise with single-qubit gate errors amplified 10x and two-qubit gate errors 5x compared with IBM quantum hardware.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Quantum architecture search (QAS) is the automated design of quantum circuits, often using reinforcement learning (RL) where an agent adds gates sequentially. However, standard RL-QAS agents have state and action spaces that grow with the number of qubits, making it hard to transfer a trained agent from small to large systems. A variational autoencoder (VAE) is a generative model that learns a compressed latent representation of data, here used to encode entire circuits into fixed-size vectors. Absolutely maximally entangled (AME) states are highly entangled quantum states useful for quantum error correction.

## [SAE feature ablations often fail to transfer across random seeds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34637) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.34637) shows that when a feature is removed from a sparse autoencoder (SAE) trained on a DINOv2 or SigLIP vision encoder layer, the same removal applied to the matched feature from an SAE trained with a different random seed produces a similar change in encoder output for only 12% to 70% of matched pairs, depending on the setting. The share drops to 0% in two control conditions: an SAE with random directions and a post-hoc shuffle of matches among trained features. This result is a cautionary finding for mechanistic interpretability: feature ablations, a common tool for causal validation of SAE features, may not generalize across independently trained SAEs, undermining reproducibility and cross-study comparisons. Researchers who rely on single-seed SAEs to make causal claims about model internals should be aware that matched features can point alike yet respond to different image parts. The study fixes a DINOv2 or SigLIP layer and its SAE feature count, removes a feature and its matched counterpart, and compares changes in encoder output on the same held-out images; at the chosen tolerance, removal carries for 12%–70% of matched pairs across three settings. Post hoc, layer contributions predict which removals carry more accurately than feature directions, but some incorrect predictions persist even on independent images, so pairs can be screened at the layer while measuring the output detects errors the screen misses.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) decompose a model's activations into a set of learned directions, called features, most of which have zero coefficient for any given input; researchers use them to interpret and edit models by removing individual features. Because SAEs are trained with random initialization, different random seeds yield different feature sets, and earlier work matches features across SAEs one-to-one by how closely their directions agree. This paper tests whether such matched features are functionally equivalent by comparing the effect of ablating each feature on the encoder's output.

MotionRep introduces a visual representation world model that jointly generates future DINO features and 3D object flow for object-centric dynamics, using a DINO-VAE for compression and a unified diffusion transformer. The model conditions on an initial observation, object query points, and a language instruction, and employs asymmetric modality-specific noise schedules so the visual stream guides flow generation during joint denoising. It outperforms pixel-based baselines in 3D object flow accuracy and generalization to unseen scenes, while requiring only three denoising steps for inference. DINO-VAE combines temporal and channel compression with latent regularization to make high-dimensional DINO features amenable to generative modeling while preserving semantic and geometric information. The unified diffusion transformer uses joint attention to predict future visual representations and 3D object flow, with asymmetric noise schedules keeping the visual stream ahead of the flow stream.

A new arXiv paper proposes requiring permissioned-blockchain endorsers to verify a succinct proof that a maintenance prescription was produced by the agreed model and decision rule before releasing a work order. On a four-organization Hyperledger Fabric testbed, this approach stopped all nine contested or mis-operated prescriptions, whereas simple hash checks accepted four problematic ones. ## [DemoFoundry uses coding agents and generative rendering to create photorealistic robot demos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34671) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DemoFoundry is an automated framework that couples coding agents with generative rendering to synthesize photorealistic and physically grounded robot demonstrations. Coding agents construct aligned real-proxy video pairs to train a video renderer and write executable interaction programs that, when run in a physics simulator, produce proxy videos with aligned robot states and actions, which are then translated into photorealistic observations. This approach addresses the visual gap in simulation-based robot learning and the difficulty of obtaining aligned action labels from video-only generation, potentially enabling scalable production of high-quality demonstrations. Policies trained on these generated demonstrations outperform raw-simulation training and approach the performance of training on an equal number of real demonstrations, which could significantly reduce the cost and effort of collecting real-world robot data. The framework requires only a scene reference and task instruction, without target-task human demonstrations, and uses automated task-execution checks to select successful trajectories for rendering and policy learning. The video renderer reduces the need for detailed appearance modeling in each scene by translating proxy videos into scene-consistent, photorealistic observations.

Generative rendering uses generative models to produce realistic images or videos, and coding agents are AI systems that can write and execute code to accomplish tasks. DemoFoundry combines these techniques to generate synthetic demonstrations that are both visually realistic and physically consistent, helping to bridge the sim-to-real gap.

RoboEvo² introduces a recursive harness-policy co-evolution framework in which policy failures guide a coding agent to discover reusable skills and strategies, and verified trajectories train the policy via Counterfactual Preference Distillation (CPD). This work suggests a path for embodied AI systems to improve autonomously by turning their own failures into training signal and reusable code, potentially reducing reliance on costly human demonstrations. If validated, such recursive self-improvement could make vision-language-action policies more adaptable to unfamiliar environments and new embodiments. The framework alternates between Policy-to-Harness (P2H) evolution, where a coding agent searches Code, Code+ICL, and Code+Policy routes, and Harness-to-Policy (H2P) evolution, where verified trajectories provide supervised training and CPD teaches the policy to prefer successful corrections over failures from the same starting state. Experiments also show autonomous task acquisition, cross-embodiment harness transfer, and 95.0% hybrid success on the Unitree G1-D, though the work remains an arXiv preprint without peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies are models that map visual observations and language instructions directly to robot actions, offering efficient control but often requiring human demonstrations to handle unfamiliar settings. Reasoning agents can use broad prior knowledge and robot-control APIs to discover solutions, but repeated deliberation and repair are costly. Harness-policy co-evolution refers to jointly evolving the runtime scaffolding (harness) around an agent and the underlying policy, an idea also explored in prior work such as SafeEvolve. Counterfactual preference distillation borrows from counterfactual thinking—considering alternative outcomes—to train the policy to favor successful corrections over failures from the same state.

## [ClueRL: RL method improves entity recognition in thinking VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34681) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2611.34681) analyzes thinking vision-language models on knowledge-based visual question answering and finds that 76.3% of errors on ViQuAE stem from entity misrecognition, with 84.9% of these resolved when the entity name is provided. The authors propose ClueRL, a reinforcement learning approach that extends Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with an advantage localized to entity-recall clues in the thinking trace, outperforming GRPO and a perception-reward baseline on three entity recognition benchmarks. This work pinpoints entity recognition as the primary bottleneck in thinking VLMs for knowledge-based VQA and shows that a targeted RL signal can improve recognition without injecting new knowledge, with gains generalizing to general multimodal reasoning (e.g., +4.4 and +4.0 points on ScienceQA and MMMU subsets of MMStar for Qwen3.5-4B). It suggests a more efficient way to enhance VLM reasoning by changing how models attend to visual details rather than adding knowledge. ClueRL modifies GRPO by localizing the advantage to entity-recall clues—distinctive visual details that help recall the entity—rather than providing new entity knowledge. The method is trained only on entity recognition but the clue-seeking behavior generalizes beyond that task, and the paper reports improvements on three entity recognition benchmarks and on knowledge-based VQA.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Knowledge-based visual question answering (e.g., the ViQuAE dataset) requires recognizing an entity in an image and then recalling knowledge about it. Thinking VLMs generate long reasoning traces before answering, but how well they perform each step is underexplored. GRPO is a reinforcement learning algorithm that optimizes policies using group-based normalization, commonly used for reasoning models.

## [Maco-RL scales mixed-task reinforcement learning with quota scheduling and Tri-Buffer Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34703) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Maco-RL introduces a quota-based scheduler for asynchronous rollouts across task domains and Tri-Buffer Sampling (TBS) to allocate compute among samples with varying mastery levels. Experiments on five benchmarks show it raises Qwen3.5-27B's overall score from 43.6 to 54.2, and to 60.1 with continual expansion, matching Qwen3.8-27B's frontier and pushing it further by +5.7. This work suggests that mixed-task reinforcement learning can be a practical and compute-efficient post-training approach for building general agent capabilities, potentially reducing the need for separate specialists or an extra distillation stage. It also outperforms single-task RL with less compute for policy updates, which could lower training costs for multi-domain agents. The quota-based scheduler stabilizes training by balancing gradient contributions and mitigating off-policy effects, while TBS promotes sustained frontier exploration while preserving acquired capabilities. The results are demonstrated on Qwen3.5-27B and Qwen3.8-27B models across five benchmarks, but the abstract does not specify the benchmarks or the exact compute savings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Mixed-task reinforcement learning (MixRL) trains a single unified policy across multiple domains to continually expand an agent's capabilities without training separate specialists or requiring a distillation stage. Scaling MixRL is challenging because different domains can produce imbalanced gradient updates and off-policy effects, and because compute must be allocated efficiently across tasks with varying difficulty. Maco-RL addresses these issues with a quota-based scheduler for asynchronous rollouts and a sampling method called Tri-Buffer Sampling.

## [CARE-OPD: Adaptive On-Policy Distillation for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34756) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces CARE-OPD, an adaptive and efficient on-policy distillation method that improves trajectory coverage and evidence-based replay to reduce rollout costs for long-horizon agents. On SWE-bench Verified, it raises task success to 59.5% while reducing end-to-end training time by 45% and environment actions by 44% relative to asynchronous online OPD. This work addresses key inefficiencies in training long-horizon LLM agents, where online distillation is costly due to frequent environment rollouts. By reducing training time and environment interactions while improving success rates, CARE-OPD could make agent training more practical and scalable for complex tasks like software engineering and deep research. CARE-OPD combines student-seeded teacher completions with response-level confidence gating to expand history coverage while filtering unreliable updates, and uses hindsight replay allocation to select consequential turns based on downstream execution evidence. It outperforms online OPD and prior prefix replay baselines across long-horizon tasks in Software Engineering and Deep Research.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student model using its own generated trajectories, with supervision from a teacher model, but for long-horizon agents this requires costly fresh environment rollouts for each update. Prefix replay is an alternative that reuses stored histories by sampling current-student responses from them, reducing idle time and allowing multiple updates per trajectory. However, existing prefix replay methods suffer from limited history coverage and fixed replay schedules that miss critical decisions.

## [Unified Scoring and J++-Lens Improve Backdoor Detection in Fine-Tuned Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34758) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces a common scoring system based on token readouts to evaluate weight, activation, and output-based model diffing methods for backdoor detection in fine-tuning. It also improves existing weight and activation based methods by incorporating the J-lens and its refinement, the J++-lens, showing that the ΔW J++ method ranks a backdoor token in the top-10 in 11 of 12 settings. This work provides a standardized way to compare model diffing techniques for backdoor detection, which is crucial for AI security as fine-tuned models are widely deployed. It highlights that no single method is robust against all attacks, motivating the need for combined defenses. The strongest single method, ΔW J++, decodes the weight difference through the J++-lens of the base model and ranks a backdoor-naming token among its first ten tokens in 11 of 12 settings. However, output and activation based methods like ADL and Diff Mining can break if the backdoored model is penalized for giving backdoor words probability on clean inputs, while weight-based methods are unaffected; conversely, permuting hidden units or training only residual stream reading layers hides the backdoor from weight-based methods but not from ADL and Diff Mining.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Backdoor attacks in machine learning involve injecting a trigger into a model during training so that it behaves maliciously on inputs containing the trigger while performing normally otherwise. Model diffing is a technique that compares a fine-tuned model with its base model to identify internal changes, such as weight or activation differences, that may indicate a backdoor. The J-lens and J++-lens are interpretability tools that project model weights or activations into token space to reveal what the model is "thinking" at each layer.

## [Stateful Deception as a Cost-Exchange Primitive for AI-Era Cyber Defense](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34770) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper proposes stateful, adversary-specific deception as a cost-exchange primitive for AI-era cyber defense, formalized via a Deception Exchange Rate (DER) and instantiated in an LLM-backed SSH honeypot that pairs Cowrie with a local LLM. The authors report that deterministic-layer and prompt optimizations cut per-session defender cost by around 63% against a naive all-LLM decoy across five open-weight models. This work reframes cyber deception around economic cost exchange rather than realism or detection, offering a metric (DER) that could help defenders justify and optimize deception investments as LLMs lower attacker costs. It is relevant to security teams and researchers exploring AI-era defense strategies, though the paper is early-stage with limited empirical validation. The design keeps each recognized adversary in a persistent, isolated state coherent with its own past actions, while the LLM acts only as a constrained renderer of explicit state, never as its store. The paper focuses on defender-side evaluation of the exchange, while specifying but not yet measuring per-adversary state and the adversary side of the DER.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Honeypots are decoy systems designed to attract and observe attackers; Cowrie is a widely used open-source SSH/Telnet honeypot that logs attacker activity. Traditional honeypots often reset or merge sessions, which can reveal their nature or limit the realism of persistent engagement. Stateful systems, by contrast, track session information across requests, and this paper applies that concept to deception by maintaining adversary-specific persistent state. The Deception Exchange Rate (DER) is a proposed metric relating mission-weighted adversary effort burned to defender resources spent.

## [SPLICE: Fixing All-Fail Groups in Long-Horizon Agent RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34777) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SPLICE introduces a method that constructs local training targets from failed current-policy trajectories online, using a proposal model to select splice points and generate bounded options, then estimates their value via repeated current-policy continuations. Evaluated on SWE-bench Verified and BrowseComp-Plus across two policy families, it outperforms GRPO and full-trajectory guidance baselines by 2.8–9.8 percentage points and raises GRPO's graduation rate on initially failed SWE tasks by 10.8 percentage points. This addresses a critical bottleneck in group-based reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards for long-horizon agent tasks, where all-fail groups provide no learning signal. By avoiding expensive full-trajectory guidance from stronger models, SPLICE offers a more efficient and scalable way to train LLM agents on complex tasks like software engineering and deep research. SPLICE estimates option values using repeated current-policy continuations, converts positive estimates into a return-weighted local target, and fits the current policy on the options. It also reduces costly proposal-model computation compared to full-trajectory guidance, though the method relies on a proposal model to select splice points and generate options.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Group-based reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (like GRPO) samples multiple trajectories per prompt and uses group-relative rewards to compute policy gradients. On long-horizon agent tasks, such as software engineering with SWE-bench or deep research, it is common for all sampled trajectories to fail, yielding zero reward and no gradient. A common remedy is to supply complete solutions from a stronger model, but full trajectories are expensive and expose the learner to long off-policy histories. SPLICE instead constructs local training targets from failed trajectories online, using a proposal model to splice in bounded options and estimate their value.

## [PersonaFactory generates 1.24M persona-styled synthetic conversations in 85 languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34785) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PersonaFactory introduces an end-to-end pipeline that creates over 740,000 structured synthetic personas and uses them to augment user prompts, producing roughly 1.24 million persona-styled synthetic conversations across 85 languages. The paper reports that its attribute-based style representation enables per-axis control and better intent preservation than free-text persona descriptions, with human experts rating the augmented conversations as plausible. This work addresses a known limitation of LLM-generated synthetic data—monolingual and stylistically flat user prompts—by injecting diverse persona-driven styles, which could improve AI alignment, evaluation, and multilingual research. The large-scale dataset and demonstrated downstream benefits for assistant persona control make it a valuable resource for developers training and evaluating conversational models. The evaluation combines LLM-judge and heuristic metrics to assess augmentation fidelity and intent preservation, and the augmented conversations perform comparably to original training data on capability benchmarks while being preferred on real user prompts at equal reply length. However, the work is an arXiv preprint without community discussion yet, and its impact, while significant, is not groundbreaking.

## [Coding agents outperform video models on visual reasoning benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34813) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper benchmarks coding agents that write rendering programs against video generation models on the VBVR-Pro-Bench visual reasoning suite, finding that three closed-model agents (Codex with gpt-6-astra, Claude Code with Fable 5.1, and Gemini CLI with Gemini 3.1 Pro) score 0.923, 0.894, and 0.893 respectively, each more than 0.22 above the strongest video model. The results challenge the assumption that native visual reasoning—where reasoning unfolds directly in generated frames—is the most reliable path, suggesting that for tasks computable from a first frame and prompt, program synthesis via coding agents is currently a substantially more reliable route and could reshape how visual reasoning systems are designed. On the 500-instance VBVR-Pro-Bench spanning five cognitive categories with symbolic ground truth, agents received only the first frame and prompt with no access to ground truth, metadata, or generation models, and one attempt per instance; among 23 runnable open-weight models, the best (GLM-5) reached 0.572, above all video models except the RL-trained one, and the paper distinguishes depictive programs that recover a transferable scheme from propositional programs that operate on hard-coded values.

## [Cross-Architecture Initialization Transfers Optimization Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34816) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes aligning a target network's initialization to the representational geometry of a randomly initialized guide network from a different architecture, showing that this transfers an optimization prior and benefits training. Experiments demonstrate that aligning shallow networks to deeper ones instills depth-like optimization benefits, and aligning coarse networks to fine-resolution ones transfers representational granularity, both improving downstream performance. This work reframes initialization as an optimization prior that can be decoupled from architecture, potentially enabling models to inherit training benefits of larger or different architectures without changing their physical structure. It could impact how practitioners design initialization and training strategies, especially for resource-constrained scenarios. The alignment procedure uses no labels, never receives gradients, and cannot transfer learned features, as the guide network is randomly initialized. The method is evaluated on two tasks: instilling depth benefits into shallow networks and transferring representational granularity from fine to coarse networks.

This paper explores whether a network's initial geometry can be shaped by a different architecture to improve training outcomes.

## [JEPA and RSSM world models differ in epistemic uncertainty propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34830) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.34830) presents a controlled empirical comparison of post-hoc epistemic uncertainty estimation between LeWorldModel (a JEPA-based model) and DreamerV3 (an RSSM-based model) across ten simulated visual-control environments. The study finds that RSSM attenuates state perturbations through its recurrent averaging and reconstruction objective, while JEPA passes changes directly to the uncertainty estimate. This work highlights that the reliability of model-based decision making depends not only on the uncertainty estimator but also on the underlying world model's training objective and architecture. The findings could influence how researchers choose and design world models for safety-critical applications where detecting unfamiliar states is crucial. The comparison uses last-layer Laplace approximation and ensembles as post-hoc uncertainty methods, and evaluates five downstream tasks including unfamiliar-state detection and uncertainty-penalised planning. The RSSM's reconstruction objective weights entities by pixel coverage, so small objects can be lost, whereas JEPA's decoder-free embedding prediction avoids this bias.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: World models are learned simulators that predict environment dynamics, allowing agents to plan by evaluating candidate actions without real-world interaction. JEPA (Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture) predicts future latent representations without reconstructing observations, while RSSM (Recurrent State-Space Model), used in DreamerV3, reconstructs observations from a recurrent latent state. Epistemic uncertainty signals where a model's experience ends, which is essential for safe decision making.

## [Repairing Trustworthiness Regressions in Distilled Language Models Without Original Corpus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34831) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper proposes a targeted repair framework based on the proximal Bregman response function (PBRF) that fixes trustworthiness regressions in distilled language models without needing the original distillation corpus. The method estimates the required curvature using a surrogate proxy dataset and extends to preference-based supervision via an odds-ratio objective, improving trustworthy behaviors by up to 8% with less than 1% perplexity increase. Knowledge distillation is widely used to compress large language models for deployment, but trustworthiness can degrade even when downstream performance is preserved. This work offers a practical alternative to costly re-distillation or unconstrained preference optimization, which is important for the safe deployment of compressed models. The approach removes dependence on the original transfer corpus by estimating PBRF curvature from a surrogate proxy dataset, and it constrains changes to the distilled model when using preferred and dispreferred response pairs. Experiments across different distilled models show up to 8% improvement in trustworthiness with less than 1% perplexity decrement, for both pre-training and post-training models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Knowledge distillation trains a smaller student model to mimic a larger teacher model, enabling efficient deployment of large language models. However, compression can introduce regressions in trustworthiness (e.g., safety, fairness) even when task performance is maintained. Direct preference optimization (DPO) is a popular method for aligning models with human preferences, but applying it directly to distilled models may alter capabilities inherited from the teacher. The proximal Bregman response function (PBRF) is a technique that constrains model updates to stay close to the original model, and this paper adapts it for targeted repair.

## [μ-Bench: A New Benchmark for Multi-User Voice Agents in Shared Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34920) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced μ-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating voice agents on joint task completion in closed-loop, full-duplex multi-user interactions. It contains 800 cases across 20 domains, including 408 tool-augmented and 392 tool-free scenarios, with metrics for Joint Task Success (JTS), Pure JTS, and Attribution, Threading, and Coordination (ATC) diagnostics. Existing voice agent evaluations rarely test interdependent multi-user requests with privacy and authority constraints, so μ-Bench fills a gap for shared-space deployments like smart homes and meeting rooms. Its results show proprietary systems peaking around 62–65% success while open-weight models stay below 20%, highlighting a large capability gap that researchers and developers will need to address. The benchmark simulates participants with isolated profiles of goals, knowledge, and permissions who react to the agent's speech and tool outcomes, and Pure JTS further disallows unauthorized tool calls. ATC diagnostics localize failures to multi-turn attribution and threading, and controlled contrasts show that larger groups consistently degrade joint task success across capable systems.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Voice agents are increasingly deployed in shared spaces where multiple people may speak to the same assistant, each with different goals and privacy boundaries. Full-duplex interaction means the agent can listen and speak simultaneously, and closed-loop evaluation means simulated users react dynamically throughout a session rather than following a fixed script. Joint task success requires satisfying all users' goals, privacy constraints, and terminal states at once, which is harder than single-user task completion.

## [DART Mines Diverse Alternative Reasoning Traces to Boost Hard Math Problem Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34928) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose Diverse Alternative Reasoning Traces (DART), a trace-mining method that starts from rejected LLM traces, decomposes them into reasoning steps, ranks steps by position-weighted uncertainty, and generates deliberately distinct alternative candidates for highly ranked steps using reference-answer guidance and a meta-thought. On greedy-unsolved DeepMath-103K problems, DART recovers 37% more problems than Monte Carlo sampling at a matched number of generated traces, with 30% lower cost per accepted trace. This method improves the efficiency of generating post-training data for mathematical reasoning, especially for hard problems where accepted traces are rare under independent sampling. It could reduce the cost of creating high-quality training data for LLM post-training and expand problem coverage, benefiting researchers and practitioners working on mathematical reasoning. DART's estimated generation cost per recovered problem is nearly identical to Monte Carlo sampling, but its cost per accepted trace is 30% lower. Post-training Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on DART-mined traces achieves the highest point estimate in five of six evaluations across MATH-500 and AIME benchmarks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Post-training large language models for mathematical reasoning often uses accepted traces whose final answers pass a verifier. For hard math problems, such traces can be rare under independent Monte Carlo sampling, where multiple reasoning paths are generated independently. DeepMath-103K is a large-scale, rigorously decontaminated mathematical dataset designed for reinforcement learning and high-level reasoning. DART addresses the rarity of accepted traces by revisiting rejected traces and generating diverse alternatives.

## [Attack Policy Selection Can Mislead AI Monitor Evaluations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34934) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.34934) evaluates an ex-production OpenAI monitor and finds that its measured false negative rate varies from 0.5% to 20.8% across optimization levels of a single reinforcement learning attack method, and from 6.4% to 23.0% across different attack methods at their best optimization level. This finding suggests that monitor evasion evaluations using only basic attack methods can give false confidence about a monitor's strength, which could lead to unsafe deployment of AI monitors and affect best practices in AI safety evaluation. All attack policies were developed against proxy monitors only, and the reported results show attacks transferring to a held-out GPT-5.4 target monitor; the authors note that attack transcripts can contain visible artefacts, such as reasoning about a 'side task' absent from the monitor's instructions, that affect monitor judgments.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: AI monitors are classifiers that flag dangerous behavior by AI agents, and they are typically evaluated against transcripts generated by attack policies that serve as proxies for dangerous behavior in deployment. The false negative rate is the proportion of dangerous cases that the monitor incorrectly fails to flag, so a higher rate indicates a weaker monitor. Reinforcement learning is a method for training policies by optimizing rewards, and here it is used to generate attack policies that try to evade the monitor.

## [LLMs Show Stable Moral Framework Preferences Across Irrelevant Variations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.10297) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2509.10297v2) empirically assesses LLMs' moral competence by testing the stability of their preferences for five normative ethical frameworks across 16 model configurations and 18 dilemmas in six thematic domains. The study finds a consistent ordering—libertarianism ranked last in all 16 configurations, while care ethics and virtue ethics occupied the top two positions in 15—with high reliability across repeated runs and robustness to reporting format and thematic domain, though longer prompts reduced the scores of libertarian, virtue ethics, and care ethics outcomes. This work shifts the evaluation focus from moral performance (whether outputs are morally appropriate) to moral competence (the capacity to judge based on morally relevant considerations), offering a novel methodology for AI safety and alignment research. The findings suggest that LLMs may possess a stable moral framework preference, which has implications for trusting LLM judgments in ethically sensitive applications. The study tested 16 LLM configurations (reasoning and non-reasoning) on 18 dilemmas, measuring the relative ordering of five ethical frameworks rather than verdicts, and found that prompt length significantly affects scores: longer prompts lower libertarian, virtue ethics, and care ethics scores but not utilitarian and deontological ones, making care ethics, utilitarianism, and virtue ethics indistinguishable under long prompts. Reasoning configurations showed closer agreement between reporting formats but less reliability in rankings, and the authors note that since human moral judgment also shows sensitivity to morally irrelevant features, perfect stability cannot be the criterion for competence.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Normative ethical frameworks are philosophical theories that guide moral decision-making, such as utilitarianism (focusing on consequences), deontology (focusing on duties and rules), virtue ethics (focusing on character traits), care ethics (focusing on relationships and empathy), and libertarianism (focusing on individual liberty and property rights). Evaluating LLMs' moral competence is challenging because moral dilemmas rarely have a single correct answer, so researchers often assess the consistency of models' judgments rather than their correctness. Previous evaluations have largely measured moral performance—whether outputs align with human moral norms—rather than the underlying capacity to reason morally.

## [Semantic Gravity Wells: Why Negative Constraints Backfire in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.08070) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2601.08070v2) presents a mechanistic investigation of why negative constraints like 'do not use word X' fail in Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, introducing 'semantic pressure' as a measure of the model's intrinsic probability of generating the forbidden word. Using 40,000 samples from 2,500 prompts, the study shows violation probability rises from 5% at low pressure to 44% at high pressure, with a logistic relationship (p = σ(-2.95 + 2.76 P₀)). This work provides a quantitative framework for understanding a common failure mode in instruction-following, which could inform better prompt engineering and alignment strategies for LLMs. It matters for researchers and practitioners working on LLM reliability, as negative constraints are widely used but poorly understood. The study locates the divergence in layers 22-27, where feed-forward networks push toward the forbidden word more strongly in failures and attention pushes against it less; activation patching confirms these late layers carry the instruction's effect. An attention-based 'priming' signature does not distinguish failures from successes (90% vs. 93%), but a probability-based one does (instruction raises target probability in 27% of failures vs. 13% of successes).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Large language models (LLMs) like Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct are trained to follow instructions, but negative constraints (e.g., 'do not mention X') often fail. Mechanistic interpretability aims to reverse-engineer the internal computations of such models, and this paper applies those techniques to understand why suppression instructions sometimes backfire. 'Semantic pressure' is defined as the model's baseline probability of generating the forbidden word without any constraint, capturing how strongly the model 'wants' to produce it.

## [HLA: Hadamard Linear Attention with Higher-Degree Softmax Approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.12128) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2602.12128v2) introduces Hadamard Linear Attention (HLA), which applies nonlinearity after computing pairwise similarities between queries and keys, unlike standard linear attention that applies kernels separately to queries and keys. This design yields a higher-degree rational function approximation of softmax while maintaining an efficient computational scheme similar to standard linear attention. HLA offers a more accurate approximation of softmax attention without sacrificing the linear computational complexity, potentially improving efficient transformer architectures for tasks involving very large numbers of tokens. Its demonstrated effectiveness on a large diffusion transformer for video generation suggests practical benefits for long-context and high-resolution generative models. The proposed nonlinearity amounts to a higher-degree rational function approximating softmax, and the efficient computational scheme avoids time-consuming tensor reshaping required by other approaches. The method is validated on a large diffusion transformer model for video generation, an application with very large token counts.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Standard softmax attention computes pairwise similarities between all tokens and applies a nonlinearity afterward, but its quadratic cost in sequence length is prohibitive for long sequences. Linear attention reduces this cost by applying kernel functions to queries and keys independently before the similarity computation, enabling efficient matrix operations but only approximating softmax with a low-degree rational function. HLA bridges these approaches by introducing nonlinearity after pairwise similarities while retaining linear complexity.

## [Bloom's Taxonomy Levels Are Linearly Decodable in LLM Activations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.17229) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2602.17229v3) uses linear probing on high-dimensional activation vectors from multiple LLMs to show that Bloom's Taxonomy cognitive levels—from Remember to Create—are linearly separable within the models' residual streams. Linear classifiers achieve high accuracy across all levels, and separability increases across layers, suggesting early hierarchical organization of complexity representations. This work bridges cognitive theory and mechanistic interpretability, offering a novel framework for evaluating LLMs beyond surface-level performance and potentially enabling finer-grained control and diagnosis of model reasoning. It could influence how researchers design interpretability probes and assess cognitive capabilities in AI systems. The study analyzes residual stream activations from different LLMs and finds that linear classifiers can decode Bloom levels with high accuracy, with representations becoming increasingly separable across layers. However, the abstract does not specify model sizes, datasets, or exact accuracy numbers, and linear separability does not guarantee causal use of these features by the model.

**Background**: Mechanistic interpretability aims to reverse-engineer the internal computations of neural networks, often by training simple probes on internal activations. Linear probing tests whether a concept is encoded in a linearly accessible subspace by fitting a linear classifier to predict that concept from activation vectors. Bloom's Taxonomy is a hierarchical framework from education that classifies cognitive skills into six levels of increasing complexity: Remember, Understand, Apply, Analyze, Evaluate, and Create.

## [Search-E1: Self-Distillation Drives Self-Evolution in Search-Augmented Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22511) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Search-E1 introduces a self-evolution framework that alternates vanilla GRPO with on-policy self-distillation (OPSD), using the search skeletons of successful sibling trajectories as privileged context to improve search-augmented reasoning agents without external teachers. On seven single-hop and multi-hop QA benchmarks, it achieves average Exact Match scores of 0.440 with Qwen2.5-3B and 0.482 with Qwen2.5-7B, the highest seven-benchmark averages in the comparison. This work shows that search-augmented reasoning agents can iteratively improve by learning from their own successful rollouts, eliminating the need for stronger external teachers, auxiliary reward models, or complex reward shaping. It offers a simple and effective path to self-evolution that could be widely adopted in training LLM agents for retrieval-intensive tasks. After each GRPO round, Search-E1 mines sibling trajectories generated by the policy itself and exposes the search skeleton (the sequence of queries) of an efficient successful trajectory as privileged context. A token-level forward KL objective then aligns the policy under its standard inference context with its own distribution under this privileged context, converting contrasts among rollouts into dense token-level supervision without additional annotation beyond reference answers.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is a reinforcement learning algorithm for fine-tuning large language models that simplifies PPO by removing the critic and using group-relative advantages. On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) is a training strategy where a single model acts as both teacher and student with different contexts, using privileged information to guide its own learning. Search-augmented reasoning agents interleave multi-step reasoning with external information retrieval to answer complex questions.

## [PerMemBench: First Benchmark for Personalized Memory in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25535) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces PerMemBench, the first benchmark that jointly models multi-domain interaction histories and cross-user heterogeneity to evaluate personalized memory policies in LLM-based agents. It also presents the first empirical study of memory personalization, proposing session-level storage gating that selectively bypasses memory operations for transient sessions. Current memory systems use universal, static policies that waste limited memory budget on transient interactions and fail to preserve critical context for long-horizon tasks. Personalized memory policies could significantly improve retention and efficiency for agent applications that serve diverse users over extended periods. The study finds that personalization yields substantial retention gains under perfect gating, but accurate gating remains an open and critical challenge. The benchmark and source code are available at https://github.com/yeonjun-in/PerMemBench.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: LLM-based agents often rely on memory systems to store and retrieve past interactions, enabling them to handle long-horizon tasks that span many steps. However, most existing systems apply the same memory policy to all users, ignoring that different users have different contexts worth remembering. This paper addresses that gap by creating a benchmark and studying how memory policies can be personalized per user.

## [FAX verification method and CRAFTER-XAI-Bench improve agentic XAI faithfulness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27879) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Faithful Agentic XAI (FAX), a verification method that decomposes draft explanations into claims and tests them with inherently faithful tools that query the target model directly, filtering unsupported or contradicted claims before final generation. It also presents CRAFTER-XAI-Bench, an open-world reinforcement learning benchmark for assessing model-specific faithfulness, where FAX improves simulation faithfulness from 0.20 to 0.46 over the strongest baseline while maintaining high informativeness, relevance, and fluency. This work addresses a critical risk in LLM-driven explainable AI: plausible but unfaithful explanations that can mislead users about complex model behavior. By providing an explicit verification method and a benchmark designed to test explanations against the target model itself, it pushes the XAI community toward more reliable and trustworthy agentic explanation systems. On three tabular benchmarks, FAX performs competitively with prior Agentic XAI baselines, but the authors' analysis shows that these settings can conflate task accuracy with model-specific faithfulness. This suggests that existing evaluation setups may overstate explanation quality and that faithfulness benchmarks must be designed to probe the target model's actual behavior.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Explainable AI (XAI) aims to help users interpret model behavior and identify faults, and agentic XAI systems use Large Language Models (LLMs) to make explanations more accessible through natural-language interaction. However, LLMs can amplify unreliable XAI outputs for complex models, producing explanations that sound plausible but are not faithful to the model's actual reasoning. Faithfulness here means the explanation accurately reflects the target model's behavior, and reinforcement learning benchmarks provide controlled environments to evaluate such properties.

## [HiViG: A History-Aware, Visually Grounded Critic for GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11078) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced HiViG, a test-time framework built around a multimodal critic trained on real GUI trajectories that abstracts past interactions into a compact macro-action history and verifies proposed actions via visual grounding against the current screenshot. Across web, mobile, and desktop benchmarks, HiViG improved average success rates over the strongest baseline by 5.8% for Qwen3-VL-32B and 9.0% for Gemini-3-Flash. Existing critic models for Computer Use Agents are short-sighted and lack visual grounding, so HiViG's dual mechanisms directly address these gaps and could make long-horizon GUI automation more reliable. The consistent gains across platforms suggest the approach generalizes, which is valuable for the agent and GUI research community. Ablations confirm that macro-action history mitigates short-sighted planning and visually grounded critique reduces execution errors, with both components being critical for test-time scaling in long-horizon GUI tasks. The critic intercepts errors before execution by verifying raw execution coordinates against the current screenshot.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Computer Use Agents (CUAs) operate graphical user interfaces by taking screenshots, sending them to a multimodal model, and receiving actions expressed as screen coordinates such as clicks or typing. Test-time interventions, including critic models, evaluate proposed actions before execution to catch mistakes. Visual grounding refers to a model's ability to relate language to specific visual entities in an image, which is essential for detecting flawed actions like clicking the wrong UI element.

## [State-bound model ensures authorization continuity for evolving AI agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23586) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper formulates the problem of authorization continuity for long-lived, evolving AI agents and proposes a state-bound model that fixes a transition envelope and an immutable effect ceiling at grant time. It proves that under complete mediation, sound effect abstraction, attenuating delegation, and monitor integrity, agent mutations cannot amplify protected effects beyond the user-issued ceiling. This work addresses a critical gap in AI agent security: as agents evolve after deployment, existing grants may no longer match the user's original intent, potentially enabling harmful actions. The proposed framework could influence future standards for agent governance and access control, impacting developers and organizations deploying long-lived autonomous agents. The model distinguishes requested from realized effects and maps six mutation classes to their authorization consequences. Authority may contract freely below the ceiling but can expand only under specified evidence conditions, and agent-produced evidence cannot raise the ceiling.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Long-lived AI agents can retain experience, acquire new skills, and delegate work, which improves adaptation but complicates authorization. Tool-enabled agents can turn model errors and prompt injections into real-world actions, and existing tool policies constrain actions but do not determine when a grant survives evolution. This paper introduces authorization continuity to address when an existing grant remains valid and how authority may change.

## [Fisher-R1: Training LLM Agents for Reliable Hypothesis Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07437) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces P-Bench, a benchmark of 425 open-ended, realistic hypothesis-testing tasks across economics, biology, and medicine, and Fisher-R1, an open-weight LLM agent trained with synthetic tasks and reinforcement learning. Fisher-R1-14B outperforms strong proprietary and open-source baselines, including GPT-5.4 and DeepSeek-V4-Pro, achieving a 21% average relative improvement in single-trial success over DeepSeek-V4-Pro, with gains up to 26% on the most challenging tasks. This work highlights that current LLM agents often make subtle inferential errors in hypothesis testing, leading to incorrect conclusions despite correctly executed analyses, and shows that reinforcement learning with verified statistical rewards can substantially improve reliability. It is relevant to AI/ML and scientific automation, as reliable statistical reasoning is foundational for empirical scientific claims. P-Bench tasks require an agent to select a statistical method, compute a p-value, and draw a conclusion given only a scientific hypothesis and a dataset, and existing benchmarks rarely assess whether a reported p-value is statistically valid given the assumptions underlying the data. Fisher-R1 is trained on synthetic tasks with reinforcement learning using verified statistical reward, and the 14B model demonstrates strong gains, though the paper is an arXiv preprint without community discussion provided.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Hypothesis testing is a core statistical method used to evaluate scientific claims, where a p-value quantifies the evidence against a null hypothesis under specific assumptions. Large language model agents are increasingly used to automate data analysis by inspecting datasets, generating code, and producing end-to-end analyses, but they can make inferential errors that invalidate conclusions. Reinforcement learning is a training paradigm where a model learns from reward signals, and here it is used with verified statistical rewards to teach an agent rigorous hypothesis testing.
