## [iBrain: A Unified Foundation Model for Invasive Neural Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06960) ⭐️ 7.0/10

iBrain is a unified foundation model that jointly pretrains on intracranial EEG (iEEG) and intracortical spiking recordings using signal-specific encoders and a shared spatiotemporal Transformer backbone. It was pretrained on over 7,000 hours of heterogeneous neural recordings with masked signal reconstruction and channel-view alignment, and it outperforms single-signal pretraining baselines and achieves state-of-the-art results on multiple benchmarks. Existing neural foundation models have largely been built separately for each invasive recording paradigm, so iBrain shows that joint pretraining across heterogeneous invasive signals can yield transferable and data-efficient representations. This could benefit brain-computer interface research and neuro-AI modeling by enabling models that generalize across recording settings and downstream tasks. The model uses signal-specific encoders to accommodate the distinct characteristics of iEEG and spikes, while the shared spatiotemporal Transformer models dependencies across recording channels and time. Experiments further show transferability and data efficiency across diverse recording settings, though the work is an arXiv preprint and its benchmarks and generalization claims await peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Intracranial EEG (iEEG) records brain activity directly from the surface of the brain or deep within brain structures with millisecond precision and millimeter spatial specificity, while intracortical spiking recordings capture the activity of individual neurons at even finer temporal resolution. These invasive modalities provide high-fidelity measurements at different spatial and temporal scales, but they differ substantially in signal characteristics. Foundation models are large neural networks pretrained on broad data with self-supervised objectives such as masked reconstruction, then adapted to downstream tasks; applying this paradigm across heterogeneous neural signals is still underexplored.

## [SpecRead Benchmark Tests Whether LLMs Truly Understand Hardware Specs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33699) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SpecRead v2.2 is a new benchmark of 383 questions over 10 open-source OpenTitan IP blocks, designed to isolate specification comprehension from RTL generation. It includes four question types—exact retrieval, cross-section reasoning, contradiction detection in mutated specs, and spec-RTL consistency checking—plus 41 distractor and 41 consistent-RTL controls. Existing hardware LLM benchmarks evaluate downstream artifacts like generated RTL, so failures are ambiguous between misreading the spec and failing to code. SpecRead provides a cleaner diagnostic for the EDA/LLM-for-hardware community, showing that even a capable small model struggles with fine-grained spec comprehension. Ministral-3B scores only 33.7% overall (129/383); on contradiction detection it reaches 48.0% verdict-plus-location accuracy with a 51.2% distractor false-positive rate, and on spec-RTL consistency its violation-vs-compliant decision is degenerate (MCC -0.06, balanced accuracy 49.5%) while flagging all 41 consistent controls. A with-spec vs. without-spec ablation suggests the excerpt is needed, though memorization of unmutated source text cannot be ruled out, and a rule-table prompting intervention lowered accuracy on every type except t2.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: OpenTitan is an open-source silicon root of trust project providing hardware IP blocks such as security controllers and cryptographic accelerators. RTL (register-transfer level) is the abstraction used to describe synchronous digital circuits in hardware description languages like Verilog, and Icarus Verilog is an open-source simulator used to verify that RTL behaves as intended. SpecRead builds on these public artifacts to test whether a language model can read a hardware specification and reason about it, rather than merely producing plausible-looking code.

## [CompMat-Bench: A 94-Task Benchmark for AI Agents in Computational Materials Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00636) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced CompMat-Bench, a benchmark of 94 tasks derived from recently published computational materials studies, where agents prepare inputs and analyze outputs for expensive simulations that were reproduced in advance. Agents are graded with fixed rules against pre-reproduced ground truth rather than an LLM judge, and the benchmark spans four conditions: single tasks and workflows, each with full or reduced methodological guidance. Evaluating AI agents on real scientific research is often bottlenecked by the cost of repeating experiments or simulations, so pre-reproducing results and grading with fixed rules offers a cheaper, more reproducible evaluation paradigm. This matters for the scientific-agent community because it enables systematic comparison of agents on authentic materials-research steps and supports failure-mode analysis. With full guidance on single tasks, agents based on nine LLMs achieved pass rates of 62.8–97.9% across the 94 tasks; longer workflows and reduced guidance lowered pass rates for weaker agents, while stronger agents only declined when a long workflow was combined with reduced guidance. Failure analysis attributed more failures to scientific errors than to software-usage errors.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Computational materials science relies on expensive simulations, such as first-principles calculations, to predict material properties, and running these repeatedly for each agent trial makes evaluation impractical. AI agents are LLM-driven systems that can call tools and execute multi-step tasks, and benchmarks are standardized test suites used to compare their capabilities. Many agent benchmarks use an LLM as a judge to score outputs, but this approach can be costly and inconsistent, motivating rule-based grading against precomputed ground truth.

## [FluidPD adds in-place elasticity to prefill-decode disaggregated LLM serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06917) ⭐️ 7.0/10

FluidPD introduces SLO-aware in-place elasticity for prefill-decode disaggregated LLM serving through two mechanisms: FluidToken, which offloads a bounded portion of prefill computation to idle decode workers to absorb transient imbalance, and FluidRole, which switches running workers between prefill and decode roles in place without model reload or engine restart to handle sustained imbalance. On workloads derived from Azure production traces and ShareGPT conversations, FluidPD improves overall SLO attainment by up to 47.9 percentage points over the best static P/D split for each load, without additional workers. Fixed prefill/decode worker ratios in disaggregated serving can violate latency SLOs during demand shifts while capacity sits idle in the other pool, and autoscaling reacts too slowly and requires spare GPUs. FluidPD's in-place elasticity addresses both short bursts and sustained shifts without extra hardware, potentially improving cost efficiency and tail latency for production LLM serving systems. FluidPD is guided by indices that expose pressure in each phase before it causes SLO violations, and FluidToken offloads only a bounded portion of prefill computation to decode workers with slack. The evaluation uses workloads derived from production Azure traces and ShareGPT conversations with sustained shifts in the prefill-to-decode demand ratio, but the paper is an arXiv preprint without community discussion provided.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Prefill-decode disaggregation runs the two phases of LLM inference on separate workers: prefill processes the entire prompt and builds the KV cache (compute-heavy), while decode generates tokens one at a time (memory-bandwidth-heavy). This separation prevents long prefills from stalling token generation, but typical deployments use a fixed prefill/decode worker ratio. Real workloads exhibit both short bursts and sustained shifts in the prefill-to-decode demand ratio, so a static split can violate latency SLOs (service-level objectives) while capacity sits idle in the other pool.

## [SearchWorld: Spatial Value-Grounded Imagination for UAV Object Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09335) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SearchWorld introduces a recurrent state-space world model that combines explicit bird's-eye-view (BEV) spatial memory with value-guided imagination to enable prospective planning for UAV object search. On the UAV-ON benchmark, it achieves a 23.8% success rate, improving over the strongest published agent's 19.5%, and raises oracle success to 35.5% while maintaining 19.9% success on unseen scenes. This work addresses key challenges in autonomous UAV search under partial observability, such as limited coverage from narrow views and safe motion in dense 3D environments, by enabling prospective planning without the high inference latency of image-generating world models. It could advance real-world drone applications in urban search and rescue, inspection, and delivery. The model maintains BEV exploration and obstacle memory, decodes a task-aware spatial value layer, and uses a cognition-action network that leverages this learned spatial value prior to improve the policy through imagined rollouts, eliminating the need for a separate scalar critic. Training proceeds in stages: world-model learning, expert imitation, and imagination-based exploration refinement.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: World models are internal representations that let agents simulate future states to plan ahead, and they have been popularized by model-based reinforcement learning methods like Dreamer. Recurrent state-space models are a class of sequence models that maintain a compact hidden state over time, offering an alternative to transformers for long-horizon reasoning. UAV object search requires the drone to find a specific target in a large, cluttered area using only its onboard camera, which is challenging because the drone sees only a narrow view and must decide where to fly next.

## [Decision Model Jev Diagnoses HVAC Faults with Physical Features, Robust Under Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09937) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper evaluates the pretrained decision model Jev on 128 fault days from four public HVAC datasets, testing which faults it can diagnose, what input representation it needs, and whether its probabilities remain reliable under distribution shift. Given physical features, Jev and a larger open language model diagnosed faults whose evidence is carried by a single feature, but failed on faults requiring operating context; under shift they maintained accuracy and calibration while a supervised model lost 0.33 macro-F1. This work maps the boundaries of pretrained decision models for building operations, suggesting a division of labor where code computes the physics and the model ranks candidate faults for an operator. It offers a path to reduce reliance on expert rules and labeled data, which are major barriers to AI adoption in HVAC fault diagnosis. The study used nine tests that varied season, control configuration, or building, and found that while Jev and the larger open model kept their accuracy and calibration under shift, their probabilities still needed correction and fault detection was weak. The supervised model, despite losing 0.33 macro-F1 under shift, still led or tied within a building, highlighting the importance of context-specific models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: HVAC systems are complex building infrastructure, and diagnosing their faults typically relies on expert rules tuned per system or supervised models requiring labeled data that buildings rarely record. Decision models are a newer class of pretrained models that return a probability for each allowed answer, potentially serving many decisions without training. Brick is an ontology for building metadata that provides a standardized topology of building systems.

## [RAN-JEPA: Action-Conditioned World Model for Energy-Efficient 5G RAN Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17584) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce RAN-JEPA, an action-conditioned world model that separates representation learning from intervention learning to overcome the scarcity of action-response data in mobile networks. Pretrained on public operator data and adapted to a real four-cell 5G testbed, it achieves lower action-conditioned prediction error than a GPT-2 world-model baseline and a mechanistic rule, and supports higher sleep ratios in closed-loop control at comparable congestion cost. This work addresses the observation-intervention asymmetry that hinders practical world models for RAN control, showing that abundant passive telemetry can be leveraged to learn effective control policies with limited intervention data. It could enable more energy-efficient 5G networks by allowing operators to sleep cells more aggressively without sacrificing user experience. RAN-JEPA first learns a predictive latent representation from observation-only data, adapts it to the target domain, then learns action-conditioned latent dynamics from controlled sleep/wake trajectories while keeping the encoder fixed. Evaluated over four future 15-minute intervals, it remains close to a strong persistence reference and produces distinct future trajectories under matched SLEEP and KEEP plans.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: JEPA (Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture), proposed by Yann LeCun, is a framework for learning world models by predicting in a latent representation space rather than generating raw observations. A RAN (Radio Access Network) is the part of a mobile network that connects user devices to the core network; controlling it energy-efficiently often involves putting cells to sleep during low traffic. World models aim to predict future states of an environment under different actions, enabling planning and control.

## [OwlSight: A Panoramic Monitor for Chain-of-Thought Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23670) ⭐️ 7.0/10

OwlSight introduces a factor-based Chain-of-Thought (CoT) monitor that uses a single prompt-driven model to oversee reasoning across multiple dimensions, including capability, safety, and trustworthiness. Experiments on 15,971 reasoning trajectories show it outperforms general-purpose LLMs by 1.32–30.57 points at fine granularity and 11.71–27.81 points at coarse granularity, achieving state-of-the-art results on OpenAI's CoT monitorability benchmark. This work addresses a key limitation of existing CoT monitors, which are typically task-specific, by enabling a single customizable monitor to cover diverse oversight needs. It could significantly improve AI safety and oversight for frontier reasoning models, allowing early detection of misbehavior across a broad range of tasks. OwlSight supports both fine-grained and coarse-grained factors (e.g., monitoring a specific email address leak versus general privacy leakage) and generalizes to unseen models' CoTs while remaining robust to factor rephrasings, CoT compression, and obfuscation. The approach relies solely on factors specified in the prompt, eliminating the need for task-specific training.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) monitoring involves analyzing the intermediate reasoning steps of AI models to detect unsafe or undesirable behavior, and prior work has shown that LLMs can effectively monitor other LLMs' reasoning. However, most existing monitors are designed for specific tasks, limiting their applicability to broader AI regulation. OwlSight introduces a factor-based strategy that allows users to customize monitoring requirements via prompts, enabling a single monitor to handle multiple tasks and dimensions.

## [Kairos: A Single PDDL Planner with Formal Guarantees for Robot Task Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39454) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Kairos is a new symbolic task planner that covers the entire PDDL language, including durative actions, numeric resources, time windows, and continuous change, and provides formal guarantees on plan existence and unsolvability. It is open source, packaged for ROS, and serves as the default planner in a widely used planning framework. This work addresses a key challenge in AI planning: most existing planners handle only fragments of PDDL and give ambiguous failure feedback. By providing a single planner with proven guarantees, Kairos could enable more reliable and faster on-line task planning for autonomous robots, impacting robotics and AI planning research. Kairos proves unsolvability via search exhaustion and a temporal relaxation, and its compression of durative actions is sound but not complete, so compression failure is never reported as a proof. On 1,756 public tasks from nine suites, it solves more tasks than the strongest evaluated planner of each suite within 100 ms, 1 s, and 30 s, proves ten of eleven unsolvable tasks, and handles all seventeen probed language features, whereas no other planner handles more than eight.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: PDDL (Planning Domain Definition Language) is a standardized language for AI planning that has evolved from classical planning to include temporal and numeric features. Traditional planners often support only subsets of PDDL and differ in how they report failure, making it hard to know whether no plan exists or the planner simply gave up. Kairos aims to unify planning across the full language with clear semantics for each answer.

The paper introduces AceAction, a multi-game 3D gameplay dataset with hierarchical task, subgoal, and skill annotations, built via a vision-language model (VLM) annotation pipeline. The dataset contains temporally aligned vision-language-action data across navigation, reconnaissance, combat, interaction, and recovery, and an audit of 500 trajectories yields a 95.8% pass rate. This dataset addresses key limitations in existing vision-language-action (VLA) datasets, such as restricted game coverage and sparse language supervision, by providing explicit stage boundaries that link task progression to fine-grained control. The VLM-based pipeline identifies subgoals from changes in objectives or interaction targets, anchors navigation goals to visually recognizable waypoints, and composes adjacent subgoals into task instructions. Annotation quality is verified through model-based checks and human inspection, though the dataset is an incremental contribution rather than a paradigm shift.

Video games offer a cost-effective alternative to physical environments for collecting such data, but existing datasets often lack hierarchical annotations that reflect changes in task objectives. AceAction fills this gap by organizing demonstrations around complete tasks with ordered subgoals and local skill instructions.

## [ConceptARC at Scale: Generator Creates Thousands of ARC Tasks to Probe Abstraction in Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06732) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces ConceptARC at Scale, a generator that produces thousands of ARC-style tasks targeting specific concepts, and uses it to build ConceptARC-100, a dataset of 1,600 manually reviewed tasks (100 per each of 16 concepts), ten times the size of the original ConceptARC benchmark. The authors evaluate recent large reasoning models and models trained from scratch on this dataset, and analyze internal representations to show that task representations cluster by transformation operations such as recoloring, removal, or translation. This work addresses a key gap in ARC-style benchmarks, which typically contain only a few manually constructed tasks per concept, by providing a scalable way to test whether large reasoning models form generalizable conceptual abstractions rather than memorizing individual tasks. The finding that models develop stable internal abstractions of transformation operations has implications for evaluating and improving reasoning models' generalization. The dataset covers 16 concepts defined by ConceptARC, with 100 tasks per concept, all manually reviewed; the authors also probe models' internal representations and find semantic clustering by operation type even when the underlying rules and scenarios differ substantially. As an arXiv preprint, the work has not yet undergone peer review and no community discussion is available.

**Background**: The Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus (ARC), introduced by François Chollet in 2019, is a benchmark where solvers must infer transformation rules over 2D grids from a few examples, testing out-of-distribution generalization and abstraction. ConceptARC extends ARC by grouping tasks into recurring spatial and semantic concepts, but it contains only a handful of manually constructed tasks per concept, limiting statistical power. Large reasoning models (LRMs) are LLM-based systems optimized for multi-step reasoning, and it remains unclear whether their strong benchmark performance reflects generalizable abstractions.

A new arXiv study analyzed text and images from campaign websites, X accounts, and emails of 12,977 U.S. candidates from 2023 to 2025, finding that detectable AI-generated content increased significantly across modalities and was often not disclosed. ## [RSI-PDEbench: A Benchmark for Self-Improving LLM Agents in PDE Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14949) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced RSI-PDEbench, a benchmark of 1,001 partial differential equation (PDE) cases spanning 42 equation templates, along with RSI-PDE, a recursively self-improving agent harness. The benchmark includes three evaluation settings (Full, Noisy, Hard-OOD) and tests four frontier LLMs, showing that RSI-PDE improves pass rates by 106.5–181.9% over single-run baselines and reduces model calls by 46.9%. This work addresses the challenging problem of automating PDE discovery and solving from data, which traditionally requires substantial expert effort. By providing a rigorous benchmark and demonstrating the effectiveness of validated recursive self-improvement, it could accelerate AI-driven scientific discovery and enable more efficient numerical solver configuration. The benchmark includes both classical and fractional PDEs, with the Noisy setting adding Gaussian noise at 50% of the clean-signal RMS, and the Hard-OOD setting testing generalization to unseen equation templates and shifted coefficient ranges. RSI-PDE uses residual loss to shortlist candidate equations, relative L2 feedback to refine coefficients and solvers, and a validation gate for policy and memory updates.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Partial differential equations (PDEs) are fundamental in modeling physical phenomena, and discovering them from data typically involves equation discovery, coefficient estimation, and numerical solver configuration. Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly used to automate complex workflows, and an agent harness is the software infrastructure that manages tool execution, memory, and verification for such agents. Recursive self-improvement refers to an agent's ability to iteratively refine its own policies and memory based on feedback.

## [AutoE2E: Open-Source End-to-End Driving Model for ADAS and Robotaxis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22374) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The Autoware Foundation's Robotaxi Working Group released AutoE2E v1.0, an open-source end-to-end driving model that uses a temporal bird's-eye-view (BEV) architecture combining multi-camera perception, HD-map and route conditioning, and a lightweight recurrent planner. The release includes model checkpoints, training and evaluation code, and a visualization dashboard called DataModelConsole, with the fine-tuned checkpoint achieving an average displacement error (ADE) of 0.745 m and final displacement error (FDE) of 2.020 m at a three-second horizon on the KITScenes validation benchmark. This release provides the autonomous-driving community with an inspectable, open baseline for end-to-end driving research, which is valuable because most comparable models remain proprietary. It lowers the barrier for academic and industry teams to experiment with BEV-based end-to-end architectures and could accelerate collaborative development toward L2++ ADAS and L4 robotaxi systems. The evaluated model has approximately 79.9 million parameters, of which only about 1.45 million are trainable in the evaluated configuration, and its quantitative evaluation is open-loop only, meaning it has not yet demonstrated validated closed-loop autonomous driving. The paper also reports preliminary real-vehicle research with the Technical University of Munich's EDGAR platform and ongoing collaboration with the Karlsruhe Institute of Technology.

**Background**: End-to-end driving models aim to map raw sensor inputs directly to vehicle control or trajectory outputs, replacing hand-engineered perception-planning pipelines. L2++ ADAS refers to advanced driver-assistance systems with near-autonomous capabilities that still require human supervision, while L4 robotaxis denote high-automation vehicles operating without a human driver within defined domains.

## [PlayScriptBench tests if VLMs can synthesize executable game-playing programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27741) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces PlayScriptBench, a script-based game benchmark that evaluates whether vision-language models (VLMs) can synthesize an executable game-playing program from game states instead of relying on step-by-step VLM calls. It also proposes a four-stage pipeline (Explore, Perception, API Construction, Strategy Composition) to decompose controller synthesis and make evaluation tractable for current general-purpose VLMs. This benchmark shifts VLM game-playing evaluation from step-by-step interaction to program synthesis, addressing real-time latency and cost limitations of repeated model calls. It provides a more comprehensive assessment of visual grounding, game dynamics understanding, long-horizon reasoning, and executable program synthesis, which could influence the design of future multimodal agents. Experiments on 16 games across 4 genres show that even strong VLMs struggle on PlayScriptBench, revealing a substantial gap between passive visual game understanding and executable closed-loop game control. The four-stage pipeline decomposes controller synthesis into game exploration, state perception, interface construction, and strategic control, enabling agents to progressively construct an executable program.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) are AI systems that jointly interpret and generate information from both images and text, extending large language models with visual perception. Existing game benchmarks for VLMs typically use a step-by-step interaction paradigm where the current visual observation is sent to the agent before each action, which requires waiting for model inference and incurs repeated API costs. Program synthesis is the task of automatically generating a program from a specification, such as input-output examples or visual demonstrations. PlayScriptBench combines these ideas by asking VLMs to produce an executable game-playing program from game states, rather than selecting actions one at a time.

## [Engram: Multi-Stage Memory System Cuts Agentic Tuning Trials by 47%](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28356) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present Engram, a memory system for LLM agents that links past tuning trial outcomes with the hypotheses and rationale behind them and judges whether that evidence applies to a new model. On two ads models, Engram matched the 32-trial result of a no-memory control with 47% fewer trials and ended with a better model on average. LLM agents that tune neural networks currently waste costly trials rediscovering lessons because they lack cross-session memory, and reuse is hard when production models differ in architecture, training setup and inference budget. Engram's approach of curating evidence at decision points could make agentic hyperparameter tuning and architecture search substantially more sample-efficient across heterogeneous model fleets. The system uses an online agent at three decision points—Initial Guidance (validated lessons at the start), Session Retrieval (related past sessions before each batch of hypotheses), and Trial Candidate Review (checking each candidate against matching past trials)—while an offline agent distills validated lessons between sessions so the memory self-improves. Results are reported on only two ads models from an arXiv preprint, so generalization to other domains and model families remains unverified.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: LLM agents are increasingly used to automate machine learning engineering tasks such as hyperparameter tuning and neural architecture search, but each tuning session typically starts from scratch. Memory systems for agents aim to persist knowledge across sessions, and prior work in the broader agent ecosystem has explored cross-session recall and self-improving skills. Engram applies this idea to the specific problem of transferring tuning lessons between models that differ in architecture, training setup and inference budget.

## [MOZAIC Benchmark Exposes Multimodal LLM Weakness in Audio-Video Deepfake Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31301) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers from the Vector Institute have released MOZAIC, a paired audio-video deepfake benchmark containing 137,745 clips from 15,014 source pairs, each labeled with a 4-way combination of real/fake video and real/fake audio. Evaluating 12 audio-video multimodal LLMs, they found the best model achieved only 35.6% accuracy on the four-way authenticity task, while trained detectors reached 98.4%. This benchmark reveals a critical gap: leading multimodal LLMs are far worse at detecting audio-video deepfakes than specialized detectors, and they are especially poor at catching voice-only fakes (7.5% vs 43.6% for video-only). These findings have direct implications for AI security, content moderation, and the development of more robust multimodal models. The benchmark includes four tasks: AV authenticity (T1), manipulation type (T2), identity verification (T3), and explanation (T4). While LoRA fine-tuning improved explanation scores (e.g., OmniVinci from 4.17 to 7.40), the best T1 detector dropped to 78.3% when the face-swap generator was held out of training, highlighting generalization challenges.

**Background**: Audio-video deepfakes can manipulate the face, the voice, or both, but most existing benchmarks only ask whether a clip is real or fake, failing to isolate which modality is being manipulated. MOZAIC addresses this by releasing every source clip in all four combinations of real/fake video and audio, enabling direct measurement of modality contributions. Multimodal LLMs are AI systems that process multiple data types (text, audio, images, video) simultaneously, and LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique that freezes base model weights and trains small low-rank matrices.

## [EffectWAM: World-Action Model Grounds Visual Prediction in Robot Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32053) ⭐️ 7.0/10

EffectWAM introduces a world-action model that connects visual change prediction with executable robot control through three components: Effect (visual change as difference between future and current observation encodings), camera positional encoding (CaPE) for pose-conditioned pooling, and Action Grounding that trains the visual representation to retain end-effector pose and gripper state changes. A shared generation expert jointly predicts Effect and action chunks via flow matching, enabling action execution without waiting for video synthesis. This work addresses a key challenge in embodied AI: making learned visual world models directly useful for control rather than just video prediction. By grounding visual change prediction in executable actions and decoupling action execution from video synthesis, it could enable more responsive and data-efficient robot manipulation policies, benefiting robotics and embodied AI researchers. The model is trained on over 20,000 hours of heterogeneous embodied data with dataset-specific joint, end-effector, and hand-pose representations, and evaluated using an 8B Small configuration on five benchmarks plus a 26B Large configuration on RoboDojo. CaPE shares learned parameters with pose-free pooling for uncalibrated trajectories, and an exponential moving average of the observation encoder supplies Effect targets.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: World-action models aim to combine world models—which predict future observations—with action policies that control robots. Flow matching is a generative modeling technique that learns to transform noise into data via continuous flows, often used for efficient sampling. Camera positional encoding (CaPE) conditions visual representations on camera pose, which is important because the same scene looks different from different viewpoints.

## [Contrastive Action Identification Improves Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32776) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Contrastive Identification of Actions (CIA), a method that trains latent world models by contrasting the executed action against plausible alternatives sampled from a history-conditioned behavior proposal. CIA outperforms inverse dynamics regression and the base world model on five visual control benchmarks, achieving higher planner-averaged mean success without modifying the planner. This work addresses a known limitation of inverse dynamics regression, which only rewards information that changes the conditional action mean, and shows that contrastive identification can capture additional action-relevant information. The improvement in planning performance across multiple benchmarks suggests CIA could be a valuable auxiliary objective for model-based reinforcement learning from offline visual data. Under exact behavior matching, the authors theoretically show that CIA can reward action information that leaves optimal inverse-regression error unchanged, and they verify this distinction with a controlled learning example. The method requires sampling alternatives from a history-conditioned behavior proposal and does not alter the planner architecture.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Latent world models learn compressed representations of observations and their dynamics from offline visual data, enabling goal-directed planning by predicting future states. Inverse dynamics is a common auxiliary task that predicts the action taken between two consecutive observations, but squared-error regression only encourages representations to encode information that affects the mean predicted action. Contrastive learning, which compares positive and negative samples, has been widely used in representation learning to capture richer distinctions.

## [Neur2BiLO: Neural Networks for Fast Bilevel Optimization Heuristics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.02552) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Neur2BiLO is a data-driven framework that trains neural networks to approximate leader or follower value functions and embeds them into mixed-integer programs to quickly produce high-quality heuristic solutions for constrained bilevel optimization problems. It is presented in arXiv paper 2402.02552v3 and demonstrated on four applications with linear and non-linear objectives and pure and mixed-integer variables. Constrained bilevel problems with integer variables are notoriously hard, and exact solvers scale poorly, so Neur2BiLO offers a fast heuristic that could significantly impact operations research and machine learning communities by enabling routine solving of similar instances. The neural network is trained via supervised regression to approximate the value function, and the resulting mixed-integer program is easy to solve; however, the approach is heuristic and relies on a data-driven setting where similar instances are solved routinely, and its performance may depend on the quality and representativeness of the training data.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Bilevel optimization involves nested problems where a leader optimizes their objective while anticipating a follower's best response, and it becomes especially challenging when integer variables are involved. Mixed-integer programming handles problems with some discrete decision variables, and solving such bilevel problems exactly is computationally intensive, motivating data-driven heuristics like Neur2BiLO.

## [Polyrating: A Cost-Effective and Bias-Aware Rating System for LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.00696) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Polyrating is a new rating system for LLM evaluation that uses maximum a posteriori estimation to detect and quantify biases in human preferences, and it reduces human evaluation costs by up to 59% for new models and 77% for new tasks by leveraging existing benchmark scores. This matters because accurate and fair LLM evaluation is crucial for model development and comparison, and Polyrating's bias-aware approach and cost reductions can make evaluations more reliable and accessible, especially for researchers with limited budgets. Polyrating is based on maximum a posteriori estimation, which allows it to incorporate prior knowledge and detect biases such as position bias; for example, position bias can give a model around 38 rating points when using an LLM-based judge, and the system enables direct comparisons of ratings across different tasks.

**Background**: Rating-based human evaluation is commonly used to assess LLM performance, but traditional systems are univariate and fail to account for biases, require large preference datasets, and do not allow cross-task comparisons. Maximum a posteriori (MAP) estimation is a Bayesian method that finds the most probable parameter values given data and prior knowledge, providing a regularized alternative to maximum likelihood. Polyrating builds on MAP to create a multivariate rating system that addresses these limitations.

## [LLMs Fail to Predict 2024 EU Election Voting Behavior, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.09045) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2409.09045v3) evaluates three LLMs by prompting them with background information on 26,000 eligible European voters to predict individual voting behavior in the 2024 European Parliament elections. The study finds that LLM-based predictions largely fail, are unequally accurate across national and linguistic contexts, and require detailed attitudinal information in the prompt. This study provides a rigorous empirical test of 'silicon samples'—LLM-generated survey data proposed as a cheap alternative to human surveys—and shows they are currently unreliable for public opinion prediction. The unequal accuracy across contexts raises concerns that using LLMs in social science and policymaking could reinforce existing biases and inequalities. The study prompted three LLMs with individual-level background data for 26,000 voters before the elections and compared predictions to actual results; accuracy was found to depend heavily on detailed attitudinal information in the prompt and varied significantly across national and linguistic contexts.

## [Neural Fidelity Calibration Uses Diffusion Models for Online Sim-to-Real Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.08604) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Neural Fidelity Calibration (NFC), a framework that employs conditional score-based diffusion models to calibrate simulator physical coefficients and residual fidelity domains online during robot execution. It fine-tunes pretrained policies only under anomalous scenarios, builds sequential NFC with a pretrained proposal prior, and uses optimistic exploration when NFC uncertainty is high. This approach addresses the sim-to-real gap without requiring expert physics knowledge for domain randomization, potentially enabling more robust robot navigation in challenging real-world conditions such as a broken wheel axle on snowy surfaces. It could simplify the deployment of reinforcement learning policies in diverse and unpredictable environments. The residual fidelity captures simulation model shift and perception uncertainty, allowing sampling of realistic environments for policy fine-tuning. The framework achieves superior calibration precision compared to state-of-the-art methods across diverse robots with high-dimensional parametric spaces, and demonstrates robust navigation under a broken wheel axle on snowy surfaces.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Sim-to-real transfer in robotics often relies on domain randomization, which randomizes simulator parameters to make policies robust, but this requires expert physics knowledge and may not capture all real-world details. Diffusion models are generative models that learn to denoise data, and conditional variants can generate samples based on given conditions, making them suitable for modeling complex distributions like residual fidelity.

## [VITA: Noise-Free Flow Matching Policy for Efficient Robotic Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.13231) ⭐️ 7.0/10

VITA introduces a noise-free and conditioning-free flow matching policy that directly maps visual representations to latent actions via an action autoencoder, eliminating iterative denoising and visual conditioning during generation. It achieves 1.9x faster inference than conventional flow matching policies while matching or outperforming state-of-the-art methods on 9 simulation and 5 real-world tasks. This work addresses the computational overhead of diffusion and flow matching policies, which require many iterative steps and repeated visual conditioning, making VITA a promising step toward faster, more efficient robotic control that could enable real-time deployment on resource-constrained robots. To bridge the dimensionality mismatch between visual representations and lower-dimensional actions, VITA introduces an action autoencoder that maps raw actions into a structured latent space aligned with visual latents, trained jointly with flow matching. It also proposes flow latent decoding, which backpropagates action reconstruction loss through the flow matching ODE solving steps to prevent latent action space collapse during end-to-end training.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Diffusion and flow matching policies are generative models that produce robot actions by iteratively denoising from random noise, often conditioned on visual observations. While effective, they require many sampling steps and repeated visual conditioning, leading to slow inference and high memory use. VITA simplifies this by starting the flow directly from visual representations, removing the need for noise and conditioning, but requires aligning action and visual dimensions via an autoencoder.

## [Mechanism-Based Intelligence Aligns Multi-Agent Systems via Differentiable Incentives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.20688) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces Mechanism-Based Intelligence (MBI), an architecture that uses a differentiable price mechanism and Groves transfers to align incentives in multi-agent systems. The approach yields an exact potential game with a unique equilibrium at the global optimum, enabling gradient-based play to converge under a step-size condition. This framework offers a principled way to coordinate specialized modules in autonomous multi-agent systems, addressing the Hayekian information problem and the Hurwiczian incentive problem. It has potential impact on AI alignment and multi-agent coordination by providing an auditable, incentive-compatible approach. Each agent's incentive signal is the negative gradient of the global loss with respect to its actions, and transfers take the Groves form. The equilibrium requires no knowledge of agents' private costs, so asymmetric information causes no welfare loss; each iteration scales linearly with the number of agents, though iteration count may grow with N for losses summed over agents.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Mechanism design, particularly the Vickrey-Clarke-Groves (VCG) mechanism, provides truthful ways to achieve socially optimal outcomes using monetary transfers. Potential games are games where a single potential function captures the incentive to change strategies, guaranteeing pure Nash equilibria. MBI combines these ideas with differentiable optimization to align self-interested agents with a global objective.

## [Neural Isomorphic Fields: Transformer-based algebraic numerical embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.12095) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Neural Isomorphic Fields (NIF), a Transformer-based digit-sequence autoencoder that learns numerical embeddings preserving algebraic properties, achieving at least 99.9% exact match on algebraic laws and improving language model performance on word problems from 12.0% to 39.7%. This approach could mitigate numerical instabilities like overflow and underflow in neural networks, enabling more reliable arithmetic reasoning in language models and benefiting applications requiring precise numerical computation. The embeddings provably form an ordered integral domain isomorphic to decimal fractions at zero loss, with error bounds derived from decoding margins and Lipschitz constants; the method scales linearly with four channels per decimal place and handles numbers up to 200 digits.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Neural networks often struggle with very small or large numbers due to overflow, underflow, and unstable outputs. Embedding numbers as vectors instead of raw values can preserve algebraic properties while avoiding these issues. An ordered integral domain is a mathematical structure with addition, multiplication, and order, isomorphic to the integers, and its field of fractions is isomorphic to the rationals. Lipschitz continuity bounds how fast a function can change, providing a way to bound errors in neural operations.

## [TopoPrune: Topology-Based Data Pruning for Robust Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02739) ⭐️ 7.0/10

TopoPrune introduces a dual-scale topology-based data pruning framework that uses manifold approximation and differentiable persistent homology to rank samples by structural complexity, improving robustness over geometric methods. This approach addresses the instability of geometric pruning methods, enabling more reliable data-efficient learning and better transferability across architectures, which could impact efficient ML training at scale. TopoPrune operates at two scales: a topology-aware manifold approximation for global low-dimensional embedding, followed by differentiable persistent homology for local topological optimization. It maintains high accuracy even at 90% pruning rates and is robust to latent feature noise and architecture transfer.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Data pruning reduces training dataset size by selecting informative samples, often using geometric methods that rely on pretrained model embeddings. However, these methods are sensitive to perturbations in latent space. Topological data analysis, particularly persistent homology, captures intrinsic structural features that are more stable, offering a principled alternative.

## [interwhen: Single-Trajectory Test-Time Verification for Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11202) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers including Subbarao Kambhampati introduced interwhen, a single-trajectory test-time verification framework that periodically polls reasoning traces, forks inference to recover intermediate states, and runs verifiers asynchronously to steer reasoning models with minimal overhead. The framework also automatically synthesizes code-based verifiers, including provably correct ones in Lean and z3, from natural-language policy documents. Existing verification approaches either check only final answers, missing early errors, or rely on costly branch-and-verify exploration of multiple trajectories. interwhen offers a plug-and-play way to improve task completion and policy compliance for any reasoning agent, with reported gains such as Qwen3-30B's task completion rate on the telecom domain of tau2-bench jumping from 32% to 87% without finetuning. The monitoring system adds negligible overhead on correct executions and intervenes only when violations occur, while verifiers run asynchronously alongside generation. On reasoning benchmarks where policies encode mathematical or logical constraints, interwhen achieves near-perfect accuracy using a fraction of the tokens of baselines, and code is available at github.com/microsoft/interwhen.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Reasoning models such as those producing long chains of thought generate extended traces of intermediate decisions and tool calls, so verifying these traces at test time is important for catching errors early. Test-time verification typically uses verifiers—programs or models that check outputs—but many approaches only check the final answer or explore many branches, which is expensive. Process verification, which checks intermediate steps, is limited by the scarcity of verifiers outside domains like math and code.

## [ATFS: Architecture-Agnostic Defense Against Heterogeneous Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14860) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes the Architecture-Agnostic Targeted Feature Synergy (ATFS) framework, which reformulates multi-model defense as a unified feature space alignment task using a target guidance image. It overcomes destructive interference in pixel-space ensembles by aligning high-level features across heterogeneous generators, achieving over 90% performance within 40 iterations and state-of-the-art protection in Diffusion+GAN scenarios. Existing defenses are often architecture-specific, creating fragile 'defense silos' that fail against heterogeneous generative threats. ATFS offers a universal, computationally efficient solution that can extend to unseen architectures, potentially enabling broader AI safety and content protection across diverse generative models. ATFS aligns high-level feature representations instead of pixel gradients, avoiding orthogonal gradient interference. It converges rapidly (90% within 40 iterations), maintains attack potency under tight perturbation budgets, resists JPEG compression and scaling, and can be extended to new architectures like VQ-VAE by switching the feature extractor; code and models are open-sourced.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Generative models such as Diffusion Models, GANs, and VQ-VAEs are increasingly used to create realistic content, raising concerns about misuse like deepfakes and privacy violations. Adversarial defense aims to protect images by adding imperceptible perturbations that disrupt generation, but defenses designed for one model type often fail on others. Ensemble methods combine perturbations from multiple models, but naive pixel-space averaging can cause destructive interference due to conflicting gradients.

## [Depth-Recurrent Transformer Enables Memory-Efficient Latent Chain-of-Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.21676) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2603.21676v3) introduces a depth-recurrent Transformer that iterates a shared-weight block to perform latent chain-of-thought reasoning without generating tokens, keeping peak inference memory constant and latency growing only linearly with the number of thinking steps. The authors show stable recurrence over 20+ steps using a silent thinking objective, LayerScale initialization, and an identity-biased gate, and evaluate it on three compositional generalization tasks. This work addresses a key bottleneck in chain-of-thought reasoning: the memory cost of the key-value cache when serving large query batches. By decoupling computational depth from parameter count and memory, it could enable more efficient reasoning over many queries and improve compositional generalization, impacting deployment of reasoning models. The model reaches near-perfect accuracy on graph reachability and nested boolean logic tasks once the number of thinking steps meets task complexity, but plateaus lower on unstructured relational text. It extrapolates beyond the training range on the graph task, and on sequence tasks comes within two points of fixed-depth Transformers with 4–6.4× more parameters; notably, intermediate per-step supervision harms extrapolation at widths d ≥ 256 on the graph task.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Standard Transformers have a fixed computational depth, which limits their ability to generalize to tasks requiring variable-depth reasoning. Chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting addresses this by generating intermediate reasoning tokens, but this inflates the key-value cache, making memory the limiting cost when serving large batches. Depth-recurrent Transformers, also called looped Transformers, reuse a single block multiple times, decoupling depth from parameter count and memory. Latent chain-of-thought reasoning performs these intermediate steps in hidden states without emitting tokens.

## [NSFL: Training-Free Neuro-Symbolic Fuzzy Logic for Boolean Operators in Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10604) ⭐️ 7.0/10

NSFL is a training-free neuro-symbolic fuzzy logic framework that injects multi-atom Boolean logical constraints into neural embedding spaces using a first-order finite-difference treatment of similarity. It reports mAP@100 improvements up to +81% on QUEST and +70% on a COCO-derived text-to-image benchmark across six encoder configurations and two modalities. Dense retrievers lack a native mechanism for multi-atom logical constraints, so NSFL's training-free approach could enable more precise Boolean queries in retrieval systems without retraining encoders. The reported gains across multiple encoders and modalities suggest potential broad applicability in information retrieval and embedding research. NSFL anchors logical operations on zero-order atomic scores while steering with Neuro-Symbolic Deltas (NS-Delta) to preserve atomic meaning and avoid representation collapse. For latency-constrained deployment, Spherical Query Optimization (SQO) uses Riemannian optimization to project logical formulas into manifold-stable query vectors, and the decomposition is exact and general, though the operators are engineering realizations rather than a closed algebra.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Dense retrieval represents queries and documents as vectors in a continuous embedding space, enabling semantic search but lacking explicit support for Boolean operators like AND, OR, and NOT. Neuro-symbolic methods combine neural networks with symbolic reasoning to enforce logical constraints, while fuzzy logic allows partial truth values that are more compatible with continuous embeddings. NSFL builds on these ideas to inject logical structure into embeddings without additional training.

## [Branching-Process Model Reveals Fault Propagation Threshold in AI-Agent Repositories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.19827) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2604.19827v2) models how faults spread among concurrent AI coding agents' open changes as a branching process over file contention, defining a repository-level 'reference threshold' — the per-contention-link crossing probability above which fault propagation becomes self-sustaining. Analyzing 33,105 agent-authored pull requests across 2,731 repositories, the authors found 13 repositories with thresholds below 5% that contained 42.5% of all changes. This work highlights a blind spot in code review: reviewing each change in isolation cannot reveal repository-level conditions that allow faults to spread among concurrent agent-authored changes. As AI coding agents generate more code and pull requests, engineering teams may need new metrics to assess where concurrent work exposes their repositories to cascading faults. The model borrows the reproduction-number concept from epidemiology: spread sustains itself once each faulty change passes the fault, on average, to more than one other change. The reference threshold is computed from contention degrees in a random graph and can be derived from records repositories already keep, though the analysis is theoretical and its practical predictive validity remains to be validated.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: AI coding agents are increasingly used in parallel within shared repositories, producing many concurrent pull requests. A branching process is a stochastic model in which each individual independently produces a random number of offspring, and it underlies the reproduction number R0 used to describe whether an epidemic will grow or die out. 'Contention' here refers to two changes open at the same time that modify a common file, creating a link through which a fault can propagate.

## [GRASP: Graph Agentic Search over Propositions for Multi-hop QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16598) ⭐️ 7.0/10

GRASP introduces an agentic retrieval system that decomposes multi-hop queries into dependency-aware plans and explores a three-layer hierarchical graph of entities, propositions, and passages. It achieves the highest QA accuracy on MuSiQue and 2WikiMultihopQA in open-corpus retrieval while using 40-50% fewer tokens than IRCoT+HippoRAG2, and leads on EM and F1 across all three datasets in the LongBench setting with 30% fewer tokens than the next most accurate method. This work addresses the high cost of combining agentic retrieval with knowledge graphs, showing that hierarchical graph traversal and dependency-aware decomposition can improve accuracy while significantly reducing token usage. It also proposes 'success economy' as a new efficiency-aware metric, which could influence how future agentic QA systems are evaluated. GRASP uses the entity layer for targeted traversal and the proposition layer for high-recall passage retrieval via reciprocal-rank voting, and it dynamically scales the number of sub-agents based on problem complexity. The proposed 'success economy' metric measures amortized token cost per correct answer, weighted by difficulty, and the authors advocate for efficiency-aware evaluation as standard practice.

**Background**: Multi-hop question answering requires combining information from multiple passages to answer complex queries, and agentic retrieval gives language models autonomy to iteratively gather evidence. Knowledge graphs can provide structured traversal but introduce expensive graph construction and compounding token costs at inference. GRASP builds a hierarchical graph of entities, propositions (atomic factual statements), and passages to balance accuracy and efficiency.

## [HydraGNN-based heterogeneous graph foundation model scales optimal power flow surrogate modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23194) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces a scalable heterogeneous graph-learning workflow built on HydraGNN for data-driven optimal power flow (OPF) surrogate modeling and graph foundation-model development, evaluated on approximately three million power grid graphs from ten PGLib-OPF cases spanning 14 to 13,659 buses. It compares six heterogeneous GNN families under a common hyperparameter optimization campaign on the Frontier supercomputer, resulting in two compact models (HeteroSAGE and HeteroHEAT, ~1.6-1.7 million parameters) that achieve the lowest validation MSE. Fast and reliable OPF approximation is critical for power system operation, and this work demonstrates a scalable, generalizable foundation-model approach that could reduce computational burden in smart grid operations. The use of millions of graphs and distributed training on up to 1,024 GPUs shows a path toward large-scale graph foundation models for power systems, potentially benefiting grid operators and researchers. The workflow preserves buses, generators, loads, shunts, AC lines, transformers, and device-to-bus relations, and provides a common implementation for distributed preprocessing, multi-GPU training, HPO, and downstream adaptation. Scaling tests on up to 1,024 Frontier nodes show that 256 nodes (1,024 AMD MI250X GPUs) provide the best time-to-solution balance, and downstream tasks on the IEEE 118-bus case include classification of extreme-overload conditions and N-1 outage regression, where partial fine-tuning offers the best trade-off.

**Background**: Optimal power flow (OPF) is a fundamental optimization problem in power systems that determines the most efficient operating setpoints while respecting physical and operational constraints. Heterogeneous graphs are needed to represent the diverse components of a power grid (buses, generators, loads, etc.) and their relationships, and graph foundation models aim to pre-train on large graph corpora for broad downstream use. A new arXiv paper (2606.07007v2) proposes a unified mathematical framework that formalizes concepts as sets of data points and casts concept learning as a set-alignment problem between human-defined and model-induced concepts. It distinguishes three increasingly strong notions of learning—detection, separation, and approximation—and derives geometric conditions, error bounds, and capacity constraints for when concepts can be represented by individual neurons or multi-neuron units. This work provides a principled theoretical foundation for sparse autoencoders (SAEs), a key tool in mechanistic interpretability, by clarifying what it means for an SAE to 'learn a concept' and explaining phenomena like feature splitting and absorption. It could guide the design and evaluation of SAEs and help researchers interpret neuron behavior more rigorously. The framework uses formal concept analysis to connect concept learning and neuron interpretation, showing that the two directions need not agree and that their many-to-many structure can be organized by concept lattices. Experiments on synthetic data with ReLU and Top-K SAEs illustrate the theory and reveal how SAE size and sparsity affect concept learning.

Researchers propose Entropy-Guided Power Sampling (EGPS), a training-free and verifier-free approximate sampler that uses token-level entropy to focus MCMC resampling on high-entropy decision points. On Qwen2.5-Math-7B, EGPS achieves best or tied-best accuracy on MATH500 (75.8%), HumanEval (62.2%), and GPQA (42.4%), with wall-clock speedups up to 12.6x over the standard Metropolis-Hastings baseline. This method makes inference-time reasoning enhancement for base language models significantly more efficient by scaling sampling cost with entropy mass rather than sequence length. It could enable practical deployment of training-free reasoning improvements without expensive post-training or external verifiers. EGPS skips deterministic blocks, localizes each MCMC move to a high-entropy neighborhood, and applies Multiple-Try Metropolis at decision points. Across three base models and three benchmarks, it attains best or tied-best accuracy on 5 of 9 settings, though results vary by model and task.

**Background**: Power sampling from the sequence-level power distribution can elicit reinforcement-learning-level reasoning from base language models without parameter updates, but standard Metropolis-Hastings (MH) MCMC sampling is expensive and slow-mixing. The power distribution mainly departs from the base distribution at sparse, clustered high-entropy decision points, while MH proposes resampling positions uniformly, wasting compute on near-degenerate conditionals. EGPS addresses this structural mismatch by re-deriving proposals from token-level entropy already computed in the forward pass.

## [LARA: Lagrangian Reward Augmentation for Safe Inference-Time Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02781) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LARA is a new inference-time alignment framework that dualizes a KL-regularized constrained objective into a one-dimensional convex problem over a nonnegative dual variable, yielding an augmented reward that can be used as a drop-in scoring signal in existing methods like Best-of-N reranking. Evaluated on both sequence-level and token-level inference-time alignment methods, LARA improves the helpfulness-harmlessness tradeoff, with Best-of-N achieving the best performance among inference-time methods and approaching finetuning-based direct alignment baselines. This work provides a principled way to enforce explicit safety constraints during inference without retraining, which is valuable for AI safety and alignment researchers seeking efficient deployment of safe language models. It bridges constrained optimization theory and practical inference-time alignment, potentially reducing the need for costly weight updates while maintaining safety guarantees. The dual variable is estimated on a small calibration set and corresponds to the solution of the expected-cost constrained problem for sequence-level sampling methods like Best-of-N reranking, but for token-level reward-guided decoding it yields a principled dual-calibrated heuristic rather than an exact constrained-policy guarantee. The approach reduces the optimization to a 1D convex problem, making it computationally efficient.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Inference-time alignment steers a frozen language model during decoding using auxiliary reward signals, avoiding repeated weight updates. Existing methods typically optimize a single scalar score, so safety constraints must be ignored or encoded via manually tuned penalties. Lagrangian duality is a mathematical framework for solving constrained optimization problems by converting them into unconstrained ones with dual variables. KL regularization penalizes deviation from the base model's next-token distribution, and Best-of-N reranking is a sampling method that generates multiple candidates and selects the best according to a score.

## [Quantum RL Algorithms Approach Lower Bounds for MDP Policy Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02826) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.02826v2) proposes quantum algorithms for computing approximate optimal policies in both finite-horizon and infinite-horizon discounted Markov Decision Processes. The algorithms combine standard value iteration with quantum mean estimation and quantum maximum finding, enhanced by techniques from sample-optimal classical algorithms, achieving query complexities that improve on prior work and approach established quantum lower bounds. This work tightens the gap between upper and lower bounds for quantum reinforcement learning, suggesting that the proposed approach may be near-optimal in query complexity. It matters for researchers in quantum machine learning theory and could guide future algorithm design for quantum-accelerated decision-making. The improvement is in query complexity, a theoretical measure of environment interactions, and the algorithms are designed under a generative model that allows sampling of state transitions. The paper is a theoretical contribution; practical implementation costs, quantum memory requirements, and end-to-end speedups are not addressed.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning trains agents to maximize reward through interaction, often formalized as a Markov Decision Process (MDP) where an optimal policy maps states to actions. Classical algorithms like value iteration compute such policies, and quantum computing promises speedups by manipulating superpositions of states. Quantum mean estimation estimates averages of values encoded in quantum states, while quantum maximum finding identifies the largest among many candidates, both used here as subroutines. Query complexity counts how many times an algorithm accesses the environment, and lower bounds establish the minimum queries any algorithm needs, so approaching them indicates near-optimal efficiency.

## [Graph Signal Processing Reveals How LLMs Organize Numerical Sequences in Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03015) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.03015v2) applies graph signal processing to analyze how large language models internally represent numerical sequences during in-context learning. It finds that as context length grows, attention-induced token graphs and hidden-state signals become increasingly differentiated by the input's dynamical complexity. This work offers an interpretability lens beyond output-level metrics like prediction error, potentially helping researchers understand why LLMs succeed or fail at numerical reasoning. It could inform the design of better prompting strategies and model diagnostics for numerical tasks. The analysis shows that simpler inputs yield attention graphs with stronger global connectivity and smoother, spectrally concentrated hidden-state signals, while complex inputs produce more localized graphs with broader spectral support and higher high-frequency energy. These internal signatures are qualitatively conserved across different model families.

**Background**: Graph signal processing extends classical signal processing to data defined on irregular graph structures, where nodes carry signals and edges encode relationships. In transformers, attention weights can be viewed as forming a weighted graph over tokens, with hidden states as signals on those nodes. In-context learning refers to an LLM's ability to perform tasks from examples provided in the prompt without weight updates.

## [Factor analysis of 1,618 LLMs questions a single general intelligence factor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36515) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.36515v2) applies psychometric factor analysis to 13,230 published evaluation scores from 1,618 language models across 456 text-only benchmarks, finding that a general intelligence factor (G) explains only a modest and highly variable share of variance. Across imputations passing a quality filter, G's share of summed-score variance (omega_h) ranges from 0.04 to 0.76 and its share of common variance (ECV) from 0.11 to 0.52. The study directly tests a widely held but rarely verified assumption in LLM development—that abilities are organized around a domain-free general intelligence factor—and suggests that, from public benchmark scores alone, general intelligence is hard to define, identify, and target as a single construct. This has implications for how researchers design benchmarks, interpret leaderboards, and plan training regimes. Because the score matrix is sparse, the authors triangulate across multiple data densifiers and imputation methods, and report three robust patterns: same-subject benchmarks are not guaranteed to cluster closely in vector space (weak median label cohesion), benchmarks loading highest on G tend to be miscellaneous rather than thematically coherent, and standard "intelligence" benchmarks do not dominate the ability variance that G explains. The authors hypothesize that targeting a single conceptual ability has little support because the first-order abilities it would need to reach are often partially idiosyncratic and not identifiable in practice.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Factor analysis is a latent-variable technique from psychometrics that infers unobserved dimensions (factors) from correlations among observed test scores; it is commonly used to study constructs like human intelligence. In this framing, a general factor (often called G, analogous to fluid intelligence in humans) is thought to underlie performance across many cognitive tasks. The paper imports this framework to language models, using metrics such as omega_h (the proportion of total summed-score variance attributable to the general factor) and ECV (the proportion of common variance explained by the general factor) to quantify how much a single factor accounts for benchmark performance.

## [Sparse-WAM: Training-Free Acceleration for World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38984) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Sparse-WAM is a training-free framework that accelerates world-action model (WAM) inference by using action-guided sparse imagination to selectively process future-frame tokens during joint denoising. It achieves approximately 2.0x speedup on LIBERO with FastWAM-Joint and 1.8x on RoboLab-120 with Cosmos 3 Edge over dense eager inference on an NVIDIA RTX 4090, while largely preserving task performance. World-action models that jointly predict future visual states and actions are powerful for robot control but suffer from high inference cost due to repeated processing of dense future-frame tokens. Sparse-WAM addresses this bottleneck without retraining, making these models more practical for real-time robotic applications. The method exploits the observation that action-to-future attention distributions overlap substantially between consecutive denoising steps, enabling Action-Guided Token Selection that retains frame-specific action-relevant regions plus cross-frame context. A naive implementation would incur attention-scoring and token-packing overhead, so the authors introduce Pilot, an efficient engine that uses lightweight scoring and cross-step reuse of token selections to reduce sparse inference overhead.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: World-action models (WAMs) leverage pretrained video diffusion models to jointly predict future visual frames and robot actions, improving generalization in robot control. However, during the iterative denoising process, dense future-frame tokens are repeatedly processed, leading to substantial inference cost. Prior token pruning methods for video diffusion focus on visual fidelity but ignore action relevance, which is critical for WAMs where actions guide the prediction.

## [VisionQ benchmarks VLMs as judges of computer vision method outputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00666) ⭐️ 7.0/10

VisionQ introduces a benchmark of blind multiple-choice questions built from 1,409 CVPR and ICCV papers, where each question tests one named criterion (e.g., visual sharpness) and the correct answer is the authors' claimed best result. The best of 21 VLM judges matches authors' claims 69% of the time versus 32% chance, but remains largely accurate without the criterion and changes answers when options are reordered; a trained VisionQ-Judge raises accuracy on unseen papers from 50% to 61%. This work provides a systematic, large-scale evaluation of VLMs as objective judges for qualitative comparisons in computer vision papers, an area traditionally reliant on human judgment. It reveals reliability issues such as option-order sensitivity and limited criterion grounding, and offers a trained judge that could support more objective, scalable assessment of method outputs across past, present, and future research. The benchmark uses 1,409 papers and 21 judges, with the best judge achieving 69% agreement against 32% chance; however, it retains most accuracy without the criterion and is sensitive to option ordering. Training on every option order of a larger question set yields VisionQ-Judge, which improves unseen-paper accuracy from 50% to 61%, and data, weights, and code are released on Hugging Face and GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) are AI models that process both images and text, and they are increasingly used as automated judges in evaluation pipelines, a practice known as LLM-as-judge. In computer vision papers, qualitative comparison figures often show outputs from different methods side by side to argue which is best on a named criterion, but such judgments are usually made by human authors. VisionQ tests whether VLMs can serve as objective, criterion-specific judges for these comparisons.

## [GlitchPatch repairs glitch tokens in frozen LLMs via local retokenization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04399) ⭐️ 7.0/10

GlitchPatch introduces a two-stage framework that repairs glitch tokens in frozen language models by optimizing input tokenization rather than modifying model internals. It achieves an 85.10% mean fix rate across ten models from six tokenizer families, outperforming the strongest baseline by 14.37 percentage points and reducing the average glitch rate from 14.88% to 2.27%. This approach enables repair of deployed, frozen checkpoints without access to model weights or internals, making it practical for real-world LLM services. It also provides empirical insights into BPE merge-rule deletion, showing that disrupting shared merge nodes can increase overall glitch rates. The offline stage uses Behavioral Path Optimization (BPO) to find optimal replacement token sequences and compiles them into a rule table, while the online stage substitutes only matched glitch token IDs with no parameter changes. The method achieves 0.00% RR in full-vocabulary evaluation and leaves rule-unmatched inputs unchanged by design.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Glitch tokens are anomalous vocabulary entries that cause large language models to produce outputs inconsistent with their inputs, sometimes leading to crashes or hallucinations. Tokenization, typically based on Byte Pair Encoding (BPE), splits text into subword units, and glitch tokens often arise from rare or malformed merge rules. Existing repair methods require access to model internals, which is impractical for frozen checkpoints deployed in production.

## [In LoRA, Which Factor You Freeze Matters More Than Spectral Band at Low Rank](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06621) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A controlled 2x2 study on models from 1B to 8B parameters finds that at ranks 1–8, freezing the input factor A in LoRA variants outperforms freezing the output factor B by +12 to +20 points at rank 2 on a format task, and this factor choice matters more than the choice of spectral band (minor vs. dominant singular directions). This finding gives practitioners a simple, actionable rule for parameter-efficient fine-tuning: at low ranks, prioritize freezing factor A over factor B, which can yield significant accuracy gains without extra parameters. It also challenges the assumption that spectral band selection is the primary design axis in LoRA variants like MiCA and PiCa. The advantage holds under a Holm correction over 258 primary tests in 15 of 16 tests at ranks 1, 2, and 4, but at rank 8 it passes its own test yet not the correction; it narrows above rank 8 and closes at the last step of longer training on OpenBookQA, and is smaller or undetected at MiCA's and PiCa's own settings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method that freezes pre-trained weights and injects trainable low-rank matrices A and B into each Transformer layer. Variants like MiCA and PiCa differ in which factor they freeze and which band of singular directions they target, but prior work had not disentangled the effects of these two choices.
