## [CFGNP: End-to-End Counterfactual Prediction Without Causal Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13724) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces the Counterfactual Graph Neural Process (CFGNP), an end-to-end method that uses Graph Neural Networks to answer counterfactual queries directly from observational data without first estimating a causal graph. CFGNP achieves 1.6–7× lower MSE than baselines that receive the true causal graph and the same counterfactual supervision across seven benchmark variants. This work addresses a key limitation in causal inference: errors in estimated causal graphs can propagate to downstream counterfactual predictions. By eliminating the need for explicit causal graph estimation, CFGNP could make counterfactual reasoning more practical in fields like healthcare and policy, where causal structure is often unknown. CFGNP exploits a dual-graph structure by restricting cross-world message passing to corresponding factual–counterfactual variables. Notably, a model trained only on 10-node graphs maintains strong predictive performance on graphs with up to 30 nodes without retraining, suggesting good scalability.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Counterfactual inference asks how an observed outcome might have differed under different circumstances, such as whether a patient would have recovered with a different treatment. Traditionally, answering such queries requires knowing the underlying causal structure, often estimated from observational data. However, errors in the estimated causal graph can propagate to counterfactual predictions. Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) are deep learning models designed to operate on graph-structured data, and they have been increasingly used for causal inference tasks.

## [Tab-Loop: Looped Transformers Match Tabular ICL with Fewer Parameters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14998) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce Tab-Loop, a looped tabular foundation model built on TabICLv2 that reuses a small set of shared-parameter transformer blocks in a loop during in-context learning. Under equal compute budgets, Tab-Loop matches the accuracy of a standard model with three times as many ICL blocks while using roughly 60% fewer parameters. This work shows that tabular foundation models can scale compute independently of parameter count, suggesting test-time compute scaling as a practical path to efficient tabular models. It challenges the prevailing assumption that each transformer block needs unique parameters, which could reduce memory and deployment costs for tabular ICL systems. Tab-Loop matches a standard model with 3x more ICL blocks while using ~60% fewer parameters, and a non-looped baseline of the same size underperforms it even when trained on 32% more tables. Running additional loops at inference improves accuracy without retraining, and with deeper training loops it eventually matches released TabICLv2 on TabArena at 2.7x the ICL compute.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Tabular foundation models perform in-context learning (ICL): they predict unlabelled rows from labelled rows in a single forward pass, similar to how large language models use examples in a prompt. TabICLv2 is an open tabular foundation model that chains column-wise embedding, row-wise interaction, and dataset-wise ICL. Looped transformers, such as Google's Universal Transformer and ALBERT, reuse the same block repeatedly to improve parameter efficiency and expressivity.

## [Redistribution Sensitivity Score Reveals Recurring SAE Feature Changes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21240) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces the redistribution sensitivity score (RSS) to identify large, recurring sparse autoencoder (SAE) feature changes across prompt variants and evaluates their downstream contribution across 27 pretrained SAEs and 8 language models. RSS-selected features show approximately twice the mean held-out contribution of features chosen by the largest readout coefficients, and combining RSS contributions with the clean margin improves recurrent failure AUROC from 0.863 to 0.935 and from 0.901 to 0.958. This work offers a systematic method for linking recurring sparse representation changes to downstream safety-relevant behavior, which could improve interpretability-based safety monitoring and failure prediction in language models. It matters for researchers studying SAE features and for practitioners building safety readouts, as it shows that activation responses alone can identify features with meaningful causal effects. RSS selects features on one set of prompt variants and evaluates them on separate held-out variants, revealing that context and wrapper transformations produce stronger recurring responses than local edits. The method uses two frozen safety readouts, and an exact decomposition explains how feature changes and readout coefficients determine effects on the decision margin; code is available on GitHub.

The paper introduces SAE Additivity Residuals (SAR) to quantify higher-order feature interactions in sparse autoencoders, proving that SAR measures interaction energy and bounds ranking errors. Pairwise modeling improves ranking of held-out feature combinations in all 18 settings, and in MMLU-Pro steering it raises rank fidelity from 0.5874 to 0.7790 while reducing ordering error from 0.1444 to 0.0606. The analysis assumes independent feature subset sampling and focuses on behavioral responses rather than direct activation reconstruction.

## [Conditional Flow Models Convert Epistemic Uncertainty into Apparent Aleatoric Variance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29172) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.29172v2) identifies a systematic phenomenon in Conditional Flow Models (CFMs): their generated samples often have larger variance than the true conditional distribution, even in nearly deterministic regression tasks. The authors show that this excess variance arises because CFMs convert epistemic uncertainty about the conditional distribution into apparent aleatoric variance, and that the effect decreases with more training or data. This finding is significant for uncertainty quantification and model reliability, as CFMs are increasingly used as probabilistic predictors in place of deterministic models. Practitioners relying on CFM-generated distributions for decision-making may misinterpret inflated variance as inherent data noise, leading to overconfident or miscalibrated uncertainty estimates. The paper provides a theoretical account in a simplified setting and shows that the induced variance tracks model underfitting of complex or hard-to-fit targets, with a structure unique to CFMs compared to other standard probabilistic models. It also demonstrates a practical use-case: designing high-coverage predictive sets that cover the ground-truth regression target.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Conditional Flow Models (CFMs) are generative models that learn a conditional distribution by transforming a simple base distribution through a continuous flow, often trained via conditional flow matching. They have become popular for tasks like regression because they can capture complex, multi-modal output distributions. In uncertainty quantification, aleatoric uncertainty refers to inherent noise in the data, while epistemic uncertainty refers to uncertainty due to lack of knowledge or data. The paper shows that CFMs can blur this distinction by turning epistemic uncertainty into apparent aleatoric variance.

Researchers fine-tuned a pre-trained V-JEPA1 predictor from bidirectional latent inpainting to action-free causal forecasting while keeping the encoder frozen, enabling it to forecast eight future token groups (sixteen frames) in one forward pass. On CARLA Town12, the model retrieved the correct future among 591 candidates in 69.0% of cases, compared to 55.3% for persistence, with greater gains at longer horizons. This work demonstrates that a self-supervised video model pre-trained on bidirectional inpainting can be adapted to causal forecasting for autonomous driving without retraining the encoder, suggesting a path toward efficient latent-space prediction for driving perception. The approach outperforms a compact predictor trained from scratch with six times fewer parameters, highlighting the value of pre-trained representations. The model uses contiguous past observations, horizon-aligned targets, and group-level causal attention; semantic analysis shows a fixed linear probe achieves 0.16 mIoU on patches with semantic-class changes versus 0.13 for persistence, and vehicle-conditioned retrieval improves over persistence by 15 percentage points at the longest horizon. However, pixel-space evaluation shows only modest L1 gains and little improvement under LPIPS, and downstream utility remains unverified.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: V-JEPA (Video Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture) is a self-supervised framework from Meta that learns video representations by predicting masked spatiotemporal regions in an abstract embedding space rather than reconstructing raw pixels. Latent inpainting refers to filling in missing parts of a latent representation using bidirectional context, which is the pre-training objective of V-JEPA. Causal forecasting, in contrast, predicts future latents using only past observations, making it suitable for real-time driving scenarios. CARLA is an open-source urban driving simulator, and Town12 is one of its most complex maps used for autonomous driving research.

MAAS (Multi-Area Auditory Synthesis) introduces an end-to-end framework that directly conditions a pretrained audio diffusion transformer (Stable Audio) on ROI-wise fMRI responses, bypassing the need for an intermediate stimulus representation. On a 7T fMRI dataset, it outperforms baselines including two-stage fMRI-to-latent conditioning on acoustic and semantic metrics. This work extends direct brain conditioning from vision to audio diffusion models, potentially simplifying brain-to-audio pipelines and improving reconstruction fidelity. It could advance brain-computer interfaces and deepen understanding of how auditory cortical regions contribute to perceived sound. MAAS uses region-specific projections and learned brain tokens to condition Stable Audio through a dedicated cross-attention pathway. The authors also introduce a fidelity metric based on a computational model of auditory neural processing to quantify the contribution of individual auditory cortical regions.

## [Four-Lens Framework Maps Where Uncertainty Lives in LLM Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15735) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.15735v2) proposes a mechanistic framework that defines four observation lenses—structural, representational, distributional, and semantical—to answer where uncertainty is observed in an LLM's computation. The lenses are related through the residual stream and ordered by the information they carry, providing a principle for selecting uncertainty quantification (UQ) methods for different targets. This framework addresses a gap in existing UQ taxonomies by focusing on the observation point, which determines whether an estimator has enough information to recover a given target. It could guide the development of more reliable UQ methods, impacting AI safety and interpretability research. The paper surveys existing UQ methods through the four lenses and distinguishes evaluation targets, criteria, and metrics to clarify different UQ evaluation protocols. It also identifies open problems and research directions to connect uncertainty estimation to the internal computation and downstream use of LLMs.

Existing UQ taxonomies organize methods by uncertainty source or estimation mechanism but leave unresolved where uncertainty is observed in the model's computation.

## [DuoJEPA translates between unaligned self-supervised embedding spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15968) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DuoJEPA trains one self-supervised encoder per modality with the LeJEPA objective and adds a single transformer layer to each encoder that translates its embedding into the other encoder's space, supervised by a contrastive loss computed only in the target space. Experiments show that with a residual translator the two encoders become directly aligned, while a skipless translator yields no detectable alignment yet comparable cross-modal performance. This work challenges the standard assumption that cross-modal learning requires aligned embedding spaces, showing that translation can decouple alignment from performance. It could influence how multimodal models are designed, potentially enabling more flexible and modular representations. The learned class-token translation is well approximated by a single scaled rotation of the source space, and DuoJEPA achieves a zero-shot AUC of 86.6 and retrieval score of 78.5 on CheXpert Plus, compared to 60.0 and 64.2 for a LeVLJEPA reimplementation. On Sentinel-1/2 imagery, the translator retrieves the paired image among 4,000 candidates in 77-79% of cases.

**Background**: JEPA (Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture) is a self-supervised learning approach that predicts abstract representations rather than reconstructing raw inputs. LeJEPA is a variant used here to train each modality's encoder independently. Cross-modal retrieval typically relies on aligning embeddings from different modalities into a shared space, but DuoJEPA explores an alternative where alignment is not enforced directly.

## [Imitation Policies Learn a Proprioceptive Anchor to Discount Noisy Object Positions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17658) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.17658v2) shows that imitation policies for robot manipulation learn to discount noisy object position readings by a factor α = V/(V + s²), where V is the error variance of a proprioceptive anchor derived from the robot's own state and s² is the reading noise. This predictive law, measured once, forecast ten held-out sensitivities across two robomimic tasks, two learners, and four new noise levels with mean absolute error 0.025, compared to 0.37 for classical errors-in-variables regression. This work provides a theoretical explanation for why imitation policies are robust to noisy object positions, showing that the robot's own state acts as an anchor that reduces reliance on noisy readings. It offers a predictive law that outperforms classical errors-in-variables regression, which could help researchers better understand and design robust imitation learning systems for robotics. The discount factor α does not carry over to deployment, where closed-loop sensitivity to a persistent reading offset exceeded α in all 15 policies that passed registered gates, though the stricter prediction that all would follow the offset fully held for 8. Lower α correlated with lower success, especially for diffusion policies, and a prospective test confirmed that measured α predicted success within 0.03 (95% intervals ±0.08) for diffusion on the Can task at two new noise levels, although training noise predicted success equally well. All results are in simulation with synthetic Gaussian noise, and every test was pre-registered.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Imitation learning trains robot policies by mimicking expert demonstrations, but demonstrations often contain noisy object positions, and policies must learn to cope with this noise. Errors-in-variables regression is a classical statistical method that accounts for measurement errors in independent variables, but it can overestimate the discount factor because it assumes a linear model of the demonstrations. Proprioception refers to the robot's sense of its own body state, which can provide information about the environment, such as where an object is located, because demonstrators steer toward the object.

## [Machine-learned oracle models automate parameter selection for sample-based quantum diagonalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18307) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.18307v2) demonstrates that machine-learned oracle models can predict optimal configuration choices—subspace dimension, timestep, and sampling allocation—for Sample-based Quantum Diagonalization (SQD) directly from Hamiltonian properties. The models achieve strong rank correlations with true error and runtime (Spearman ρ = 0.969 and 0.905) and correctly classify held-out molecules at the molecule level with balanced accuracy of 61.8% in-distribution and 100% for five larger held-out molecules. SQD methods lack a formal way to choose hyperparameters, so this work shows that AI can automate a critical tuning step, potentially making quantum chemistry simulations more reliable and resource-efficient on near-term hardware. It bridges machine learning and quantum computing, offering a practical tool for predicting feasibility and runtime before execution. The study uses synthetic Hamiltonians with controllable spectral properties to validate predictions against classical Krylov propagation, and extends to 50 molecular Hamiltonians (20-50 qubits) with reference ground-state energies from classical calculations. Classifiers trained on 600 SqDRIFT quantum-hardware runs distinguish whether a configuration achieves chemical accuracy (1.6×10⁻³ Ha ≈ 1 kcal/mol), and the models provide a priori feasibility and resource estimates.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Sample-based Quantum Diagonalization (SQD) is a hybrid quantum-classical technique that estimates ground-state energies by sampling from a quantum circuit and then classically diagonalizing the resulting subspace. Its accuracy and runtime depend heavily on choices like subspace dimension and sampling budget, but no formal selection method exists. SKQD (Sample-based Krylov Quantum Diagonalization) extends SQD by using Krylov subspace methods, and SqDRIFT is a randomized variant run on quantum hardware.

A new arXiv thesis presents four theoretical contributions to graph neural network expressivity: EGenNet for geometric message passing on sparse graphs, representation-theoretic characterizations of equivariant linear layers, MASNet for containment-preserving set representations, and FSW-GNN, a bi-Lipschitz stable architecture with strong performance on long-range tasks. These results deepen the theoretical understanding of how GNNs can preserve geometric, algebraic, order-theoretic, and metric structure, which could guide the design of more powerful and reliable models for structured data in domains like chemistry, biology, and network analysis. The thesis introduces three new architectures (EGenNet, MASNet, FSW-GNN) and establishes dimensional bounds and impossibility results for permutation-invariant set representations that preserve containment; FSW-GNN uniquely combines strong expressivity with bi-Lipschitz stability guarantees.

Geometric deep learning extends these ideas to data with symmetries, using tools from group theory and representation theory to design architectures that respect underlying invariances.

## [MRPD Replays RL Logs to Consolidate Experts Without Resampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24059) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose Multi-Expert Replay Policy Distillation (MRPD), which consolidates multiple domain-specific RL experts into a single student model by replaying the experts' existing training logs instead of resampling from environments. On three long-horizon agentic domains (reasoning, search, and SQL), MRPD produces the best student model, outperforming online RL baselines and surpassing multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) at roughly one-seventh of the compute cost. This approach drastically reduces the cost of consolidating RL experts, which is a key bottleneck in frontier post-training pipelines, and it mitigates training-inference mismatch by following the experts' own trajectory order. It could make multi-domain model consolidation more accessible and efficient for teams training specialized agents. MRPD recomputes policy gradients on replayed scored trajectories and distills the final experts' distributions at logged prefixes, keeping the student within the clip range so the replay is off-policy in origin but quasi-on-policy throughout consolidation. Notably, the order of the logs matters more than their amount or rewards, suggesting that expert training logs act as a curriculum rather than a data mixture.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: In frontier post-training, a common recipe is to train one expert per domain with reinforcement learning, then consolidate them into a single model via multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD). MOPD is expensive because the student must sample in every environment again and an expert must score every sampled token. MRPD instead leverages the rollouts, sampling probabilities, and rewards already produced during the experts' RL training, avoiding additional environment interaction.

## [Concordia AI and Z AI Release Risk Framework for Open-Weight Frontier Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28082) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Concordia AI and Z AI co-developed a new framework that adapts the full AI risk management lifecycle—assessment, mitigation, and governance—to the unique properties of frontier open-weight models, as detailed in arXiv paper 2611.28082v2. The framework is presented as the first comprehensive, evidence-based foundation for navigating tensions between openness and safety in open-weight releases. Open-weight models offer transparency and innovation but carry irreversible risks, such as the inability to withdraw a released model or prevent safeguard removal via fine-tuning. This framework fills a timely policy and technical gap by providing lifecycle-based best practices and standards for developers, policymakers, and researchers as frontier open-weight releases accelerate. The framework explicitly acknowledges that it does not resolve every tension between openness and safety, instead offering a foundation for further work. It addresses distinct open-weight risks like irreversible releases, fine-tuning removal of safeguards, and the lack of a single party to monitor use or withdraw the model.

## [Entangle: Scaling Human-Video In-Context World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28732) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Entangle is a new in-context world-action model that takes a single human video and current robot observations to predict future visual states and executable actions. It scales training by converting robot videos into temporally aligned human contexts via video-to-video generation and uses an intra-task contrastive objective to prevent shortcut learning, achieving 83.5/100 on six unseen tasks with an Astribot S1 robot and 69.5% success on seven RoboTwin tasks. This work addresses key challenges in scaling human-video in-context learning for robotic manipulation, showing that performance improves steadily with aligned training data without task-specific fine-tuning. It outperforms Zero-WAM by 22.6 percentage points and demonstrates transfer to out-of-distribution contexts, which could advance general-purpose robot learning from human demonstrations. Entangle combines variable-length video windows with an intra-task contrastive objective that distinguishes the paired action trajectory from other executions of the same task, preventing reliance on task-identity shortcuts. Under Context-OOD, it transfers context-specified behaviors across rearranged layouts and substituted tools, though it is an arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

**Background**: In-context learning (ICL) allows foundation models to perform new tasks from context without parameter updates. Entangle tackles these issues by generating aligned human contexts from robot videos and using contrastive training to enforce context tracking.

This shows that pairing coding agents with external static analysis feedback can dramatically improve their ability to fix project-specific rule violations that are invisible in the code itself, and that a medium model with CodeAssure can outperform a large model alone at about one-fifth the cost, which has significant implications for AI-assisted software engineering workflows. RoboThink introduces a framework where a reasoning agent communicates with a robot policy through instruction and feedback channels, enabling mid-episode corrections without retraining. On LIBERO, it raises success from 0.09 to 0.87 under unknown constant drift and from 0.42 to 0.84 under random drift, while using 55 seconds and 8k tokens per episode compared to 2.1 minutes and over a million tokens for GPT-6 Astra acting alone. This approach addresses a key limitation of current robot learning systems: policies fail without understanding why, and reasoning agents are too slow to control directly. By separating thinking from acting, RoboThink enables efficient, plug-and-play integration of reasoning agents with existing policies, potentially accelerating deployment of adaptable robots in dynamic environments. The framework uses agent-labelled DAgger, role tokens, and counterfactual feedback to teach the feedback channel, which is read only by the policy's action module. It works across vision-language-action models and world action models, and scales from one arm to two arms and a mobile manipulator, with success on RoboTwin 2.0 two-arm tasks rising from 0.12 to 0.74 under constant drift.

RoboAda introduces a test-time adaptation method that keeps a pretrained robot policy's plan unchanged while correcting execution commands by inferring hidden physics from the robot's own motion. Evaluated on LIBERO with five frozen policies and on RoboTwin 2.0, it more than doubles average success under eleven physical shifts (27.8% to 62.9%) and raises success under joint-target offsets from 24.9% to 78.8%. By correcting commands without retraining or rewards, it could make deployed robot policies more robust to real-world changes like payload shifts or actuator degradation. RoboAda uses an inverse-dynamics model of the original robot to convert observed motion into labels for command correction, weighting competing hypotheses about the robot's response without gradient steps or data from the changed robot. It beats the best of seven test-time adaptation methods by 17.3 points on Cosmos Policy and largely preserves success when nothing has changed.

Actionwave introduces a systematic benchmark for evaluating waveform-centric clinical prediction models across intensive care, operating room, and emergency department settings, varying signal duration, warning time, and input availability. The benchmark includes 20 tasks and 1.4 million task-labelled windows, with experimental grids accounting for over 87,000 model fits. This benchmark addresses a critical gap in clinical machine learning by providing a standardized way to assess how model performance depends on real-world factors like signal duration and warning time across different care settings. It enables more reliable comparisons and could lead to better-validated predictive models for bedside monitoring. The protocol enforces patient-level splits, strict information cutoffs, and controlled comparisons to isolate modeling factors, and it evaluates diverse architectures and pretrained biosignal encoders under fixed model-size and compute budgets. The results highlight the need for matched cohorts, unimodal controls, and explicit timing when interpreting waveform model performance.

## [C3H Attack Exploits Compression-to-Consensus Bias in Multimodal LLM Recommenders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32780) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.32780v2) introduces C3H, an inference-time attack that manipulates rankings in multimodal LLM recommender systems by exploiting a compression-to-consensus (C2C) bias. Unlike noisy perturbations or training-time poisoning, C3H tailors a target item's multimodal content to align with the recommender's implicit decision criteria, substantially increasing its exposure while maintaining overall recommendation utility. This work challenges the common assumption that multimodal compression and cross-modal fusion inherently enhance robustness against content manipulation, revealing instead that they can create an exploitable bias. It has significant implications for the security and trustworthiness of AI-powered recommender systems, affecting platforms that deploy multimodal LLMs and the users who rely on their rankings. C3H is an inference-time attack that does not require access to model weights or training data, making it practical for adversaries. ## [LEGOSpec: Composable Framework for Speculative Draft Design and Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33004) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LEGOSpec introduces a modular framework that decomposes speculative decoding draft models into Context, Backbone, Proposal, and Objective components, enabling systematic evaluation under matched training conditions. The framework yields three key findings and produces DFlux, a balanced draft model that improves acceptance length by up to 13.7% on regular benchmarks and 34.7% on long-context workloads over the strongest baseline. This work provides a systematic methodology for understanding and optimizing speculative decoding, moving beyond ad-hoc recipes to isolate component effects and interactions. It offers actionable insights for workload-aware configuration and a concrete model (DFlux) that significantly accelerates LLM inference, benefiting practitioners deploying LLMs at scale. The framework integrates metadata-aware scheduling and asynchronous execution, increasing training throughput by up to 79% and accelerating offline supervision preparation by up to 3.11× over SpecForge. DFlux combines a convolution-equipped SWA-GQA hybrid backbone, a Residual Corrector, and hierarchical supervision, with experiments across Qwen and Gemma models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Speculative decoding accelerates large language model generation by using a lightweight draft model to propose multiple tokens, which are then verified in parallel by the target model. The draft model's design is critical: it must balance acceptance rate (how often proposals are correct) with latency (the cost of drafting), and existing methods often evaluate complete recipes without isolating component contributions. LEGOSpec addresses this by providing a composable framework to systematically study and optimize draft design.

A new arXiv paper (2611.33190) evaluates whether general-purpose decision models like Jev can guide AI configuration tuning across eight tasks. It finds that conventional optimizers rank first in six of eight comparisons and established predictors outperform Jev in seven, but Jev's region guidance yields configurations with lower perplexity at the cost of extra decision time and API fees. This work provides actionable guidance for practitioners choosing decision strategies in AI tuning, showing that general-purpose decision models are not a drop-in replacement for specialized optimizers or predictors. The study compares Jev in three roles—optimizer, performance predictor, and region guider—against conventional methods and a large language model on eight tuning tasks. Region guidance is the only role where Jev shows an advantage, achieving lower perplexity than the native tuner when additional decision time and API fees are acceptable.

A new arXiv paper audits 1,000 B2B SaaS brands across 333 product categories using Gemma 4 12B, finding that category prompts elicit 510 brands (51.0%) while a five-candidate probe ranks the correct category first for 787 brands but misclassifies 19.8% of the 981 separated rankings. ## [Collaborative Language Models for Zero-Shot IoT Semantic Abstraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33411) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.33411) proposes a five-stage workflow using collaborative language models as a zero-shot semantic abstraction layer to convert low-level IoT sensor observations into higher-level event and activity labels. Evaluated on the CASAS Aruba smart home dataset with eleven configurations, the approach achieves a macro F1 of 0.643 in a 1.5-day comparison and 0.567 (weighted F1 0.680) in a one-week evaluation, but finds that collaboration alone does not guarantee improved performance. This work shows that modular semantic event abstraction using off-the-shelf language models without task-specific fine-tuning is a viable alternative design point for IoT data processing, and that the structure of collaboration and task decomposition matters more than simply adding more models. It has implications for process mining and smart home analytics where labeled data is scarce. General-purpose auxiliary small language models (SLMs) can degrade recognition, whereas assigning complementary specialist roles leads to better results and allows a smaller final classifier to retain much of the performance of a larger model. Although trained ML approaches remain more accurate on this dataset, the results demonstrate a different design point: modular semantic event abstraction using off-the-shelf models without task-specific parameter fine-tuning.

In retrieval-only evaluation at k=6, it improves Precision@k from 0.5268 to 0.5692, Recall@k from 0.5402 to 0.5950, and F1@k from 0.5389 to 0.5672 over standard RAG. PeerReveal introduces a detector that distinguishes fully AI-generated peer reviews from AI-assisted human reviews by learning relationships between review claims and reference claims, achieving 70.52% Macro-F1 on a new multi-domain benchmark called PeerShift. An adversarial training framework further boosts pooled Macro-F1 to 82.40% by using a small local generator to produce challenging reviews. As AI-generated reviews become more prevalent in scientific publishing, robust detection is crucial for maintaining peer review integrity and preventing the spread of low-quality or biased evaluations. The PeerShift benchmark comprises 5,430 reviews across machine learning, life sciences, and social sciences, incorporating diverse AI models and workflows. PeerReveal improves over the strongest baseline by 11.48 points in Macro-F1, and adversarial training with a local generator raises performance from 70.52% to 82.40%, though the approach relies on reference claims from AI-generated reviews of the same paper.

Validated over the air with OpenAirInterface, FlexRIC, and commercial Sierra Wireless 5G devices, it reduces SLA latency violations by up to 86% over static slicing and 50% over Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL)-based slicing. This approach addresses the critical need for safe, adaptive network slicing in Open RAN, where purely data-driven controllers struggle to guarantee SLA compliance without continuous retraining. It could enable more reliable and auditable RAN reconfiguration, benefiting telecom operators and service providers deploying 5G and beyond networks. SliceSense monitors live KPIs, retrieves context-conditioned evidence, and ranks candidate RAN actions through a surrogate-guided admissibility filter, while SliceGuard validates post-action KPI responses, rolls back harmful configurations, and refreshes rules under distribution drift. The framework maintains comparable cell throughput and auditable decisions without online retraining.

## [FBQ: Learning Future-Position Probes from the First Generated Token for KV Cache Eviction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33679) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces Faster and Better Query (FBQ), which learns additive offsets on the first generated token's embedding to create pseudo queries for future decoding positions, improving KV cache eviction decisions. On LongBench, FBQ matches the performance of eight-step Lookahead Q-Cache at the same cache budget while requiring only a single forward pass. Efficient KV cache management is critical for reducing memory and latency in LLM inference, especially for long-context applications. FBQ's single-pass approach could enable more practical cache eviction policies without the overhead of draft generation, potentially benefiting deployment of LLMs on resource-constrained systems. FBQ builds on FTQ-8, which places eight copies of the first token ID at consecutive future positions; FBQ preserves the first embedding and learns offsets for the remaining seven positions to predict average prompt attention of a full-cache continuation. The method is trained to approximate future queries but may have limitations in generalization across tasks or models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: In LLM inference, the KV cache stores key and value vectors for all previous tokens to avoid recomputation, but its size grows with sequence length, creating a memory bottleneck. Cache eviction policies decide which tokens to keep when the cache is full, often using attention scores from future queries that are not yet available. Lookahead Q-Cache approximates future queries by generating a draft continuation, but this requires extra autoregressive steps. FBQ instead leverages the first generated token's embedding, already computed during prefill, to construct pseudo queries cheaply.

P-FedSkill introduces a personalized federated skill learning framework that extracts, selects, and routes skills based on task contributions to improve agent performance under heterogeneous client tasks. The framework consists of three components: local skill extraction (LSE), personalized skill selection (PSS), and task-aware skill routing (TASR), and provides a theoretical guarantee for personalized skill selection. This work addresses a key limitation in existing federated skill learning methods, which share skills based on similarity without considering their contributions to heterogeneous tasks, thereby degrading agent performance. By enabling personalized skill selection and task-aware routing, P-FedSkill could improve collaborative learning across clients with diverse tasks while preserving privacy. LSE extracts skill patches (task applicability conditions and execution strategies) and task execution outcomes from trajectories; PSS uses these outcomes to evaluate each skill patch's performance on client tasks and constructs a personalized skill set; TASR adaptively selects a task-relevant subset of skill patches based on applicability conditions to guide agent execution. The paper provides a theoretical guarantee for personalized skill selection and extensive experiments demonstrate effectiveness.

Skill learning aims to improve agent performance by extracting reusable skills from trajectories, but trajectories often contain sensitive information. Federated skill learning (FSL) addresses this by distilling trajectories into compact skill representations that are shared via a central server, but existing methods typically share skills based on similarity without considering their contributions to heterogeneous tasks across clients.

KeyDeck is introduced as the first multi-paper-to-slide generation (multi-PTSG) framework, which integrates per-paper and cross-paper analysis to synthesize multiple research papers into a coherent presentation narrative. It also features reference-guided personalization that infers presenters' narrative, content, textual, and visual preferences to adapt slide generation, and is evaluated on KeyBench, a new benchmark of 100 expert-authored multi-paper presentations. This work formulates a novel task that addresses the real-world need of researchers and professionals who must synthesize multiple papers into a single presentation, moving beyond single-document summarization. The personalization component is crucial because presentation preferences vary widely, and the introduction of KeyBench provides a standardized evaluation resource that could spur further research in this niche area. KeyDeck combines per-paper and cross-paper analysis to identify relationships among sources and synthesize their contributions, and uses reference-guided personalization to infer four types of presenter preferences (narrative, content, textual, visual). The evaluation framework assesses generation quality, personalization adherence, and conversational goal satisfaction, with results showing KeyDeck outperforms existing approaches on all three metrics.

Asset4D-Bench introduces a benchmark to evaluate coding agents on reconstructing editable Blender scenes from visual references, covering 114 conditions and measuring scene quality and dynamics. The benchmark includes 24 from-scratch tasks, where each model earns full dynamics credit on 14, and three GPT-6.1-Sol and eight GPT-6-Astra reconstructions earn zero relation points. This benchmark is significant because it provides a standardized way to assess coding agents' ability to recover objects, motion, and interactions as editable 3D scenes, which is crucial for applications in animation, game development, and simulation. It highlights the gap between models in handling spatial relations, with relations contributing 5.15 points to the 6.50-point difference in mean recovered fidelity between GPT-6.1-Sol and GPT-6-Astra. The benchmark uses 228 archived scores including original delivery outcomes and versioned measures of scene quality, and it independently reopens projects to measure them against native reference states. However, differences in execution and recovery policy across archived runs preclude a causal estimate of starter-asset effects.

JOVE ICE is a new framework that routes AI glasses requests among the device, a paired smartphone, and cloud servers based on hierarchical intent classification and dynamic subagent orchestration. Evaluations on three public benchmarks show it reduces time to first token by about 13% and token usage by over 11% compared with conventional device-and-cloud architectures while maintaining task quality. It could influence the design of future AI assistants on wearables by showing that intent-aware routing improves efficiency and scalability without sacrificing quality. The framework integrates hierarchical intent classification with dynamic subagent orchestration to select efficient execution paths and flexibly incorporate new applications through specialized subagents. The analysis of real-world AI glasses requests reveals that simple device control tasks account for over 30% of interactions, which JOVE ICE handles directly on device.

The paper proposes Spectral Optimal Transport (SOT), which augments the pose-space optimal transport cost with a spectral feature distance to better align temporal-frequency motion dynamics in flow-based sign language generation. The method augments the pose-space OT cost with a spectral feature distance, reweighting the coupling toward the temporal-frequency structure of target motions. ## [EATS: Experience-Augmented Tool Selection for Medical Reasoning Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33897) ⭐️ 6.0/10

EATS proposes an experience-augmented tool selection strategy that builds a memory of past tool-use trajectories—including queries, tool choices, arguments, and self-evaluated feedback—and retrieves relevant experiences to guide tool selection for new queries without requiring ground-truth supervision. Evaluated on four public medical datasets (ChestAgentBench, CheXbench, MIMIC-CXR, SLAKE-VQA) across three multimodal LLMs (Qwen3-VL 2B, Qwen3-VL 32B, GPT-5.5), it shows consistent gains in tool selection behavior and downstream medical reasoning performance. This work addresses a key bottleneck in medical AI agents: general-purpose LLMs lack training exposure to domain-specific medical tools, and the uncertainty of neural tool outputs makes tool selection harder than in deterministic settings. By enabling agents to learn from test-time experience without ground-truth labels, EATS could improve the reliability and scalability of tool-augmented medical reasoning systems, potentially benefiting clinical decision support and automated report generation. EATS constructs memory from past interactions without ground-truth supervision, allowing it to scale naturally with test-time experience. Experiments span closed-ended visual question answering and free-form report generation, with code available on GitHub.

## [EGSE: Evidence-Grounded Skill Evolution for Medical Reasoning Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33909) ⭐️ 6.0/10

EGSE is a new framework that distills historical dialogues from similar medical cases into reusable conditional workflows and progressively refines a multi-faceted skill library through evidence-supported updates. It evaluates intermediate reasoning and tool use, contrasts successful and failed rollouts to extract practices and avoidance guidance, and requires source-grounded review and execution validation before adding or merging skills. Medical reasoning agents often produce correct final answers with flawed reasoning or unsupported advice, so EGSE's focus on evidence-grounded skill evolution could improve reliability and reduce tool calls in clinical AI systems. Its consistent gains across seven foundation models and skill transfer across datasets suggest a generalizable approach for building safer medical agents. Experiments on four chest X-ray benchmarks show EGSE outperforms representative medical agents and skill evolution methods in reasoning accuracy while reducing tool calls. At inference, it routes queries to skill nodes organized by imaging modality, question task type, and disease category, then selects applicable workflows based on conditions, current images, and the question.

## [Scalarizing vs. Constraining Service Requirements in HVAC Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33946) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper compares scalarizing service requirements versus imposing them as constraints in HVAC control, using model predictive control (MPC) and reinforcement learning (PPO) within the Smart Buildings Challenge. The study finds that in MPC, a soft constraint matches a hard constraint only when its weight exceeds the requirement's shadow price, and in PPO, a projection layer satisfies the requirement initially but is overtaken by a Lagrangian penalty with longer training. This work provides concrete guidance on a fundamental design choice in optimization and control—whether to scalarize or constrain service requirements—which affects energy efficiency, comfort, and carbon emissions in building automation. The findings are relevant to researchers and practitioners deploying MPC or learned policies in cyber-physical systems where service level agreements must be met. In MPC, the soft constraint reproduces the hard one once its weight exceeds the requirement's shadow price, which the challenge's comfort weight exceeds approximately fourfold; below this weight, comfort is sacrificed, and under a metric that values comfort below its shadow price, the soft constraint outperforms the hard one by up to 28%. A weight tuned on training buildings is 3.9% worse than the hard constraint on 48 unseen buildings due to shadow price variation, and in PPO, the projected policy leads early but deteriorates with longer training while the Lagrangian policy overtakes it.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: In automated decision systems, service requirements (like comfort bands or safety margins) can be handled by scalarizing them with costs into a single objective using a fixed or adaptive weight, or by imposing them as constraints. Scalarization is common in multi-objective optimization, while Lagrangian relaxation is a standard technique for constrained optimization. Model predictive control (MPC) is a model-based method that optimizes over a horizon, and proximal policy optimization (PPO) is a popular reinforcement learning algorithm. The Smart Buildings Challenge evaluates a single policy controlling HVAC across unseen buildings based on comfort, energy cost, and carbon emissions.

## [Paper formalizes retraction of erroneous assertions in speech act ethics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34011) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper extends a belief-based framework for evaluating speech acts under deontologism and consequentialism to cover retraction, using the FIPA-ACL performative 'disconfirm'. It proves that deontological honesty duties never require retracting an erroneous truth, adds a persistent honesty duty, and implements a legal-advice case in Answer Set Programming (ASP). This work bridges formal ethics and multi-agent systems by providing a precise logical account of when retraction is morally required, which could inform the design of ethical AI agents that communicate and correct misinformation. It also clarifies the distinction between deontological and consequentialist reasoning in speech acts. The framework uses the FIPA-ACL performative 'disconfirm' for retraction and is implemented in ASP, a declarative programming paradigm for NP-hard search problems. A key finding is that consequentialism no longer requires retraction once it comes too late to prevent harm, highlighting a temporal limitation.

## [EXPOSURE: Explaining Uncertainty Sources in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34248) ⭐️ 6.0/10

EXPOSURE is a new method that uses a single mask-aware belief network to explain both action selection and Shapley values, decomposing return variance into observational and inherent parts and explaining belief entropy. It is validated on tic-tac-toe and Minesweeper, where it provides more accurate explanations of expected return and its uncertainty compared to prior methods. This work extends explainable reinforcement learning from explaining expected behavior to explaining uncertainty, which is crucial for building trust in RL agents deployed in high-stakes domains. It enables practitioners to understand whether an agent's uncertainty stems from missing information or inherent environment randomness. EXPOSURE uses Shapley values to attribute return variance to observation features, requiring the agent's prediction for every subset of visible features, which is handled by the mask-aware belief network. The method is evaluated in tic-tac-toe and Minesweeper because exact Shapley values are computable there, allowing precise assessment of explanation accuracy.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Shapley values, from cooperative game theory, fairly distribute a total outcome among contributing players by averaging their marginal contributions across all possible coalitions. In reinforcement learning, they have been used to explain which observation features drive an agent's behavior and expected return, but not its certainty. Return variance decomposition separates the variability of returns into components, such as that due to missing information versus inherent environment randomness. A belief network is a probabilistic model that represents an agent's beliefs about hidden states.

A new arXiv preprint (2611.34407v1) formalizes the Agentic Waterfall Feed problem, where users actively direct recommendations and the system adapts to evolving needs inferred from explicit requests and browsing behavior. The authors propose the Agentic Waterfall framework, a language-model controller that maintains persistent user state and coordinates content retrieval, ranking, and state updates, along with a new dataset and replay-based evaluation protocol. The framework remains competitive with strong baselines on five public recommendation benchmarks and outperforms the strongest baseline on the new Agentic Waterfall Dataset by up to 16.1% relative improvement in both relevance and blind goal-satisfaction judgments. The dataset combines real content metadata with simulated interaction histories, and evaluation uses a replay-based protocol comparing systems under shared histories.

The paper introduces INSPIRE, a two-stage forecasting framework that first learns a shared-pattern predictor across the full candidate catalog and then optimizes a lightweight individual-evidence adapter to improve reasoning about emerging entities in temporal knowledge graphs. Across four benchmarks based on ICEWS and GDELT, INSPIRE improves Emerging MRR by 16.73%-88.83% over the strongest baselines. Emerging entities with sparse, bursty interaction histories are a persistent challenge for temporal knowledge graph reasoning, and INSPIRE's two-stage design offers a way to combine catalog-wide pattern transfer with entity-specific evidence. The reported gains suggest this approach could improve forecasting for newly appearing actors in domains such as political event prediction and dynamic social networks. Stage I uses an availability-aware shared-pattern predictor with coordinated query- and catalog-side masking, while Stage II freezes it and trains an adapter combining textual semantic priors with correlation-weighted long-range event statistics under support- and role-balanced co-answer supervision. The evaluation decomposes results by endpoint roles and tracks longitudinal emerging-answer cohorts using query-micro and answer-entity macro metrics, with code publicly available on GitHub.

A new arXiv paper empirically tests 14 memory-store statistics across 805 snapshots from 13 memory methods on five benchmarks (ALFWorld, AppWorld, ScienceWorld, WebShop, and two ARC-AGI settings) and finds that none reliably predict impending task-accuracy decline. Mean pairwise cosine similarity (ρ) showed zero median lead, and classifiers trained on this corpus falsely flagged 56.9% of stable checkpoints while detecting only 18% of post-collapse checkpoints. This negative result challenges the assumption that simple memory-store statistics can serve as early-warning signals for LLM agent degradation, which matters for researchers and engineers building persistent-memory agents who might otherwise rely on such metrics for monitoring or self-correction. It suggests that memory management strategies need to be validated empirically rather than inferred from global semantic density. When using the checkpoint after peak accuracy as a secondary decline point, measures of highly similar pairs and nearest-neighbor crowding changed earlier but did not consistently outperform stored-item count. In long-horizon PropWorld runs of up to 5,000 crafting tasks, size-limited stores remained stable, while the sole failure occurred when unbounded whole-store prompting exceeded model input capacity—a condition that token growth predicted directly.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: LLM agents often use persistent textual memory—storing trajectories, rules, workflows, or strategies—to improve performance across tasks. However, repeated updates to this memory can erode task accuracy, a phenomenon sometimes called memory collapse. This paper asks whether statistics computed on the memory store, such as mean pairwise cosine similarity, can warn before held-out accuracy declines, testing this across 13 memory methods and multiple interactive benchmarks.

<a id="item-310"></a>
## [KiRA: Kronecker Input-Gated Rank Adaptation for PEFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33236) ⭐️ 6.0/10

KiRA introduces a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method that replaces the conventional low-rank update with the Kronecker product of a frozen random matrix A and a trainable input-gated factor M(x), thereby multiplying the update rank by p without adding trainable parameters. Across six medical imaging and language benchmarks, KiRA achieves the highest mean score against five published baselines while using 8.7–9.7 times fewer trainable parameters than low-rank adaptation. This work addresses the low-rank bottleneck that limits most PEFT methods, showing that input-adaptive capacity can be achieved without extra trainable parameters. If validated, it could offer a more expressive and efficient alternative to LoRA-style adaptation for fine-tuning large pretrained models. The trainable factor M(x) is decomposed as B1 diag(g(x)) B2, where the input gate g(x) is computed once per sample by a network shared across all adapted layers and adjusts the rank in discrete steps of p; when input conditioning is unnecessary, the gate can be folded into B1 at deployment. Because the gate changes rank only in discrete steps, the benefit of input conditioning can be predicted before training from a few hundred labeled inputs, and this pre-training criterion agreed with the measured benefit on all six tasks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) adapts large pretrained models by training only a small number of additional parameters, with low-rank adaptation (LoRA) being the most widely used approach. LoRA represents weight updates as the product of two small low-rank matrices, which keeps training cheap but caps the rank of the update far below that of full fine-tuning and uses the same rank for every input. The Kronecker product is a matrix operation whose rank is multiplicative, meaning the rank of a Kronecker product of two matrices is the product of their ranks, which KiRA exploits to raise the effective rank of the update.

## [SWGDN: Sliding-Window Attention Improves DeltaNet Linear Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33257) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces Sliding-Window Gated DeltaNet (SWGDN), which replaces short convolutions in Delta-Rule linear attention with sliding-window attention to perform content-dependent local aggregation of queries, keys, and values. Experiments show that SWGDN makes key-value pairs used in consecutive updates more similar in both GDN2 and KDA, and outperforms the original Delta-Rule baseline across multiple benchmarks. This work shifts focus from how the recurrent state is updated to how the key-value learning pairs are constructed, offering a simple plug-in strategy that can be incorporated into any Delta-Rule-based linear attention architecture without changing its state update equations or chunkwise parallel algorithms. It could lead to more efficient and effective long-sequence models. The local mixing strategy is applied to two Delta-Rule variants, GDN2 and KDA, and the paper frames the approach through the lens of test-time regression, where the state matrix is viewed as an online-learned regression model. The modification does not alter the state update equations or chunkwise parallel algorithms, making it broadly compatible.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Linear attention compresses historical context into a fixed-size recurrent state to reduce computational and memory costs for long-sequence modeling. In the test-time regression view, the state matrix is an online-learned regression model where keys and values are inputs and targets, and queries read from the model. Delta-Rule linear attention updates the state based on the difference between predicted and actual values, and existing work has largely focused on how the state is updated and organized rather than how learning pairs are constructed.

## [Risk Certificate Hierarchy for Representation-Based Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33333) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces a hierarchy of risk certificates for representation-based prediction, proving that expected block scores form nondecreasing lower bounds on optimal class risk that converge on compact metric feature spaces. It also provides confidence bounds using dual lower bounds and calibration data. This work offers a principled framework for assessing whether a representation-decoder class can meet an accuracy requirement, which could improve model selection and certification in machine learning systems. It addresses a gap where a fitted predictor's failure does not imply the entire class cannot succeed. The bounds are derived under pairwise Lipschitz constraints for blocks of b≥2 iid observations, with bounded targets and predictions, and yield simultaneous lower confidence bounds on optimal class risk and upper confidence bounds on fitted-predictor risk. However, no fixed block size provides a uniform relative guarantee, and efficient certification at scale and extensions to dependent observations remain open problems.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Representation-based prediction involves learning a feature representation and a decoder (e.g., a Lipschitz continuous function) to make predictions. Risk certificates provide statistical guarantees on the performance of such models, which are important for safety-critical applications. Lipschitz continuity ensures that small changes in the input lead to bounded changes in the output, enabling robustness guarantees.

## [CROMA + UPerNet Tops Mangrove Mapping Benchmark with Sentinel-1/2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33369) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper benchmarks deep learning segmentation models for large-scale mangrove mapping using free Sentinel-1 radar and Sentinel-2 optical imagery, with reference maps from high-resolution Pléiades. The study compares four configurations based on CROMA and BigEarthNet-pretrained ResNet50 backbones with different decoders, and finds that CROMA combined with UPerNet achieves the best performance, with an F1-score of 0.9218 and IoU of 0.8549. This work demonstrates that pretrained multimodal Earth observation representations can enable scalable, low-cost mangrove monitoring using freely available Sentinel data, which is crucial for tracking these vulnerable coastal ecosystems across regions like French Guiana, Suriname, and Amapá. It provides a practical benchmark for selecting models when high-resolution imagery is unavailable or too expensive. The dataset covers French Guiana, Suriname, and Amapá, and the benchmark compares CROMA (a multimodal foundation model pretrained with contrastive radar-optical masked autoencoders) against ResNet50 pretrained on BigEarthNet. The best result (CROMA + UPerNet) shows that combining radar and optical data with a strong pretrained backbone and a general segmentation decoder can overcome the spatial resolution limitations of Sentinel imagery for detailed mangrove mapping.

## [SACK: Prioritizing Samples by Semantic Relevance to Guide Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33399) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Researchers propose SACK (Sequentially Acquiring Concept Knowledge), a plug-and-play technique that scores and prioritizes incoming data based on semantic relationships with the model's current knowledge to improve continual learning. Experiments across regularization, replay, and prompt-based methods in class-incremental and task-incremental settings show that SACK improves accuracy, reduces forgetting, handles long-tail distributions, and yields better-calibrated and more robust models. This work highlights sample ordering and semantic prioritization as an underexplored lever for mitigating catastrophic forgetting, potentially offering a general add-on that can be integrated into existing continual learning pipelines without architectural changes. If validated, it could benefit applications requiring models to learn sequentially from non-stationary data streams, such as robotics and personalized AI. SACK dissects categorical knowledge into fine-grained concepts, computes relationships between previously learned and new concepts in each experience, and uses this relationship knowledge to prioritize new samples. The abstract reports improvements in accuracy, forgetting reduction, long-tail handling, focus on semantically interpretable regions, and model calibration and robustness, but does not provide specific quantitative results or comparisons in the summary.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Continual learning aims to enable models to learn from a sequence of tasks or data distributions without forgetting previously acquired knowledge, a challenge known as catastrophic forgetting. Existing methods often balance stability (retaining old knowledge) and plasticity (learning new information) through techniques like regularization, experience replay, or prompt-based adaptation. The order and nature of incoming samples is a relatively understudied factor that could influence how effectively a model integrates new information.

## [RL-Guided Continuous-Discrete Flow Matching with Temporal Selective Exploration for 3D Molecular Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33417) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces an RL-guided continuous-discrete flow matching framework that restricts stochastic exploration to timesteps that most influence target properties, reducing redundant noise. It also proposes a reward-based advantage calibration mechanism to prevent objective dominance in multi-objective optimization, and applies the method to design inhibitors for EGFR and Pin1, identifying novel candidates validated in silico. This work addresses a key gap in applying reinforcement learning to flow matching, especially in mixed continuous-discrete settings like 3D molecular design where atomic coordinates and identities must be jointly optimized. By improving exploration efficiency and multi-objective balance, it could enable more effective generative design of drug-like molecules with multiple desired properties. The method jointly optimizes continuous flow matching for atomic coordinates and discrete flow matching for molecular identities, and uses a reward-based advantage calibration to mitigate objective dominance. The framework is demonstrated on EGFR and Pin1 inhibitor design, with candidate molecules supported only by in silico validation, not experimental assays.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Flow matching is a generative modeling technique that learns a continuous transformation from noise to data by regressing a neural vector field, often formulated as an ordinary differential equation (ODE). Because ODE trajectories are deterministic, they do not naturally support reinforcement learning, so stochasticity is often introduced by converting the process to a stochastic differential equation (SDE). In molecular design, flow matching can generate 3D molecular structures by jointly modeling continuous atomic coordinates and discrete atom types, but optimizing such models for multiple properties with RL remains challenging.

## [Study: LLMs fabricate unsupported claims when context is partial, but a single instruction fixes it](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33428) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv pilot study (2611.33428) evaluated claude-haiku-4-5 and llama3.1:8b on 30 cases with partially covered questions, scoring each part of a three-part question separately. Without explicit grounding instructions, both models filled gaps with unsupported claims at rates of 0.71 and 0.90; adding one sentence to answer only from the material reduced these rates to 0.00 and 0.06. This highlights a practical risk for retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and assistant systems: when reference material only partially covers a question, models may silently invent answers instead of withholding. The finding that a simple prompt instruction largely eliminates this behavior offers an easy mitigation, though the study's small scale limits generalizability. The study also found that even with grounding instructions, models sometimes strengthen claims beyond the material (e.g., turning “many job sites prohibit bots” into a flat “no”), and that judging these added-claim cases is hard—RAGAS faithfulness penalizes correct withholding, and model differences under one judge disappear under another. The dataset, code, and hand labels are public.

**Background**: Large language models (LLMs) are often used as assistants that answer questions based on supplied reference material, a setup common in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). Grounding refers to keeping model outputs faithful to that material, and hallucination occurs when models generate unsupported or false claims. Prior work has shown that explicit instructions can reduce hallucination, but evaluations typically score one answer per question and treat partially sufficient context as simply insufficient.

## [Fractional-Response IRT Framework Jointly Estimates LLM Abilities and Item Parameters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33493) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper proposes a fractional-response item response theory (IRT) framework that integrates probability and binary correctness responses from large language models (LLMs) to jointly estimate model abilities and item parameters. Using fractional-response quasi-maximum likelihood estimation within a two-parameter logistic model, the method enables common-scale evaluation without discarding probability information. This work addresses a key challenge in LLM evaluation: different benchmarks provide either probability scores or binary correctness, making it hard to compare models on a common scale. The framework could improve the efficiency and comparability of LLM evaluations, particularly for researchers aggregating results across leaderboards like Open LLM Leaderboard and HELM. Simulations show that adding probability respondents improves item calibration more efficiently than adding binary respondents, but adding noisier binary responses to an already precise probability sample can increase estimation error under equal observation weights. An application to MMLU combining Open LLM Leaderboard probability data with HELM binary data reveals model-family response patterns and systematic ability shifts between evaluation protocols.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Item response theory (IRT) is a psychometric framework that models the probability of a correct response as a function of latent ability and item characteristics, such as difficulty and discrimination. The two-parameter logistic (2PL) model is a common IRT model where each item has its own discrimination and difficulty parameters. Fractional-response quasi-maximum likelihood estimation is a method for modeling bounded continuous outcomes, often used when the response variable is a proportion or probability.

## [HiGLR: Efficient Multi-Scale Network for All-in-One Image Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33636) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces HiGLR, a multi-scale all-in-one image restoration network that identifies and eliminates three types of spatial redundancy in self-attention and convolutional feed-forward networks (ConvFFNs) at high-resolution stages. It achieves state-of-the-art performance while delivering up to 4.24× inference speedup over existing methods. High-resolution processing is a major bottleneck in all-in-one image restoration, and HiGLR's redundancy analysis and efficient designs could enable practical deployment of restoration models on resource-constrained devices. The 4.24× speedup demonstrates that significant efficiency gains are possible without sacrificing restoration quality. HiGLR replaces costly self-attention at the finest-scale stage with a learned input-independent kernel, uses Block-Routed Hybrid Attention at intermediate stages that combines coarse-scale dense attention with fine-scale block-sparse attention over value residuals, and introduces a Dual-Space FFN that performs spatial mixing in a compact space and pointwise nonlinear transformation in an expanded space for efficient kernel fusion.

## [FLUENT Trains Looped Models to Exit Earlier via Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33722) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Researchers propose FLUENT, a method that trains looped models to perform tasks with fewer iterations by distilling the initial model's final-loop prediction into early predictions and learning an exit gate, without additional task labels or rewards. Experiments show improved performance at shallower depth on Crafter, LIBERO, and language modeling, and on a real robot success rises from 62.3% to 74.6% while mean time over successful episodes falls from 69s to 31s. This work addresses the challenge of adaptive computation in looped models, where shared-weight updates can degrade final-loop predictions while improving early ones. By enabling earlier exits without extra supervision, FLUENT could reduce inference cost and latency in reinforcement learning and language tasks, benefiting efficiency researchers and practitioners deploying models under compute constraints. FLUENT distills the initial model's final-loop prediction into early predictions, anchors the current final-loop prediction to the same frozen teacher, and trains the exit gate to balance disagreement with the current final-loop prediction against computation cost. The method requires no additional task labels or rewards, and results show maintained language-model accuracy at lower selected depth.

The paper proposes STAND, a systematic vision foundation model-to-student distillation method tailored for fine-grained few-shot class-incremental learning (FG-FSCIL). STAND addresses the performance gap between distilled students and their VFM teachers on new classes by equipping the student with a fixed copy of the teacher's classifier, aligning outputs via projectors, and using composite images for relation and difference distillation. STAND freezes the student after distillation and classifies new classes using length-calibrated prototypes to restore reduced similarity of few-shot prototypes. It achieves state-of-the-art performance across FG-FSCIL datasets at low computational cost and generalizes across student backbones and broader datasets.

The paper introduces LSRP*-LA, a new planner for Multi-Agent Path Finding for Large Agents with Asynchronous Actions (LA-MAPF-AA), where each agent can occupy multiple cells. LSRP*-LA is scalable, complete, and anytime, and in tests with up to 800 agents it handled twice as many agents as baseline methods while achieving about half the solution cost of recent synchronous planners for large agents. LSRP*-LA extends the prior LSRP* planner, which was scalable and anytime but limited to single-vertex agents, to handle agents occupying several cells. The paper provides a property analysis and benchmark results on various maps, showing completeness and continued solution improvement after the initial solution until the runtime budget is exhausted.

## [QPAT: Hybrid Quantum-Classical Training with Classical Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33806) ⭐️ 6.0/10

QPAT introduces a universal framework that uses a Quantum Circuit Born Machine (QCBM) as a learnable generative prior to inject structured inductive bias into classical model training, while enabling completely classical inference. Evaluated on six benchmarks across time-series forecasting, computer vision, and NLP, it consistently outperforms purely classical backbones and avoids NISQ-era bottlenecks such as barren plateaus and quantum inference latency. This work offers a practical path for quantum machine learning deployment by decoupling quantum training from classical inference, potentially allowing near-term quantum devices to enhance classical AI without suffering from quantum latency or noise at inference time. It could impact researchers and practitioners seeking to leverage quantum advantages in mainstream deep learning applications. The framework integrates with any classical backbone (LSTMs, CNNs, Transformers) and uses a decoupled fitness-based scoring mechanism and a stochastic 'Drop-Prior' mechanism to distill quantum structural knowledge into classical weights. Ablation studies show that gains are not reproduced by unstructured random priors or a classically learned generative prior, highlighting the specific benefit of the quantum prior.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Quantum Circuit Born Machines (QCBMs) are quantum generative models that use parameterized circuits to represent and sample probability distributions via the Born rule. Quantum Machine Learning (QML) promises efficient representation of complex distributions in exponentially large Hilbert spaces, but practical deployment in the Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) era is hindered by issues like barren plateaus (where gradients vanish) and data-loading bottlenecks. QPAT aims to bridge classical AI and QML by using a QCBM as a prior during training while keeping inference classical.

<a id="item-323"></a>
## [Dependency-Ready Decoding Often Collapses to Left-to-Right in Masked Diffusion LMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33823) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.33823) analyzes dependency-ready decoding for masked diffusion models (MDMs) and finds that when an attention-based dependency-ready decoder outperforms confidence-based or future-utility baselines, its decoding order often collapses to left-to-right decoding. The authors also provide a theoretical definition of dependency using an information-theoretic score and prove that when the margin is large relative to this score, Maximum-Margin decoding already commits dependency-ready tokens. This work questions the value of a popular decoding principle for MDMs and suggests that future dependency-ready decoders have only a narrow scope for improvement, which could redirect research toward more effective decoding strategies for diffusion-based language models. The theoretical analysis defines dependency without relying on attention, measured by how much revealing a single masked token (or a set) would shift the model's prediction for another token. The authors prove that when the margin is large relative to this information-theoretic sensitivity, no such revelation can change the top prediction, and they argue that reliance on attention in dependency-ready decoders should be questioned.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 27, 05:00

**Background**: Masked diffusion models (MDMs) generate text by iteratively unmasking tokens in any order, unlike autoregressive models that generate strictly left-to-right. This freedom in decoding order has led to various strategies, such as confidence-based or future-utility decoding, and dependency-ready decoding aims to resolve a token's dependencies before unmasking it. The paper examines whether this principle actually improves accuracy.
